Skew column RSK dynamics and the box-ball system
Takashi Imamura, Matteo Mucciconi, Tomohiro Sasamoto, Travis Scrimshaw

Abstract
. The Fomin local rules for Schensted column insertion can be seen as a two-lane box-ball system, in which a carrier moves particles forward or laterally. Running such two-lane dynamics in parallel on a periodic lattice gives rise to a two-dimensional generalization of the box-ball system, which we call the skew column RSK dynamics . Equivalently, this is a deterministic dynamics on pairs of skew semistandard Young tableaux ( P t , Q t ) t ∈ ℤ (P_{t},Q_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}} . We prove that this dynamics exhibits solitonic behavior and construct an explicit bijection ( P , Q ) ↔ ( H 1 , H 2 , κ , ν ) (P,Q)\leftrightarrow(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa,\nu) that linearizes the time evolution. The resulting coordinates consist of two horizontally weak tableaux H 1 , H 2 H_{1},H_{2} recording the asymptotic soliton data, integer riggings κ \kappa , and a weakly decreasing sequence of integers ν \nu . A key feature of the construction is an explicit projection from the skew column RSK dynamics to the classical box-ball system; under this projection, the rigging κ \kappa is precisely the Kerov–Kirillov–Reshetikhin rigging of the associated box-ball configuration. Our proof uses two commuting affine crystal structures on pairs of skew tableaux and a novel connectivity theorem for distinguished subgraphs of tensor products of Kirillov–Reshetikhin crystals. We also derive Greene-type formulas for the soliton lengths in terms of last-passage percolation on the associated cylindrical environment. Finally, by taking generating functions in the linearizing coordinates, we obtain bijective proofs of Cauchy and Kawanaka–Littlewood-type identities for transformed Hall–Littlewood polynomials.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
The box-ball system

The box-ball system (BBS) is a discrete deterministic dynamical system on a one dimensional discrete lattice introduced by Takahashi and Satsuma
[
102
,
104
]
as one of the simplest examples of a cellular automaton exhibiting solitonic behavior.
The states of the BBS are given by

(1.1)

\mathsf{BBS}=\left\{B\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}}~\middle|~\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}b_{x}<\infty\right\},

where
1
’s represent balls and
0
’s represent empty boxes.
The BBS transfer matrix

(1.2)

\mathsf{T}\colon\mathsf{BBS}\to\mathsf{BBS},

giving the one time step evolution of the dynamics is defined by the following carrier algorithm.
Let
B\in\mathsf{BBS}
be a configuration.
An initially empty carrier of infinite capacity enters the lattice from far to the left and scans the sites from left to right.
Whenever it encounters an occupied site, it picks up the ball and empties that site; whenever it encounters an empty site while carrying at least one ball, it deposits exactly one ball there.
After the carrier has passed all initially occupied sites and deposited all remaining balls, the resulting configuration is
\mathsf{T}(B)
.
Equivalently, the balls are moved from left to right, each to the nearest available empty site strictly to its right.
Iterating
\mathsf{T}
gives a deterministic and reversible dynamics, where an example is illustrated in
Figure

1
.

Figure 1
.
An example of the evolution of the box-ball system. Blue labels on top denote space coordinates; we use the convention
\overline{k}=-k
.

The mathematical structures underlying the BBS are remarkably rich and have been studied extensively from the standpoint of combinatorial representation theory, integrable systems, and more recently probability.
Despite its elementary definition, the BBS captures several hallmark features of integrable systems as it is an ultradiscrete limit of the (nonlinear) Korteweg–de Vries (KdV) equation

\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}+\frac{\partial^{3}\phi}{\partial x^{3}}-6\phi\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial x}=0,\qquad\qquad\phi:=\phi(x,t).

In more detail, integrability in the KdV equation manifests in the existence of an infinite number of conserved quantities and solitary wave solutions called solitons, which are derived from a Lax formulation
[
70
]
(see also
[
88
, S.10.1]
), move with speed proportional to their size, and separate after interaction.
To obtain the BBS, we first discretize time, where we obtain the discrete Toda lattice or the discrete modified KdV (see, e.g.,
[
42
]
), and then we ultradiscretize (tropicalize) the values
\phi
(see, e.g.,
[
103
,
107
]
or
[
47
]
for a survey).
The BBS retains infinitely many conserved quantities and, as demonstrated in
Figure

1
, any configuration under repeated time evolution
\mathsf{T}
eventually decomposes into a collection of (noninteracting) stable clusters of balls which propagate with speeds determined by their sizes (or amplitudes, which play the role of the
\phi
value) and retain their sizes after interacting (or scattering).
These stable clusters are the
solitons
of the BBS, and under repeated inverse time evolution
\mathsf{T}^{-1}
, the state again eventually separates into solitons of the
same
sizes (but in the opposite order).
The nonlinearity of the KdV equation manifests in the phase shift, the change in the positions of the solitons compared to if they did not interact.

Another manifestation of integrability comes from the work of
[
30
,
37
]
(see also
[
47
]
), where the dynamics correspond to the crystal limit of the row transfer matrix of the XXZ Heisenberg spin chain
[
61
,
62
]
.
More precisely, the states correspond to certain basis elements of a tensor product of the natural 2 dimensional representation of the affine quantum group
U_{q}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2})
, known as the spin
\frac{1}{2}
module, and the carrier corresponds to a fusion module
[
63
]
of sufficiently large spin.
By
[
50
]
, these
U_{q}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2})
-modules can be encoded combinatorially using Kashiwara’s crystal bases
[
54
,
53
]
, and the local interactions of the carrier update rule are precisely the crystal limit of
R
-matrix isomorphism between the tensor products of the large spin and spin
\frac{1}{2}
modules.

A central result
[
50
,
56
,
57
]
(see also,
e.g.
,
[
24
,
31
,
101
]
or
[
77
]
for a more recent overview) is that the nonlinear BBS dynamics can be linearized through a combinatorial change of coordinates, known as the Kerov–Kirillov–Reshetikhin (KKR) bijection (for
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
) that can be described by piecewise-linear functions
[
64
]
; see also
[
20
,
81
,
106
]
for related linearization procedures.
This transformation maps the nonlinear particle evolution into a simple shift on the angle (or rigging) parameters, providing a discrete analogue of the action-angle transformation for Hamiltonian systems
[
64
,
101
]
.
This is also considered to be a discrete analog of the quantum inverse scattering transform.
This linearization is well represented by the commutative diagram

(1.3)

B
\mathrm{KKR}
\mathsf{T}
(\mu,J)
B^{\prime}
\mathrm{KKR}
(\mu,J+\mu)

where the image
(\mu,J)
of the KKR map consists of a partition
\mu
encoding the soliton amplitudes (in
Figure

1
,
\mu=(3,1)
) and an array of integers
J
that essentially identifies the location of solitons (in
Figure

1
,
J=(-4+3t,-1+t)
).

More recently, the BBS has attracted attention from the viewpoint of non-equilibrium statistical mechanics. The theory of generalized hydrodynamics predicts that integrable systems exhibit atypical hydrodynamic behavior, dominated by persistent quasiparticle modes, whose evolution follow a system of infinitely many coupled partial differential equations; see
[
19
]
and references therein.
While such predictions remain conjectural for many models, they can be rigorously verified for the BBS thanks to its combinatorial tractability. The stationary and translation invariant measures of the BBS can be explicitly constructed using variants of the KKR bijection, which can be rigorously analyzed in several asymptotic limits
[
15
,
20
,
65
,
87
]
.

1.2.
Overview

The goal of this paper is to introduce and study a two-dimensional extension of the BBS. The starting point is a two-lane version of the BBS, defined in
Section

1.3
, in which the carrier transports pairs of particles which are allowed to move laterally between the two lanes. By running these two-lane carrier dynamics in parallel on a periodic planar lattice of width
2n
, we obtain a deterministic particle system that we call the
skew column RSK dynamics
(cRSK). The name reflects the fact that the local particle rule is a formulation of the Fomin growth rule associated with the Robinson–Schensted–Knuth (RSK) correspondence under column Schensted insertion.

This particle system admits several equivalent geometric and combinatorial representations. It can be represented as an evolving ensemble of non-intersecting lattice paths with
2n
-steps periodicity, as a two-dimensional field of interlacing signatures, or as a sequence of pairs of tableaux with entries at most
n
, illustrated respectively in
Figure

2
,
Figure

6
and
Figure

7
.

Figure 2
.
A visualization of the skew column RSK dynamics as an evolving ensemble of non-intersecting lattice paths.

Two-dimensional analogues of the BBS have appeared in the literature under the name of colored BBS, formulated through higher-rank combinatorial
R
-matrices. The cRSK dynamics is different from these constructions. Its definition does not directly involve an
R
-matrix. Moreover, its state space carries two commuting affine crystal structures, giving it a symmetry structure larger than the one of the ordinary colored BBS; see
Theorem

6.9
. As the snapshots in
Figure

2
illustrate, the dynamics also admits a natural geometric representation as an ensemble of non-intersecting lattice paths evolving on a cylinder, a viewpoint that could be valuable for probabilistic questions.

A closely related integrable dynamics was defined in
[
44
]
by the first three authors under the name of skew RSK dynamics. That dynamics, however, does not generalize the BBS.
In contrast, the column-insertion version considered here contains the classical BBS as the
n=1
case, and we believe that the use of column insertion is a more canonical option from a combinatorial standpoint. For instance, column insertion admits generalizations to other root systems and these provide direct description of the combinatorial
R
-matrices defining BBS of other affine Lie types (more generally known as
soliton cellular automata
); e.g.,
[
4
,
35
,
36
,
71
,
72
]
. By contrast, to the best of the authors’ knowledge, generalizations of row insertion
[
5
,
6
,
84
,
98
,
100
,
99
,
105
]
have not been linked to BBS-type dynamics.

Our main result (
Theorem

1.7
) is a linearization algorithm for the cRSK dynamics, generalizing the KKR bijection to this two dimensional setting. This is a combinatorial bijection, which we denote by
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}
, between pairs of semistandard Young tableaux
(P,Q)
, which encode the paths represented in
Figure

2
and quadruples
(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa,\nu)
. Here
H_{1},H_{2}
are
horizontally weak tableaux
, which encode the conservation laws of the dynamics,
\nu
is a signature (a weakly decreasing finite sequence of integers) and
\kappa
is a list of integers identifying the coordinates of the solitons. The linearization produced by the bijection
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}
of
Theorem

1.7
is well illustrated by the commutative diagram

(1.4)

(P,Q)
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}
\mathbf{cRSK}
(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa,\nu)
(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}
(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa+\mu,\nu)

generalizing that of the BBS in (
1.3
). Moreover the cRSK generalizes the BBS dynamics in the sense that there exists a projection
\Pr
mapping the space of states of the cRSK dynamics onto BBS configurations that lifts the KKR bijection as described by the following commutative diagram

(1.5)

(P,Q)
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}
\mathbf{cRSK}
\mathrm{Pr}
(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa,\nu)
\mathrm{Pr}
(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}
\mathrm{Pr}
(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa+\mu,\nu)
\mathrm{Pr}
B
\mathrm{KKR}
\mathsf{T}
(\mu,J)
B^{\prime}
\mathrm{KKR}
(\mu,J+\mu)

where
\kappa=J+\mu
; see
Theorem

1.8
.

Beyond dynamics, the linearization scheme also yields new combinatorial consequences. In particular it provides bijective proofs of summation identities for the class of symmetric polynomials called
transformed Hall–Littlewood polynomials

\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x;q)
(
Definition

10.1
); see
Theorem

1.14
and
Theorem

1.15
.
Related special functions have previously appeared in the study of the BBS
[
58
,
66
,
82
]
, but using such connection as a tool to establish summation identities has been limited to date.
On the other hand, transformed Hall–Littlewood polynomials arise in probabilistic context as they describe the law of certain models of randomly growing interfaces
[
1
,
9
]
. As a result these new summation identities provide probabilistic insights about these solvable growth processes; see
[
39
,
46
,
43
]
for related results.

1.3.
A two-lane box-ball map

The two-dimensional dynamics we study in this paper are built by composing a simple local update acting on BBS configurations on two adjacent lanes. We call this the
two-lane box-ball map

(1.6)

\mathsf{F}\colon(A,B)\in\mathsf{BBS}\times\mathsf{BBS}\longrightarrow(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\in\mathsf{BBS}\times\mathsf{BBS}

and it is defined as follows.

Let
A=(a_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{Z}},B=(b_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathsf{BBS}
be two particle configurations.
An initially empty carrier enters the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
from far to the left and scans the sites from left to right. The carrier transports pairs of balls. For
x\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
c_{x}\in\mathbb{N}
denote the number of pairs of balls carried immediately after the carrier has passed site
x
. Suppose that the carrier arrives at site
x
carrying
c_{x-1}
pairs. If both boxes at site
x
are occupied, the carrier removes the two balls and adds one pair to its load. If exactly one of the two boxes is occupied, the carrier swaps the occupation between the two lanes. Finally, if both boxes are empty, the carrier deposits one ball in each lane, provided that
c_{x-1}>0
. After the carrier has passed all initially occupied sites and deposited all remaining pairs of balls, the resulting configuration is
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})=\mathsf{F}(A,B)
. For an example, see
Figure

4
, left panel.

Figure 3
.
The local update
\mathsf{f}(a,b,c)=(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime})
in the two-lane BBS.

Equivalently, define

(1.7)

\mathsf{f}\colon\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}\times\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}\times\mathbb{N},\qquad\mathsf{f}(a,b,c)=(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}),

where

(1.8)

c^{\prime}=\max\{0,a+b+c-1\},\qquad a^{\prime}=b+c-c^{\prime},\qquad b^{\prime}=a+c-c^{\prime}.

See
Figure

3
for a representation of the map
\mathsf{f}
. Then, the states
A^{\prime}=(a^{\prime}_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}
,
B^{\prime}=(b^{\prime}_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}
are given by

(a_{x}^{\prime},b_{x}^{\prime},c_{x})=\mathsf{f}(a_{x},b_{x},c_{x-1})\qquad\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{Z},\qquad c_{-\infty}=0.

It will be useful to record by
c_{0}(A,B)
the carrier load immediately after the carrier has crossed the origin during
the update of the pair
(A,B)
.

Remark 1.1
.

The classical BBS transfer matrix
\mathsf{T}
is recovered from
\mathsf{F}
when the configurations in the two lanes are identical. In other words
B^{\prime}=\mathsf{T}(B)
if and only if
(B^{\prime},B^{\prime})=\mathsf{F}(B,B)
.

Figure 4
.
Left:
A two-lane BBS update
(A,B)\to(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})=\mathsf{F}(A,B)
. In this case
c_{0}(A,B)=1
.
Right:
The diagram of the signature
\lambda=(2,1,-1)
and its conjugate
\lambda^{\prime}
.

Compared with the classical BBS transfer matrix, the two-lane BBS map allows particles to move laterally: whenever the carrier encounters a ball-hole pair, the ball is transferred to the other lane. This makes it possible to build nontrivial multi-lane dynamics by applying the two-lane map
\mathsf{F}
in a staggered fashion to adjacent pairs of lanes. For instance, on a collection of lanes arranged cyclically, one may update in parallel all pairs
(1,2),(3,4),\dots
at odd times and all pairs
(2,3),(4,5),\dots
at even times, with the last and first lanes also paired when periodic boundary conditions are imposed in the vertical direction. In this way one obtains a genuinely two-dimensional dynamics in which particles may move both longitudinally and laterally; see
Figure

5
for an illustration on four lanes.

Figure 5
.
Two time steps of the staggered two-lane box-ball dynamics on four lanes with periodic boundary conditions in the vertical direction, so that the top and bottom lanes are adjacent. At each step the two-lane BBS map
\mathsf{F}
is applied in parallel to disjoint adjacent pairs of lanes: at odd times to the pairs indicated by the blue shaded lines, and at even times to the pairs indicated by the red shaded lines. Starting from the configuration
(A,B,C,D)
, this gives
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime},C^{\prime},D^{\prime})
after one step and
(A^{\prime\prime},B^{\prime\prime},C^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime})
after two steps.

1.4.
Fields of interlacing signatures on a cylindrical lattice

It will be convenient to encode BBS particle configurations as differences of interlacing signatures and to translate the two-lane BBS update into an update of pairs of signatures. In this language, the staggered multi-lane dynamics discussed above is naturally encoded by a field of signatures on a cylindrical lattice. We therefore begin by introducing the relevant notions.

A
signature
of length
\ell\geq 0
is a weakly decreasing sequence of integers
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{\ell})
. We denote by
\mathbb{S}_{\ell}
the set of signatures of length
\ell
, and set
\mathbb{S}=\bigsqcup_{\ell\geq 0}\mathbb{S}_{\ell}
. The
conjugate
1
1

1

With this convention,
\lambda^{\prime}
is not itself a signature, since it is a sequence indexed by
\mathbb{Z}
.
of a signature
\lambda
is the sequence
\lambda^{\prime}=(\lambda_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
defined by

\lambda_{i}^{\prime}=\#\{j\mid\lambda_{j}\geq i\}.

See
Figure

4
, right panel, for an example. Two signatures
\kappa,\mu\in\mathbb{S}_{\ell}
of the same length are said to
interlace
, which we write as
\kappa\preceq\mu
, by the equivalent conditions:

(1.9)

\kappa\preceq\mu\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\mu^{\prime}-\kappa^{\prime}\in\mathsf{BBS}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\mu_{1}\geq\kappa_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\kappa_{2}\geq\cdots.

Partitions are the special case of signatures with non-negative entries and their set is denoted by
\mathbb{Y}
.

We now use the two-lane BBS to define a local growth rule for signatures. Let

\kappa,\lambda,\mu
be signatures satisfying
\lambda\succeq\kappa\preceq\mu
. The two increments
\mu^{\prime}-\kappa^{\prime}
,
\lambda^{\prime}-\kappa^{\prime}
are BBS configurations, and we regard them as input for the two-lane BBS. Recalling the map
\mathsf{F}
of (
1.6
), we define the
Fomin growth operator

(1.10)

\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\kappa,\mu,\lambda)=\nu,\qquad\text{where}\qquad(\nu^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}-\lambda^{\prime})=\mathsf{F}(\mu^{\prime}-\kappa^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}-\kappa^{\prime}).

For a graphical representation see
Figure

6
, left panel. For example, using the two-lane BBS update of
Figure

4
, left panel we can compute

\nu=\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\kappa,\mu,\lambda)\qquad\text{with}\qquad\kappa=(2,1,-1),\quad\mu=(2,2,0),\quad\lambda=(2,1,1),\quad\nu=(3,2,1),

since
A=\mu^{\prime}-\kappa^{\prime}
,
B=\lambda^{\prime}-\kappa^{\prime}
,
A^{\prime}=\nu^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime}
,
B^{\prime}=\nu^{\prime}-\lambda^{\prime}
.
The operator
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
is invertible in the following sense: Given signatures
\lambda,\mu,\nu
satisfying
\lambda\preceq\nu\succeq\mu
, there exists a unique signature
\kappa
such that
\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\kappa,\mu,\lambda)=\nu
. This is an immediate consequence of the fact that the map
\mathsf{F}
is a bijection.

We proceed by imposing periodic boundary conditions. For any
n\geq 1
, define the cylindrical lattice

(1.11)

\mathscr{C}_{n}=\mathbb{Z}^{2}/\sim,\qquad(x,y)\sim(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\Longleftrightarrow(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=(x+kn,y-kn)\quad\text{for some }k\in\mathbb{Z}.

Similarly we define the dual lattice
\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime}=(\mathbb{Z}^{\prime})^{2}/\sim
, which is the lattice of faces of
\mathscr{C}_{n}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}=\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
.
Let
\mathbf{e}_{1}=(1,0)
and
\mathbf{e}_{2}=(0,1)
. A
simple loop
(or down-right loop) on
\mathscr{C}_{n}
is a path

(1.12)

\mathbf{p}=(p_{0},p_{1},\dots,p_{2n})\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{2n+1},\qquad\text{such that}\qquad p_{i+1}-p_{i}\sim\mathbf{e}_{1},-\mathbf{e}_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad p_{2n}\sim p_{0}.

Definition 1.2

(Fomin field of interlacing signatures)
.

A
Fomin field
of interlacing signatures of length
\ell
on
\mathscr{C}_{n}
is a map
\bm{\lambda}\colon\mathscr{C}_{n}\to\mathbb{S}_{\ell}
such that for every
p\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
,

(1.13)

\bm{\lambda}(p)\preceq\bm{\lambda}(p+\mathbf{e}_{i}),\quad\text{for }i\in\{1,2\},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{\lambda}(p+\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2})=\overline{\mathsf{F}}\left(\bm{\lambda}(p),\bm{\lambda}(p+\mathbf{e}_{1}),\bm{\lambda}(p+\mathbf{e}_{2})\right).

We associate to a Fomin field
\bm{\lambda}
an
environment

\mathbf{c}\colon\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{N}
as follows. If
p^{\prime}=p+(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2})/2
, then

\mathbf{c}(p^{\prime})=c_{0}(\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}(p+\mathbf{e}_{1})-\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}(p),\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}(p+\mathbf{e}_{2})-\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}(p)),

where
c_{0}(A,B)
was defined in
Section

1.3
.

Figure 6
.
Left:
A depiction of the Fomin growth operator.
Right:
A Fomin field on the cylindrical lattice
\mathscr{C}_{2}
. The red shaded path is the simple loop
\mathbf{p}=((0,2),(0,1),(0,0),(1,0),(2,0))
. The non-zero values of the environment
\mathbf{c}(p^{\prime})
are drawn on faces of
\mathscr{C}_{2}
.

An example of a Fomin field of interlacing signatures on
\mathscr{C}_{n}
is shown in
Figure

6
; the values of the corresponding environment are drawn in the faces of the lattice.
Since the Fomin operator is invertible, specifying the values of a Fomin field on any simple loop
\mathbf{p}\subset\mathscr{C}_{n}
determines all neighboring values recursively, both forward and backward. Thus any compatible interlacing data along such a loop extends uniquely to a Fomin field on the whole cylinder.

Choosing either the rightward or the upward direction on
\mathscr{C}_{n}
as a
time direction turns the same construction into a dynamical system on
sequences of signatures. This point of view is illustrated in

Figure

2
. There, for the cylinder
\mathscr{C}_{2}
shown
in
Figure

6
, we consider the simple loops

\mathbf{p}_{t}=\bigl(p_{0}^{t},p_{1}^{t},p_{2}^{t},p_{3}^{t},p_{4}^{t}\bigr),\qquad t\in\{-2,-1,0,1,2\},

where

(1.14)

p_{0}^{t}=(2t,2),\qquad p_{1}^{t}=(2t,1),\qquad p_{2}^{t}=(2t,0),\qquad p_{3}^{t}=(2t+1,0),\qquad p_{4}^{t}=(2t+2,0).

The signatures along these loops are represented by the point configurations

\begin{aligned} &\quad\left\{\bigl(j,\bm{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}(p_{j}^{t})-i\bigr)~\middle|~i\in\mathbb{Z}\right\},\quad j\in\{0,\dots,4\},\\[10.00002pt]
&\qquad\qquad\qquad t\in\{-2,\dots,2\}\end{aligned}\qquad\qquad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[width]{Maya_diagram.pdf}}}.

On the side we depicted the point configuration
\{\bm{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}(p^{0}_{0})-i\mid i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
, where
p^{0}_{0}=(0,0)
and
\bm{\lambda}(0,0)=(2\geq 1\geq-1)
.

A continuous limit of the dynamics illustrated in
Figure

2
was introduced in
[
43
]
under the name of “periodic multi-layer polynuclear growth”. In such special limit the dynamical rules admit a simpler direct description which bypasses the two-lane BBS.

The example in
Figure

2
suggests that the resulting dynamics on sequences of signatures exhibits solitonic behavior analogous to that of the BBS. This solitonic behavior is reflected in the following stabilization phenomenon.

Theorem 1.3

(Stabilization phenomenon and conservation laws)
.

Let
\bm{\lambda}
be a Fomin field of interlacing signatures of length
\ell
on
\mathscr{C}_{n}
. Then there exists an integer partition
\mu=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{\ell^{\prime}})
, with
\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell
and a time
t^{*}>0
such that, for all

t\geq t^{*}
,

(1.15)

\bm{\lambda}(t+n,i)=\bm{\lambda}(t,i)+(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{\ell^{\prime}},0\dots,0),\qquad\bm{\lambda}(-t-n,i)=\bm{\lambda}(-t,i)-(0,\dots,0,\mu_{\ell^{\prime}},\dots,\mu_{1}).

Theorem

1.3
contains two statements.
The first one, which is relatively elementary, is that the forward and backward asymptotic signatures of a Fomin field have constant increments.
The second, and substantially more delicate one, is that the two asymptotic increments coincide up to reversal.
This second statement is
Theorem

6.15
in the text.

To describe more explicitly the solitonic features of Fomin fields and their relation with the BBS, we next introduce the cRSK dynamics.

1.5.
The skew column RSK dynamics

Recall that given signatures
\rho\subset\lambda
(i.e.
\rho_{i}\leq\lambda_{i}
for all
i
), a semistandard Young tableau of skew shape
\lambda/\rho
is a sequence of
n+1
interlacing signatures

(1.16)

P=(\rho=\lambda^{(0)}\preceq\lambda^{(1)}\preceq\cdots\preceq\lambda^{(n)}=\lambda).

Equivalently,
P
is a filling of the cells of the Young diagram
\lambda/\rho
in which the cells
(j,k)
such that
\lambda_{j}^{(i-1)}<k\leq\lambda_{j}^{(i)}
are assigned the label
i
, for each
i=1,\dots,n
. This filling is weakly
increasing along rows and strictly increasing along columns. We denote by
\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)
the set of semistandard Young tableaux of skew shape
\lambda/\rho
with labels in
\{1,\dots,n\}
.

The following is the main definition of this paper.

Definition 1.4

(Skew column RSK dynamics)
.

Fix a pair
(P,Q)
of semistandard Young tableaux of skew shape
\lambda/\rho

(1.17)

P=(\rho=\lambda^{(0)}\preceq\lambda^{(1)}\preceq\cdots\preceq\lambda^{(n)}=\lambda),\qquad Q=(\rho=\mu^{(0)}\preceq\mu^{(1)}\preceq\cdots\preceq\mu^{(n)}=\lambda)

and construct the Fomin field
\bm{\lambda}=\bm{\lambda}[P,Q]
on
\mathscr{C}_{n}
such that

(1.18)

\bm{\lambda}(i,0)=\lambda^{(i)},\qquad\bm{\lambda}(0,i)=\mu^{(i)},\qquad\text{for }i=0,\dots,n.

The
skew RSK column dynamics
(or cRSK dynamics) with initial conditions
(P,Q)
is the sequence of pairs of semistandard Young tableaux
(P_{t},Q_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
of same skew shape such that

P_{t}=(\bm{\lambda}(tn,0)\preceq\bm{\lambda}(tn+1,0)\preceq\cdots\preceq\bm{\lambda}(tn+n,0)),\qquad Q_{t}=(\bm{\lambda}(tn,0)\preceq\bm{\lambda}(tn,1)\preceq\cdots\preceq\bm{\lambda}(tn,n)).

See
Figure

7
for an example.

Figure 7
.
An example of evolution of the cRSK dynamics.

In
Section

3
, we give an alternative description of the cRSK dynamics in terms of Schensted column insertion, which is sometimes more convenient for proving properties of the system.

Remark 1.5
.

For
n=1
, the cRSK dynamics become the classical BBS. Indeed, if
\kappa\preceq\lambda
and
\nu=\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\kappa,\lambda,\lambda)
, then the configurations
B=\lambda^{\prime}-\kappa^{\prime}
,
B^{\prime}=\nu^{\prime}-\lambda^{\prime}
satisfy
B^{\prime}=\mathsf{T}(B)
as described in
Remark

1.1
.

A consequence of the stabilization phenomenon of
Theorem

1.3
of Fomin fields is that, asymptotically, pairs of tableaux
(P_{t},Q_{t})
evolving according to the cRSK dynamics evolve linearly.
Namely, for large enough
t
,
P_{t+1}
(resp.
Q_{t+1}
) will be obtained from
P_{t}
(resp.
Q_{t}
) by translating its partitions (or labeled cells) by
\mu
.
This is evident in the example of
Figure

7
, where
P_{2}
(resp.
Q_{2}
) is obtained from
P_{1}
(resp.
Q_{1}
) by rigidly shifting the first and second row by
3
and
1
boxes to the right.
Similarly
P_{-2}
(resp.
Q_{-2}
) is obtained from
P_{-1}
(resp.
Q_{-1}
) by rigidly shifting the last and second last row by
3
and
1
boxes to the left.

We now extract from the asymptotic regime a pair of tableaux encoding the soliton data. Given a partition
\mu
, a
horizontally weak tableau
of shape
\mu
is a filling of the Young diagram of
\mu
whose entries are weakly increasing along rows, with no condition imposed along columns.
We denote by
\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
the set of horizontally weak tableaux of shape
\mu
with labels in
\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Definition 1.6

(Soliton data)
.

Given a pair
(P,Q)
of semistandard Young tableaux of the same skew shape, define the map

(1.19)

\Phi\colon(P,Q)\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho\in\mathbb{S}}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}\longrightarrow(H_{1},H_{2})\in\bigsqcup_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}}\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)^{2},

where, letting
\bm{\lambda}=\bm{\lambda}[P,Q]
, we set

(1.20a)

\displaystyle\#\{i\text{-cells at row }j\text{ of }H_{1}\}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}\bm{\lambda}_{j}(t,i)-\bm{\lambda}_{j}(t,i-1),

(1.20b)

\displaystyle\#\{i\text{-cells at row }j\text{ of }H_{2}\}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}\bm{\lambda}_{j}(t+i,0)-\bm{\lambda}_{j}(t+i-1,0).

For example, setting
(P,Q)=(P_{0},Q_{0})
as in
Figure

7
, we have

(1.21)

\Phi(P,Q)=(H_{1},H_{2})\qquad\text{with}\qquad H_{1}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 1$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 1$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern H_{2}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 1$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}~.

1.6.
Main results

The main result of this paper is an explicit linearization algorithm for the
cRSK dynamics. The construction relates the dynamics introduced
above to the classical BBS, and identifies the asymptotic soliton
data as part of a complete set of coordinates.

Let
\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,\ldots\}
be the set of natural numbers, including 0. To state our main result, we introduce the
dual energy

D^{\prime}\colon\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
of a horizontally weak tableau. It is a natural statistic originating in the study of lattice models (see, e.g.,
[
49
]
for some history) and its definition is given in
Definition

5.17
. The energy has the property that
D^{\prime}(H)=0
if and only if the horizontally weak tableau
H
satisfies the semistandard property; i.e., if the filling is also strictly increasing column-wise. On the other hand if
H
does not have the semistandard property, we have
D^{\prime}(H)>0
and
D^{\prime}(H)
assumes maximal value if the tableau
H
only has entries equal to 1. We also introduce the sets

(1.22a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(\mu):=\{\kappa=(\kappa_{1},\dotsc,\kappa_{\ell(\mu)})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell(\mu)}\mid\kappa_{i}\geq\kappa_{i+1}\text{~if~}\mu_{i}=\mu_{i+1}\},

(1.22b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{+}(\mu):=\{\kappa=(\kappa_{1},\dotsc,\kappa_{\ell(\mu)})\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell(\mu)}\mid\kappa_{i}\geq\kappa_{i+1}\text{~if~}\mu_{i}=\mu_{i+1}\}.

Theorem 1.7
.

There exists an explicit bijection

(1.23)

\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}\colon(P,Q)\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho\in\mathbb{S}}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}\longrightarrow(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa,\nu)\in\bigsqcup_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}}\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)^{2}\times\mathcal{K}(\mu)\times\mathbb{S}

with the following properties:

(1.24)

\displaystyle(H_{1},H_{2})=\Phi(P,Q),

(1.25)

\displaystyle\lvert\rho\rvert=D^{\prime}(H_{1})+D^{\prime}(H_{2})+\lvert{\kappa}\rvert+\lvert\nu\rvert,

(1.26)

\displaystyle\ell(\lambda)=\ell(\mu)+\ell(\nu),

where
\ell(\mu)
is the number of positive entries of the partition
\mu
and
2
2

2

notice that if
\rho
is a signature, then
|\rho|
is not necessarily positive.

\lvert\rho\rvert=\sum_{i}\rho_{i}
.
Moreover,
\rho,\lambda
are integer partitions if and only if
\nu
is also a partition and
\kappa\in\mathcal{K}_{+}(\mu)
.

The first two components
H_{1},H_{2}
of
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}(P,Q)
are the soliton data introduced in
Definition

1.6
as prescribed by (
1.24
).
The component
\nu
is also obtained directly from the Fomin field.
Let
\bm{\lambda}=\bm{\lambda}[P,Q]
, and set

m=\max\left\{i~\middle|~\lim_{t\to-\infty}\bm{\lambda}_{i}(t,0)\text{ exists and is finite}\right\}.

Then
\nu\in\mathbb{S}_{m}
is defined by

(1.27)

\nu_{i}=\lim_{t\to-\infty}\bm{\lambda}_{i}(t,0),\qquad i=1,\dots,m.

If
m=0
, then
\nu=\varnothing
. In the example of
Figure

7
, one has
m=1
, since the backward cRSK dynamics leaves only the first row constant. Hence, for
(P,Q)=(P_{0},Q_{0})
in that example, the corresponding signature is
\nu=(2)
.

It remains to describe the component
\kappa
. This is the most delicate part
of the construction. Its definition relies on a fundamental projection from
the cRSK dynamics to the classical BBS.

Theorem 1.8
.

There exists an explicit projection

(1.28)

\Pr\colon(P,Q)\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho\in\mathbb{S}}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}\longrightarrow B\in\mathsf{BBS},

such that, assuming
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}(P,Q)=(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa,\nu)
with
H_{1},H_{2}
of shape
\mu
, then

(1.29)

\mathrm{KKR}(B)=(\mu,J)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\kappa=J+\mu.

Remark 1.9
.

Theorem

1.8
explains why the map
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}
gives linearized coordinates for the cRSK dynamics, as illustrated in (
1.4
) and (
1.5
). Indeed, under
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}
, the data of a pair
(P,Q)
is decomposed into conserved quantities
(H_{1},H_{2},\nu)
and an angle variable
\kappa
. The latter is identified, via the projection
\Pr
and the KKR correspondence, with the rigging data of the associated BBS configuration. Since the KKR correspondence linearizes the BBS evolution by translating the riggings, the
\kappa
coordinates evolve linearly under the cRSK dynamics.

The construction of the projection
\Pr
is the main novel technical result of this paper and it uses symmetries of the cRSK dynamics, namely the operators

E_{i}^{(1)},F_{i}^{(1)},E_{i}^{(2)},F_{i}^{(2)},\qquad i\in\{0,\dots,n-1\},

defined in
Section

6
. These are Kashiwara operators for an
(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n}\oplus\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n})
-crystal structure on pairs of skew semistandard tableaux; equivalently, they define two commuting affine crystal structures. A key input in the construction is a novel connectivity property of certain subgraphs of the Kirillov–Reshetikhin
crystals
\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
, which we call
(dual) regular subgraphs
; see
Definition

5.22
and
Definition

5.37
. These connectivity results, proved in
Theorem

5.24
and
Theorem

5.40
are one of the main technical ingredients of the paper and may be of independent interest.

In
Figure

8
, we illustrate the construction of
\Pr
for
the pair
(P,Q)=(P_{0},Q_{0})
from
Figure

7
. A repeated application of the
crystal operators reduces the pair
(P,Q)
to a pair of equal tableaux, which is then
identified with the BBS configuration
B

shown in
Figure

1
.

Figure 8
.

The construction of the projection
\Pr
in
Theorem

1.8
.
The final pair of equal skew tableaux, whose entries are all
1
, is identified with a BBS configuration by placing balls at coordinates of the columns containing

1
-labelled cells.

The connectivity results described above ensure that any pair
(P,Q)
can be moved, by crystal operators, to a pair
(T,T)
of identical tableaux whose entries are all
1
. This representative determines the projected BBS configuration. Moreover, the change in the shape of the tableaux along this transformation is controlled by the dual energies of the soliton data
(H_{1},H_{2})
; this bookkeeping is the key ingredient in the proof of the identity (
1.25
).

1.7.
Greene’s invariants

It is possible to give an alternative description of the asymptotic partition
\mu
associated to a Fomin field of signatures
\bm{\lambda}
. This is a generalization of Greene’s theorem which describes the shape of semistandard tableaux which arise as the image of a matrix of natural numbers under the RSK correspondence
[
26
]
.

Given a Fomin field of signatures
\bm{\lambda}
, it is straightforward to notice, from the definition of the Fomin growth operator (
1.10
), that
\bm{\lambda}
can be reconstructed from the environment
\mathbf{c}
and from the signature
\nu
given by (
1.27
). The environment records the carrier data at the origin in every elementary square. Together with the backward asymptotic signature
\nu
, this data determines the field
\bm{\lambda}
uniquely, by recursively applying the inverse Fomin growth rule. This proves a variant of periodic RSK correspondence of
[
90
]
, which we state next.

For any set
A
and any set
B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
such that
0\in B
, we denote the set of finitely supported functions on
A
taking values in
B
as

(1.30)

\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A,B)=\{\mathbf{c}\colon A\to B\mid\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{c})\text{ is finite}\}.

Proposition 1.10

(Periodic column RSK correspondence)
.

We have a bijection

\Psi\colon(P,Q)\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho\in\mathbb{S}}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}\longrightarrow(\mathbf{c},\nu)\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime},\mathbb{N})\times\mathbb{S},

where letting
\bm{\lambda}=\bm{\lambda}[P,Q]
, we define
\mathbf{c}
as the environment associated to
\bm{\lambda}
and
\nu
is defined as in (
1.27
).

Composing the periodic column RSK correspondence from
Proposition

1.10
with the bijection of
Theorem

1.7
we obtain a refined correspondence, in which the soliton data are read directly from the environment
\mathbf{c}
. In particular, the shape
\mu
appearing in the linearizing coordinates admits a Greene-type description in terms of last passage percolation times across
\mathbf{c}
.

A
strict up-right path
is a bi-infinite sequence
\bm{\omega}=(p_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime}
such that
p_{i+1}-p_{i}\sim(a,b)
with
a,b>0
, for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. We define two variants of
last passage percolation times
across an environment
\mathbf{c}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime},\mathbb{N})
as

(1.31a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{LPP}_{k}^{\circlearrowleft}(\mathbf{c})

\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{(k)}\\
\text{non-intersecting}\\
\text{simple loops}\end{subarray}}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=0}^{2n-1}\mathbf{c}(\mathbf{p}^{(i)}_{j})\right\}

(1.31b)

\displaystyle\mathrm{LPP}_{k}^{\nearrow}(\mathbf{c})

\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{\omega}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{\omega}^{(k)}\\
\text{strict up-right}\\
\text{paths}\end{subarray}}\left\{\sum_{p\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime}}\min\left\{\mathbf{c(p)},\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{1}_{p\in\mathbf{\omega}^{(i)}}\right\}\right\}.

In words, the statistics
\mathrm{LPP}_{k}^{\circlearrowleft}(\mathbf{c})
measures the maximal cumulative weight that
k
walkers can collect from
\mathbf{c}
after performing a simple loop each (loops cannot intersect each other). To interpret the statistics
\mathrm{LPP}_{k}^{\nearrow}(\mathbf{c})
, imagine that each cell
p\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime}
carries
\mathbf{c}(p)
units of weight and each time an individual visits
p
it may extract at most one unit from that cell. Then
\mathrm{LPP}_{k}^{\nearrow}(\mathbf{c})
is the maximal weight that
k
individuals can extract from
\mathbf{c}
along
k
strict up-right paths.

For the next theorem we define the negative half infinite cylinder

\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime-}=\left\{p\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime}~\middle|~p\sim(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\text{ for some }i^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}_{<0}\text{ and }j^{\prime}\in\left\{\frac{1}{2},\dots,n-\frac{1}{2}\right\}\right\}.

Theorem 1.11
.

There exists a bijection

(1.32)

\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mathrm{col}}\colon\mathbf{c}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime},\mathbb{N})\longrightarrow(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa)\in\bigsqcup_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}}\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)^{2}\times\mathcal{K}(\mu),

such that, assuming
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}(P,Q)=(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa,\nu)
and
\Psi(P,Q)=(\mathbf{c},\nu)
, we have
\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mathrm{col}}(\mathbf{c})=(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa)
. Moreover, the following properties hold.

(I)

Assuming that
\mu
is the common shape of tableaux
H_{1},H_{2}
, we have

(1.33)

\mathrm{LPP}_{k}^{\circlearrowleft}(\mathbf{c})=\mu_{1}+\cdots+\mu_{k},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{LPP}_{k}^{\nearrow}(\mathbf{c})=\mu_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+\mu_{k}^{\prime}.

(II)

For all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
we have

(1.34)

\#\{i\text{-cells of }H_{1}\}=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}}\mathbf{c}\left(i-\frac{1}{2},\ell\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\#\{i\text{-cells of }H_{2}\}=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}}\mathbf{c}\left(\ell,i-\frac{1}{2}\right).

(III)

We have

(1.35)

\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}k\,\mathbf{c}\left(i-\frac{1}{2},j-kn-\frac{1}{2}\right)=D^{\prime}(H_{1})+D^{\prime}(H_{2})+|\kappa|.

(IV)

The map
\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mathrm{col}}
restricts to a bijection

\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mathrm{col}}\colon\mathbf{c}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime-},\mathbb{N})\longrightarrow(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa)\in\bigsqcup_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}}\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)^{2}\times\mathcal{K}_{+}(\mu).

The proof of
Theorem

1.11
is given in
Section

8.2
.

Remark 1.12
.

Theorem

1.11
appears to be a generalization of the affine RS(K) correspondence by
[
13
,
12
,
69
,
95
]
.
It would be interesting to establishing a precise connection between these related constructions and results.

Remark 1.13
.

Consider the
n=1
case of
Theorem

1.11
. In this case simple loops on
\mathscr{C}_{1}^{\prime}
are pairs
(p,p+\mathbf{e}_{1})
with
p\in\mathscr{C}_{1}^{\prime}
while strict up-right paths are sequences
(p_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
such that
p_{i+1}=p_{i}+k\mathbf{e}_{1}
with
k\geq 2
. Moreover, by
Remark

1.5
in the
n=1
case the numbers
\mathbf{c}(t-1/2,1/2)
record the number of balls carried by the BBS carrier while crossing the origin at time
t
.
Then,
Theorem

1.11
states that the total size of the
k
largest solitons is
\mathrm{LPP}_{k}^{\circlearrowleft}(\mathbf{c})
. Similarly the difference
\mathrm{LPP}_{k}^{\nearrow}(\mathbf{c})-\mathrm{LPP}_{k-1}^{\nearrow}(\mathbf{c})
counts the number of solitons of length
k
or more. These statements appear to be new, but they are reminiscent of Greene-type theorems for the multi-species BBS found in
[
76
]
.

1.8.
Application to symmetric functions

The linearization of the cRSK dynamics also has applications to symmetric functions. By taking suitable generating functions on both sides of the bijection (
1.32
), we obtain bijective proofs of Cauchy and Kawanaka–Littlewood identities for transformed Hall–Littlewood
polynomials.

We will use the
q
-Pochhammer symbols

(z;q)_{k}=\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}(1-zq^{i})\quad\text{for }k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}.

The functions
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x;q)
and the coefficients
c_{\mu}(q)
,
c_{\mu}(\alpha,\beta;q)
appearing in the statements below are defined explicitly in
Section

10
.

Theorem 1.14

(Cauchy and Kawanaka–Littlewood identities)
.

Fix a parameter
|q|<1
and
2n
parameters
x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
with absolute value
<1
. Then, we have

(1.36)

\prod_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(x_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}}=\sum_{\mu}c_{\mu}(q)\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x;q)\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\mu}(y;q).

Moreover, for any
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
we have

(1.37)

\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{(-\alpha x_{i};q^{2})_{\infty}(-q\beta x_{i};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(x_{i}^{2};q^{2})_{\infty}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{1}{(x_{i}x_{j};q^{2})_{\infty}}=\sum_{\mu}c_{\mu}(\alpha,\beta;q)\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x;q^{2}).

Taking suitable generating function of left and right hand sides of (
1.23
) we obtain the following refinement of
Theorem

1.14
. Analogous identities for
q
-Whittaker polynomials were proven in
[
44
,
45
,
39
]
. These were further generalized through the addition of an extra parameter in
[
38
,
41
,
40
]
by He and Wheeler, through algebraic techniques.

Theorem 1.15

(Refined Cauchy and Kawanaka–Littlewood identities)
.

Fix a parameter
|q|<1
and
2n
parameters
x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
with absolute value
<1
. Then, for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

(1.38)

\displaystyle\sum_{\rho\subset\lambda\,:\,\lambda_{1}^{\prime}=N}q^{|\rho|}s_{\lambda/\rho}(x)s_{\lambda/\rho}(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{N}\frac{q^{\ell}}{(q;q)_{\ell}}\sum_{\mu\,:\,\mu_{1}^{\prime}=N-\ell}c_{\mu}(q)\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x;q)\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\mu}(y;q),

(1.39)

\displaystyle\sum_{\rho\subset\lambda\,:\,\lambda_{1}^{\prime}=N}\alpha^{\mathrm{odd}(\lambda)}\beta^{\mathrm{odd}(\rho)}q^{|\rho|}s_{\lambda/\rho}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{[q^{2}+q\alpha\beta]_{q^{2}}^{k}}{(q^{2};q^{2})_{k}}\sum_{\mu_{1}^{\prime}=N-k}c_{\mu}(\alpha,\beta;q)\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x;q^{2}),

where
\mathrm{odd}(\lambda)
counts the number of odd elements of
\lambda
and the notation
[a+b]^{n}_{p}
is defined in (
10.4
).

The proof of
Theorem

1.15
is given in
Section

10
.

1.9.
Discussion

The system with cRSK dynamics is a direct integrable two-dimensional generalization of the BBS.
This model lies at the intersection of several well-developed areas of combinatorics, probability, and integrable systems.
On one side is the theory of the BBS: the classical BBS has been solved by combinatorial methods, related to continuous soliton equations through ultradiscretization, generalized using affine crystal theory and other Lie symmetries, and studied asymptotically in connection with generalized hydrodynamics.
The other side consists of growth processes and line ensembles, which are related through RSK.
They are central objects in integrable probability and in the study of KPZ universality; see, e.g.,
[
8
,
18
,
109
]
and references therein.
The cRSK dynamics interpolates between these two structures.
We have defined it as a dynamics on
2n
-step periodic line ensembles:
When
n=1
it reduces to the classical BBS, and after choosing suitable random environments and sending
n\to\infty
, these periodic line ensembles converge to the line ensembles associated with last passage percolation studied in
[
14
,
48
,
89
]
.

One natural direction suggested by this connection is therefore probabilistic.
3
3

3

This direction arose from a conversation with Amol Aggarwal, whom we thank.

Given a random environment on the cylindrical lattice, one may run the cRSK dynamics and study the long-time behavior of the associated line ensemble.
Questions of local convergence to equilibrium and mixing for this dynamics are then closely related to universality questions in last passage percolation.
Indeed, the Greene-type theorem proved in this paper expresses the soliton data encoded in the line ensemble in terms of last-passage values of the environment.
Thus, one may hope to combine these Greene-type formulas with structural characterizations of line ensembles, such as those proved by Aggarwal and Huang
[
2
]
, to obtain new perspectives on universality in last passage percolation.
This is particularly intriguing because convergence to equilibrium for discrete integrable systems appears to be largely unexplored, even for the classical BBS.

It would also be interesting to look for a rational lift of the cRSK dynamics.
The classical BBS arises as an ultradiscrete limit of integrable lattice models such as the Toda lattice or the modified KdV equation, while RSK-type growth rules are closely related to tropical forms of the octahedron recurrence (see, e.g.,
[
16
,
17
,
67
,
83
]
).
The direct connection with the BBS also points toward possible extensions to other Lie types.
As commented above, the use of column insertion may be instrumental here, since column insertion procedures have analogues for other root systems and are closely related to combinatorial
R
-matrices for soliton cellular automata.

A separate direction is to keep the two-lane BBS local rule fixed but consider it in other geometries.
In this paper, the dynamics is run in parallel on a periodic lattice, leading to the cylindrical cRSK dynamics.
One could instead impose different boundary conditions, for instance on intervals with reflecting or open boundaries; such variants may produce new symmetry structures and models related to other Lie types.

These possible extensions may also have consequences for symmetric functions.
In the present paper, the linearization of the cRSK dynamics gives bijective proofs of Cauchy and Kawanaka–Littlewood type identities for transformed Hall–Littlewood polynomials (of type
A
), while the linearization in
[
44
]
led to identities for (type
A
)
q
-Whittaker polynomials.
This suggests that analogous linearization schemes, arising from other Lie symmetries, boundary geometries, or rational lifts, could provide bijective proofs of Cauchy-type identities for the corresponding families of (specializations of) Macdonald-type polynomials.

1.10.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Signatures and partitions

In the introduction we have defined signatures
\lambda
, their conjugates
\lambda^{\prime}
and the notion of interlacing. Although the conjugate of a signature is not a partition, but an infinite sequence of non-negative integers, we will use the convention that
(\lambda^{\prime})^{\prime}=\lambda
for any signature
\lambda
. We have also defined semistandard Young tableaux with skew shape given by signatures. A
partition
is a signature whose entries are non-negative, with trailing zeros usually omitted. If
\lambda
is a partition its Young diagram consists only of cells
(i,j)
such that
1\leq j\leq\lambda_{i}
and its size is
|\lambda|=\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{\ell}
. The conjugate of a partition
\lambda
is
\lambda^{\prime}=(\lambda_{i}^{\prime})_{i\geq 1}
and it is also a partition.

2.2.
Standard tableaux

In the introduction we have defined the notion of a semistandard tableau. Given a semistandard tableau
P=(\rho=\lambda^{(0)}\preceq\lambda^{(1)}\preceq\cdots\preceq\lambda^{(n)}=\lambda)
we call
\lambda/\rho
its
shape
,
\rho
its
internal shape
and
\lambda
its
external shape
. We also define the multiplicities

(2.1)

\wt_{i}(P)=|\lambda^{(i)}/\lambda^{(i-1)}|,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{M}_{i}(P)=\sum_{j=1}^{i}\wt_{j}(P)+\mathbf{1}_{\wt_{j}(P)=0},\qquad\text{for }i\geq 1.

Representing
P
as a filling of a Young diagram,
\wt_{i}(P)
counts the number of
i
-labeled cells.
We say that a tableau
P
is
partially standard
4
4

4

In literature a tableau of
n
boxes is
standard
if
\wt_{i}(P)=1
for
i=1,\dots,n
.
if we have
\wt_{i}(P)\in\{0,1\}
for all
i
.
From a semistandard tableau
P
we can produce a partially standard one in a canonical way, called
partial standardization
. For all
i
, whenever
\wt_{i}(P)>0
we add between consecutive partitions
\lambda^{(i-1)}
and
\lambda^{(i)}
intermediate partitions
\lambda^{(i,0)},\dots,\lambda^{(i,\wt_{i}(P))}
so that

\lambda^{(i-1)}=\lambda^{(i,0)}\preceq\lambda^{(i,1)}\preceq\cdots\preceq\lambda^{(i,\wt_{i}(P))}=\lambda^{(i)}\qquad\text{with}\qquad\left(\lambda^{(i,j)}\right)^{\prime}-\left(\lambda^{(i,j-1)}\right)^{\prime}=\mathbf{e}_{m_{i,j}},

where

m_{i,1}<\cdots<m_{i,\wt_{i}(P)}.

In terms of filling of Young diagrams the above procedure consists of replacing in the order from left to right the
\wt_{i}(P)

i
-entries in
P
(whose column coordinates are
m_{i,1}<\cdots<m_{i,\wt_{i}(P)}
) with
\mathsf{M}_{i-1}(P)+1,2,\dotsc,\mathsf{M}_{i}(P)
(where by convention
\mathsf{M}_{0}(P)=0
), for all
i=1,\dots,n
.

.

and

height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{gray}\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$\hss}\vss}}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}\,.

Unless the shape of a semistandard Young tableau is a skew partition we will write labels on top of each column (reported in
blue
in the right hand side tableau in (
2.2
)) where barred labels
\overline{i}
,
i=1,2,\ldots
, denote negative numbers
-i
. In this case we also think of the tableaux as having infinitely many empty boxes to the left of its shape. The partial standardization of the tableau in (
2.2
) is

(2.4)

((0,-1,-2)\preceq(0,0,-2)\preceq(1,0,-2)\preceq(1,0,-1)\preceq(1,0,0)\preceq(2,0,0))=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.275,0.51,0.71}\cdots}$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=8.4pt\kern \cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\cdots$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=8.4pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern
