height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 2070.4pt}}$}\,.

2.3.
Graphical presentation of Fomin growth operator

The Fomin growth operator was defined in
Section

1
. Here we present a slightly different formulation for it and give its graphical interpretation (which is a variant of Viennot’s shadow lines construction
[
108
]
), which will make some of its properties transparent. First recall the piecewise linear transformation
\mathsf{f}
defined in (
1.7
).

To evaluate
\mathsf{f}(a,b,c)=(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime})
, consider a unit cell containing
c
distinct points arranged along a diagonal, with points on the right placed lower than those on the left. From each of the
c
points, draw two rays: one upward and one rightward. If
a=1
, insert a vertical ray entering the cell from below, positioned just to the right of the rightmost of the
c
points in the cell. If
b=1
, insert a horizontal ray entering the cell from the left, located just above the topmost point. Rays travel straight and stop when they intersect another ray or exit through the top or right boundary of the cell. Then
c^{\prime}
counts number of ray intersections within the cell,
a^{\prime}
counts the number of rays exiting through the top and
b^{\prime}
counts number of rays exiting through the right.

The example below shows the evaluations:

(2.5)

\begin{array}[]{c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c}\mathsf{f}(1,0,3)=(1,0,3)&\mathsf{f}(1,1,3)=(0,0,4)&\mathsf{f}(0,0,3)=(1,1,2)\\
\includegraphics[width]{Fomin_local_rule_graphical_1.pdf}&\includegraphics[width]{Fomin_local_rule_graphical_2.pdf}&\includegraphics[width]{Fomin_local_rule_graphical_3.pdf}\end{array}.

The map
\mathsf{f}
is the building block for the Fomin growth operator
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
defined in (
1.10
). For signatures
\mu\succeq\kappa\preceq\lambda
of the same length, we have

(2.6)

\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\kappa,\mu,\lambda)=\nu,

where the signature
\nu
satisfying
\lambda\preceq\nu\succeq\mu
is defined by the relations

(2.7)

\mu^{\prime}-\kappa^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i},\qquad\lambda^{\prime}-\kappa^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}b_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i},\qquad\nu^{\prime}-\lambda^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{i}^{\prime}\mathbf{e}_{i},\qquad\nu^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}b_{i}^{\prime}\mathbf{e}_{i}.

where
\mathbf{e}_{i}=\left(\mathbf{1}_{i=k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and

(2.8)

(a_{i}^{\prime},b_{i}^{\prime},c_{i})=\mathsf{f}(a_{i},b_{i},c_{i-1})\qquad\text{for all }i\in\mathbb{Z},\quad\text{with }\quad c_{-\infty}=0.

Since the sequences
(a_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
,
(b_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
are finitely supported, consequence of the fact that
\kappa,\lambda,\mu
have the same length, the condition
c_{-\infty}=0
determines uniquely the sequences
(a_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
,
(b_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
,
(c_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
and hence the operator
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
is well defined. See
Figure

10
for a graphical representation of the Fomin growth operator.

Figure 9
.
The refinement of a Fomin cell. On the right, the values of the environment following the refinement of a cell

The graphical construction of the Fomin growth operator, provides a way to refine its action into simpler operations, as depicted in
Figure

9
. Namely given a triple
\mu\succeq\kappa\preceq\lambda
, we can produce sequences

(2.9)

\mu=\mu^{(K)}\succeq\cdots\succeq\mu^{(1)}\succeq\mu^{(0)}=\kappa=\lambda^{(0)}\preceq\lambda^{(1)}\preceq\cdots\preceq\lambda^{(L)}=\lambda,

where
|\mu^{(i)}/\mu^{(i-1)}|,|\lambda^{(j)}/\lambda^{(j-1)}|\in\{0,1\}
for all
i,j
and for
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{|\mu/\kappa|}
,
j_{1}<\cdots<j_{|\lambda/\kappa|}
we have

(2.10)

\left(\mu^{(i_{k})}\right)^{\prime}-\left(\mu^{(i_{k}-1)}\right)^{\prime}=\mathbf{e}_{m_{k}},\qquad\left(\lambda^{(j_{r})}\right)^{\prime}-\left(\mu^{(j_{r}-1)}\right)^{\prime}=\mathbf{e}_{\ell_{r}},

with

(2.11)

m_{1}<\cdots<m_{|\mu/\kappa|}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\ell_{1}<\cdots<\ell_{|\ell/\kappa|}.

In other words, partitions
\mu^{(i)}
(resp.
\lambda^{(j)}
) are constructed from
\kappa
by adding, step-by-step either no cells or cells of
\mu/\kappa
(resp
\lambda/\kappa
) from the leftmost to the right. The
\nu=\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\kappa,\mu,\lambda)
can be constructed by evaluating the
(M+1)\times(L+1)
array of interlacing partitions
\{\bm{\nu}(i,j)\}_{0\leq i\leq M,\,0\leq j\leq L}
with boundary conditions
\bm{\nu}(i,0)=\mu^{(i)}
and
\bm{\nu}(0,j)=\lambda^{(j)}
and such that

\bm{\nu}(i+1,j+1)=\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\bm{\nu}(i,j),\bm{\nu}(i+1,j),\bm{\nu}(i,j+1)).

Then
\nu=\bm{\nu}(M,L)
.

In the next proposition we examine the values
c_{0}(p^{\prime})
produced by the evaluation of the Fomin growth operator
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
at each cell
p^{\prime}
the
(M+1)\times(L+1)
lattice.

Proposition 2.2
.

In the notation established above, let
\Lambda_{M,N}=\{0,\dots,M\}\times\{0,\dots,L\}
and let
\Lambda^{\prime}_{M,N}=\{\frac{1}{2},\dots,M-\frac{1}{2}\}\times\{\frac{1}{2},\dots,L-\frac{1}{2}\}
be the dual lattice.
Call
\mathbf{c}
the value of
c_{0}
in the evaluation of
\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\kappa,\mu,\lambda)
and for
p^{\prime}=p+\frac{\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}}{2}
call
\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}(p^{\prime})
be the value of
c_{0}
in the evaluation of
\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\bm{\nu}(p),\bm{\nu}(p+\mathbf{e}_{1}),\bm{\nu}(p+\mathbf{e}_{2}))
. Then, we have

(2.12)

\mathbf{c}=\sum_{i\geq 1}\left(\nu_{i}^{\prime}-\lambda_{i}^{\prime}-\mu_{i}^{\prime}+\kappa_{i}^{\prime}\right)

and if
\mathbf{c}>0

\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}(p^{\prime})=\begin{cases}1\qquad&\text{if }p^{\prime}=(A^{-}+k,B^{+}+\mathbf{c}+1-k)\text{ for }k=1,\dots,\mathbf{c},\\
0\qquad&\text{else},\end{cases}

where

A^{-}=\sum_{i\leq 0}\nu^{\prime}_{i}-\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\qquad B^{+}=\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}-\kappa^{\prime}_{i}.

If
\mathbf{c}=0
, then
\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}(p^{\prime})=0
for all
p^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{M,N}^{\prime}
.
See
Figure

9
, right panel.

Proof.

First notice that, if
a_{i}=\mu^{\prime}_{i}-\kappa^{\prime}_{i}
and
a_{i}^{\prime}=\nu^{\prime}_{i}-\lambda^{\prime}_{i}
, borrowing the notation of (
2.7
), (
2.8
) we have, from (
1.8
)

c_{i-1}=a_{i}^{\prime}-a_{i}+c_{i}=\sum_{j\geq i}a_{j}^{\prime}-a_{j},

which implies (
2.12
) setting
i=1
.

If
|\lambda/\kappa|=0
or
|\mu/\kappa|=0
, then every coefficient
c_{k}
in the evaluation of
\nu=\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\kappa,\mu,\lambda)
and in every evaluation
\bm{\nu}(i+1,j+1)=\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\bm{\nu}(i,j),\bm{\nu}(i+1,j),\bm{\nu}(i,j+1))
is equal to 0 and the proposition holds.

Assume that
|\lambda/\kappa|,|\mu/\kappa|>0
and define sequences
(m_{k}(\ell):k\in\{1,\dots,K\},\ell\in\{1,\dots,L\})
with
m_{1}(\ell)<\cdots<m_{K}(\ell)
by

\bm{\nu}(k,\ell)^{\prime}-\bm{\nu}(k,\ell-1)^{\prime}=\mathbf{e}_{m_{k}(\ell)}.

Then, by direct inspection of the Fomin growth operator we have
m_{k}(\ell+1)-m_{k}(\ell)\in\{0,1\}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}(k-\frac{1}{2},\ell+\frac{1}{2})=1
if and only if
m_{k}(\ell)=0
and
m_{k}(\ell+1)=1
. Call
(k_{1},\ell_{1}),\dots,(k_{r},\ell_{r})
all coordinates such that
\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}(k_{i}-\frac{1}{2},\ell_{i}+\frac{1}{2})=1
. Then by monotonicity of the sequences
m_{k}(\ell)
, the columns
k_{i}
all columns such that

(2.13)

m_{k_{i}}(0)\leq 0\qquad\text{and}\qquad m_{k_{i}}(L)>0

and it must necessarily be

k_{i}=k_{0}+i\qquad\text{for }i=1,\dots,r.

Arguing by symmetry with respect to the transposition of the lattice
\Lambda_{K,L}
we must also have

j_{i}=j_{0}-i\qquad\text{for }i=1,\dots,r.

It remains to show that
r=\mathbf{c}
and that
k_{0}=A^{-}
,
j_{0}=B^{+}+\mathbf{c}+1
. For the first equality notice that

\begin{split}r&=\#\{k\mid m_{k}(0)\leq 0\text{ and }m_{k}(L)>0\}\\
&=\sum_{i\leq 0}\left(\bm{\nu}_{i}(K,0)^{\prime}-\bm{\nu}_{i}(0,0)^{\prime}\right)-\sum_{i\leq 0}\left(\bm{\nu}_{i}(K,L)^{\prime}-\bm{\nu}_{i}(0,L)^{\prime}\right)\\
&=\sum_{i\geq 1}(\nu_{i}^{\prime}-\lambda_{i}^{\prime})-\sum_{i\geq 0}(\mu_{i}^{\prime}-\kappa_{i}^{\prime})=\mathbf{c}.\end{split}

By the characterization (
2.13
) we have
k_{0}=\max\{k\geq 0\mid m_{k}(L)\leq 0\}=\sum_{i\leq 0}\nu_{i}^{\prime}-\lambda_{i}^{\prime}=A^{-}
. Finally, the equality
j_{0}=B^{+}+\mathbf{c}+1
can be obtained arguing again by symmetry with respect to transposition of the lattice
\Lambda_{K,L}
, completing the proof.
∎

An advantage of this factorization is that the evaluation of the array
\bm{\nu}(i,j)
only involves the action of the Fomin growth operator
\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\kappa,\mu,\lambda)
on triples of partitions
\mu\succeq\kappa\preceq\lambda
such that
|\mu/\kappa|,|\lambda/\kappa|\in\{0,1\}
; see
Figure

10
right panel.

Figure 10
.
In the left panel a graphical representation of evaluation of the Fomin operator. In the right panel its partial standardization.

Let us now report two basic properties of the map
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
. The first is a conservation law.

Proposition 2.3
.

If
\nu=\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\kappa,\mu,\lambda)
, then
|\nu/\lambda|=|\mu/\kappa|
and
|\nu/\mu|=|\lambda/\kappa|
.

Proof.

From (
2.7
) we see that
|\nu/\lambda|=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(a_{i}+c_{i}-c_{i-1}\right)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{i}=|\mu/\kappa|
, where we used (
2.8
) and the definition (
1.7
) of the map
\mathsf{f}
.
∎

Another basic property of the Fomin operator
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
is the commutation with the global column-wise addition of a signature.

Proposition 2.4
.

For signatures
\mu\succeq\kappa\preceq\lambda
, if
\nu=\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\kappa,\mu,\lambda)
and we have

(2.14)

\overline{\mathsf{F}}\bigl((\kappa^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\prime},(\mu^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\prime},(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\prime}\bigr)=(\nu^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})^{\prime}.

In other words the action of the Fomin growth operator commutes with the column-wise addition of a signature
\rho
.

Proof.

This is straightforward since the evaluation of the Fomin growth operator
\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\kappa,\mu,\lambda)
only depends on the differences
\lambda^{\prime}-\kappa^{\prime}
and
\mu^{\prime}-\kappa^{\prime}
.
∎

2.4.
Horizontally weak tableaux

Definition 2.5

(Horizontally weak tableau)
.

A
horizontally weak tableau
(HWT) is a filling of Young diagram with positive integers which is nondecreasing in each row while there are no conditions on the columns. We call the associated Young diagram
\mu
the
shape of
the HWT
. Let
\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
denote the set of HWT of shape
\mu
with entries in the alphabet
\{1,\dotsc,n\}
.

For later use we also extend the above definition in the case where the shape
\mu
is not necessarily the Young diagram but
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\ell}
,
\ell=1,2,\cdots
in a similar way.

Example 2.6
.

The following are examples of the HWTs:

NotethatthefirstexampleisasemistandardYoungtableau
,
i
.
e
.,
entriesareadditionallystrictlyincreasingverticallyfromtoptobottomwhilethesecondoneisnot
.
Ontheotherhand
,
therightmosttwotableauxhaveshaperespectivelyμ
= (3,4,2)
and
μ
=(2,4,5)
,whicharenotpartitions.\end{example}\par

2.5.
Weight and row reading word

We define two functions, the weight and the row reading word, which we mainly use to introduce the crystal structures for the set of tableaux in
section

5
.

Definition 2.7

(Weight)
.

For a combinatorial object
P
with entries
\{1,2,\dotsc,n\}
(such as a semistandard tableau, HWT, a word, etc.),
the
weight
of
P
is defined by

(2.27)

\displaystyle\wt(P):=(\wt_{1}(P),\dotsc,\wt_{n}(P))\in\mathbb{Z}^{n},

where, for
i=1,\dotsc,n
,
\wt_{i}(P)
is the number of ‘
i
’ entries in
P
.

Definition 2.8

(Row reading word)
.

For an HWT or semistandard tableau, the
row reading word
is defined by concatenating the parts with positive elements in all rows starting from the bottom.

Example 2.9
.

For the semistandard and horizontally weak tableaux

therowreadingwordsare
1122111
and
2111221223
,respectively.\end{example}\par

3.
Column RSK dynamics

3.1.
Refinements of fields of interlacing signatures on the cylinder

Fomin fields of interlacing signatures on the cylindrical lattice
\mathscr{C}_{n}
were defined in
Definition

1.2
. To each Fomin field
\bm{\lambda}
we can associate a pair of semistandard tableaux
(P,Q)
by reading the signatures along the simple loop

(0,0),(1,0),\dots,(n,0)\sim(0,n),(0,n-1),\dots,(0,1),(0,0)

as done in (
1.17
) and (
1.18
). In this case we write
\bm{\lambda}=\bm{\lambda}[P,Q]
, since the field is fully determined by the tableaux
(P,Q)
. More in general the Fomin field
\bm{\lambda}
would be fully determined once one assigns its values along any simple loop
\mathbf{p}\subset\mathscr{C}_{n}
.

We say that a field of interlacing signatures
\bm{\lambda}
is
fully refined
if

|\bm{\lambda}(p+\mathbf{e}_{\varepsilon})/\bm{\lambda}(p)|\in\{0,1\}\qquad\text{for }\varepsilon\in\{0,1\},\qquad\text{for all }p\in\mathscr{C}_{m,n}.

It is clear that if a Fomin field
\bm{\lambda}
is fully refined the associated tableaux
(P,Q)
(i.e.
\bm{\lambda}=\bm{\lambda}[P,Q]
) are partially standard. The converse is also true. This is because if
\lambda\succeq\kappa\preceq\mu
are interlacing signatures with
|\lambda/\kappa|,|\mu/\kappa|\in\{0,1\}
, then
\nu=\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\kappa,\mu,\lambda)
also has the property that
|\nu/\lambda|,|\nu/\mu|\in\{0,1\}
. Such property follows directly from the definition of the operator
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
. It is also evident from the graphical construction presented in
Section

2.3
, where the sizes
|\lambda/\kappa|,|\mu/\kappa|,|\nu/\lambda|,|\nu/\mu|
are respectively the number of colored rays crossing the left, bottom, top and right side of a cell used to evaluate
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
as in
Figure

10
.

The partial standardization procedure of semistandard tableaux described in
Section

2.2
provides a natural way to refine Fomin fields of signatures.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\bm{\lambda}
be a Fomin field of interlacing signatures on
\mathscr{C}_{n}
, and let
N=\lvert\bm{\lambda}(n,0)/\bm{\lambda}(0,0)\rvert
. There exists a fully refined Fomin field
\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}
on
\mathscr{C}_{N}
and an explicit embedding
\phi\colon\mathscr{C}_{n}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}_{N}
such that
\bm{\lambda}(p)=\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}(\phi(p))
. Moreover, let
T
be any semistandard tableau of the form

(3.1)

T=\bigl(\bm{\lambda}(p)\preceq\bm{\lambda}(p+\mathbf{e}_{\varepsilon})\preceq\cdots\preceq\bm{\lambda}(p+r\mathbf{e}_{\varepsilon})\big)

for some
p\in\mathscr{C}_{n},r\in\{0,\dots,n\},\varepsilon\in\{1,2\}
, then the tableau

(3.2)

\widetilde{T}=\bigl(\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}(\phi(p))\preceq\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}(\phi(p)+\mathbf{e}_{\varepsilon})\preceq\cdots\preceq\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}(\phi(p+r\mathbf{e}_{\varepsilon})\big)

is the partial standardization of
T
.

Proof.

We can refine each evaluation

\bm{\lambda}(p+\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2})=\overline{\mathsf{F}}\bigl(\bm{\lambda}(p),\bm{\lambda}(p+\mathbf{e}_{1}),\bm{\lambda}(p+\mathbf{e}_{2})\bigr),

as described in
Section

2.3
. Such refinement produces a field
\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}
on
\mathscr{C}_{N}
, obtained replacing each cell
p^{\prime}=(i+kn+\frac{1}{2},j+kn+\frac{1}{2})\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime}
for
i,j\in\{0,\dots,n-1\},k,l\in\mathbb{Z}
with a matrix of cells

\left\{\bigl(\alpha+kN+\frac{1}{2},\beta+kN+\frac{1}{2}\bigr)~\middle|~M_{i}\leq\alpha\leq M_{i+1}-1,~M_{j}^{\prime}\leq\beta\leq M_{j+1}^{\prime}-1\right\}\subset\mathscr{C}_{N}^{\prime},

with
M_{i}=|\bm{\lambda}(i,j)/\bm{\lambda}(0,j)|=|\bm{\lambda}(i,0)/\bm{\lambda}(0,0)|
and
M_{j}^{\prime}=|\bm{\lambda}(i,j)/\bm{\lambda}(i,0)|=|\bm{\lambda}(0,j)/\bm{\lambda}(0,0)|
, by
Proposition

2.3
. Then, by construction, we have
\bm{\lambda}(p)=\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}(\phi(p))
, where the embedding
\phi
is defined as

\phi(i+kn,j+\ell n)=(M_{i}+kN,M_{j}^{\prime}+\ell N),

for
i,j\in\{0,\dots,n-1\},k,l\in\mathbb{Z}
. The fact that any tableau
\widetilde{T}
of the form (
3.2
) is the standardization of its pre-image
T
under
\phi
, as in (
3.1
), is evident by construction.
∎

Figure 11
.
The refinement of the field
\bm{\lambda}
of
Figure

6
as described in
Proposition

3.1
.

Proposition

3.1
will allow us to prove statements for Fomin fields of signatures by analyzing only fully refined fields, which are simpler to treat.

Remark 3.2
.

The standardization is a common technique in algebraic combinatorics; see e.g.
[
97
]
.

3.2.
Basic properties of the skew column RSK dynamics

Let us give some basic definitions.

Definition 3.3

(Skew column RSK map and shifts
\iota_{1},\iota_{2}
)
.

Fix
(P,Q)\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}
and construct the field of interlacing partitions
\bm{\lambda}=\bm{\lambda}[P,Q]
.
The
skew column RSK map

\mathbf{cRSK}
is the map
\mathbf{cRSK}(P,Q)=(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, where

(3.3)

\mathbf{cRSK}(P,Q)=(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})~:~P^{\prime}=\bigl(\bm{\lambda}(n,0)\preceq\cdots\preceq\bm{\lambda}(2n,0)\bigr),\quad Q^{\prime}=\bigl(\bm{\lambda}(n,0)\preceq\cdots\preceq\bm{\lambda}(n,n)\bigr).

We also define the operations
\iota_{1},\iota_{2}
as

\displaystyle\iota_{1}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})~:~\widetilde{P}=\bigl(\bm{\lambda}(1,0)\preceq\cdots\preceq\bm{\lambda}(n+1,0)\bigr),~~\widetilde{Q}=\bigl(\bm{\lambda}(1,0)\preceq\cdots\preceq\bm{\lambda}(1,n)\bigr),

\displaystyle\iota_{2}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=(\overline{P},\overline{Q})~:~\overline{P}=\bigl(\bm{\lambda}(0,1)\preceq\cdots\preceq\bm{\lambda}(n,1)\bigr),~~\overline{Q}=\bigr(\bm{\lambda}(0,1)\preceq\cdots\preceq\bm{\lambda}(0,n+1)\bigr).

It is clear that
\mathbf{cRSK},\iota_{1},\iota_{2}
are invertible maps.
For examples, see
Figure

12
.

Figure 12
.
In the left panel, examples of evaluation of
\iota_{1},\iota_{2}
. They correspond to sequences of interlacing signatures displayed on the lattice in the right panel. Signatures along the red down-right loop correspond to tableaux
(P,Q)
, those along the blue loop to
\iota_{2}(P,Q)=(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})
, while those on along the green loop to
\iota_{1}(P,Q)=(\overline{P},\overline{Q})

The cRSK dynamics of
Definition

1.4
with initial conditions
(P,Q)
could be equivalently defined as the sequence
(P_{t},Q_{t})
with
(P_{t},Q_{t})=\mathbf{cRSK}^{t}(P,Q)
, for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
; this is evident from the definition of the map
\mathbf{cRSK}
.

We now enumerate several basic properties of Fomin fields of signatures.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
(P,Q)\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}
and let
\bm{\lambda}=\bm{\lambda}[P,Q]
be the corresponding field of interlacing partitions on
\mathscr{C}_{n}
. The following properties hold.

(I)

if
(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})=\iota_{2}(P,Q)
and
\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}=\bm{\lambda}[\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q}]
, then
\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}(x,y)=\bm{\lambda}(x,y+1)
for all
(x,y)\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
;

(II)

if
(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})=\iota_{1}(P,Q)
and
\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}=\bm{\lambda}[\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q}]
, then,
\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}(x,y)=\bm{\lambda}(x+1,y)
for all
(x,y)\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
;

(III)

if
(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})=\mathbf{cRSK}(P,Q)
and
\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}=\bm{\lambda}[\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q}]
, then
\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}(x,y)=\bm{\lambda}(x+n,y)=\bm{\lambda}(x,y+n)
for all
(x,y)\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
;

(IV)

we have
\iota_{2}(P,Q)=\iota_{1}(Q,P)
.

(V)

we have
\mathbf{cRSK}=\iota_{2}^{n}=\iota_{1}^{n}
;

(VI)

we have
\iota_{1}\circ\iota_{2}=\iota_{2}\circ\iota_{1}
;

(VII)

Let
(\overline{P},\overline{Q})=\iota_{2}(P,Q)
. Then
\wt_{i}(\overline{P})=\wt_{i}(P)
and
\wt_{1}(\overline{Q})=\wt_{n}(Q)
, while
\wt_{i}(\overline{Q})=\wt_{i-1}(Q)
for
i=2,\dots,n

(VIII)

Call
(R,T)
and
(\overline{R},\overline{T})
the pairs of tableaux obtained by standardization of the pairs
(P,Q)
and
(\overline{P},\overline{Q})=\iota_{2}(P,Q)
. Then
(\overline{R},\overline{T})=\iota_{2}^{\wt_{1}(Q)}(R,T)
.

(IX)

Let
\mathbf{c}
be the environment associated to the field
\bm{\lambda}
, as in
Definition

1.2
. Then, for all
(x,y)\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
, we have

(3.4)

|\bm{\lambda}(x+1,y)/\bm{\lambda}(x,y)|=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}}\mathbf{c}\left(x+\frac{1}{2},j\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad|\bm{\lambda}(x,y+1)/\bm{\lambda}(x,y)|=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}}\mathbf{c}\left(j,y+\frac{1}{2}\right).

Proof.

Statements
(I)
,
(II)
,
(III)
follow straightforwardly from
Definition

3.3
. They further imply
(IV)
,
(V)
,
(VI)
. The property
(VII)
is straightforward from
Definition

3.3
and
Proposition

2.3
. The property
(VIII)
is an immediate consequence of
Proposition

3.1
and
(I)
.

Finally, we prove
(IX)
. Since the environment
\mathbf{c}
is finitely supported, let
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}
be large enough so that
\mathbf{c}(x+1/2,-m-i)=\mathbf{c}(x+1/2,m+i)=0
for all
i\geq 0
and

\bm{\lambda}_{-j}^{\prime}(x+1,m+i)-\bm{\lambda}_{-j}^{\prime}(x,m+i)=0=\bm{\lambda}_{j}^{\prime}(x+1,-m-i)-\bm{\lambda}_{j}^{\prime}(x,-m-i)\qquad\text{for all }i,j\geq 0.

In other words the signatures
\bm{\lambda}(x+1,m+i)
and
\bm{\lambda}(x,m+i)
(resp.
\bm{\lambda}(x+1,-m-i)
and
\bm{\lambda}(x,-m-i)
) only differ by cells at some positive (resp. negative) column. Then, by
Proposition

2.3
and (
2.12
), we have

\displaystyle|\bm{\lambda}(x+1,y)/\bm{\lambda}(x,y)|

\displaystyle=|\bm{\lambda}(x+1,m)/\bm{\lambda}(x,m)|=\sum_{j\geq 1}\bm{\lambda}_{j}^{\prime}(x+1,m)-\bm{\lambda}_{j}^{\prime}(x,m)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq 1}\bm{\lambda}_{j}^{\prime}(x+1,-m)-\bm{\lambda}_{j}^{\prime}(x,-m)+\sum_{|j|<m}\mathbf{c}\left(x+\frac{1}{2},j\right)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}}\mathbf{c}\left(x+\frac{1}{2},j\right).

This proves the first relation in (
3.4
). The second relation can be shown analogously.
∎

3.3.
Alternative description through Schensted column insertion

In
Section

3.2
, we defined the maps
\mathbf{cRSK},\iota_{1},\iota_{2}
in terms of the Fomin growth operator
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
. Here we give an alternative, more combinatorial, description in terms of the Schensted column insertion.

Definition 3.5

(Column insertion)
.

Let
P\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho\in\mathbb{S}}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)
be a tableau, let
x\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and let
c\in\mathbb{Z}
be a column label. The column insertion of
x
in
P
at column
c
is the following procedure

(1)

Scan the column
c
of the tableau
P
and call
x^{\prime}
the smallest entry larger or equal to
x
. If such an entry exists, replace it with
x
and insert
x^{\prime}
into column
c+1
of
P
.

(2)

If all entries of the
c
-th column of
P
are strictly smaller than
x
(which includes the case where the column is empty) create a new cell at the bottom of column
c
and assign to it the value
x
.

Definition 3.6

(Column internal insertion
\mathcal{C}_{[c]}
)
.

Let
P\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho\in\mathbb{S}}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)
be a tableau and let
c
be a column label such that the
c
-th column of
P
possesses a
corner cell
, namely a labeled cell
(r,c)
such that both cells
(r-1,c),(r,c-1)
are not labeled cells of
P
. Let
x
be the entry of the corner cell of
c
-th column of
P
. We define the
column internal insertion

\mathcal{C}_{[c]}(P)\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho\in\mathbb{S}}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)
as the tableau obtained by removing the corner at column
c
of
P
and performing column insertion of
x
into
P
at column
c+1
.

The operation of column (internal) insertion can be realized using the Fomin operator, as described below.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
P\in\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)
be a tableau and let
\widetilde{P}=\mathcal{C}_{[k]}(P)
be the tableau obtained performing a column internal insertion at column
k
. If

(3.5)

P=\left(\rho=\lambda^{(0)}\preceq\lambda^{(1)}\preceq\cdots\preceq\lambda^{(n)}=\lambda\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{P}=\left(\widetilde{\rho}=\nu^{(0)}\preceq\nu^{(1)}\preceq\cdots\preceq\nu^{(n)}\right),

then,

(3.6)

\widetilde{\rho}=\left(\rho^{\prime}+\mathbf{e}_{k}\right)^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad\nu^{(i+1)}=\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\lambda^{(i)},\lambda^{(i+1)},\nu^{(i)})\quad\text{for all }i=1,\dots,n.

In other words,
\widetilde{\rho}
is obtained from
\rho
by adding an empty cell at the
k
-th column, while all other partitions
\nu^{(i)}
are determined iteratively using the Fomin growth operator
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
.

Proof.

The relation between the Fomin growth rules and the Schensted insertion were discovered in
[
21
,
22
]
(see also
[
60
]
) and the argument goes as follows. By the factorization properties of the Fomin growth operator and the iterative definiton of the column insertion, it suffices to show that the proposition holds for tableaux
P
such that
|\lambda^{(i)}/\lambda^{(i-1)}|\in\{0,1\}
. Under these assumptions one can proceed by induction on the index
i=0,\dots,n-1
, matching the partitions
\nu^{(i+1)}
produced by the column inserition procedure with the output of
\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\lambda^{(i)},\lambda^{(i+1)},\nu^{(i)})
. We leave these checks to the reader; see
Example

3.8
.
∎

Example 3.8
.

Below we compute the internal insertion
\mathcal{C}_{[1]}(P)
step-by-step as

On the right panel we observe the representation of the column internal insertion as described in
Proposition

3.7
: the bottom row represents the tableau
P
, while the row above is
\mathcal{C}_{[1]}(P)
.

Using
Proposition

3.7
we can translate the action of the operations
\iota_{1}
,
\iota_{2}
and
\mathbf{cRSK}
on pairs of semistandard tableaux using the column internal insertion as a building block.

Proposition 3.9

(
\iota_{2}
through column insertion)
.

Let
(P,Q)\in\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}
. Then
(\overline{P},\overline{Q})=\iota_{2}(P,Q)\in\mathrm{SST}(\overline{\lambda}/\overline{\rho},n)^{2}
can be computed in the following way:

(I)

Let
k=\wt_{1}(Q)
and
i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{k}
be the column labels of the 1-cells (the cells with label 1) of
Q
. Then

(3.7)

\displaystyle\overline{P}=\mathcal{C}_{[i_{k}]}\cdots\mathcal{C}_{[i_{1}]}(P).

(II)

\overline{Q}
is obtained by vacating all
1
-cells, decreasing by 1 the labels of all remaining cells and assigning label
n
to all cells of the skew Young diagram
\widetilde{\lambda}/\lambda
(i.e. assigning label
n
to all new cells added to
P
after the action of
\mathcal{C}_{[i_{j}]},~j=1,\dotsc,k
).

By the symmetry of
Proposition

3.4
,
Item

(IV)
, the operation
\iota_{1}
can be realized as
\iota_{1}(P,Q)=\iota_{2}(Q,P)
. Finally, by
Proposition

3.4
,
Item

(V)
, we can realize the
\mathbf{cRSK}
map iterating either
\iota_{2}
or
\iota_{1}
as
\mathbf{cRSK}=\iota_{2}^{n}=\iota_{1}^{n}
.

Proof.

Let
R,T,\overline{R},\overline{T}
be respectively the partial standardizations of tableaux
P,Q,\overline{P},\overline{Q}
. Since we can recover
\overline{P},\overline{Q}
by
\overline{R},\overline{T}
and the weights
\wt_{i}(\overline{P}),\wt_{i}(\overline{Q})
for
i=1,\dots,n
and the latter are prescribed by
Proposition

3.4

(VII)
, we focus on the description of
\overline{R},\overline{T}
. By
Proposition

3.4

(VIII)
we have
(\overline{R},\overline{T})=\iota_{2}^{\wt_{1}(Q)}(R,T)
and by
Proposition

3.9
each of the
\wt_{1}(Q)
iterations of the operation
\iota_{2}
can be realized through a column insertion. More precisely letting
(\overline{R}_{\ell},\overline{T}_{\ell})=\iota_{2}^{\ell}(R,T)
we have that
\overline{R}_{\ell}=\mathcal{C}_{[i_{\ell}]}(\overline{R}_{\ell-1})
where
i_{\ell}
is the column label of the 1-cell in
\overline{T}_{\ell-1}
, which is also equal to the column label of the
\ell
-th 1-cell in
Q
from the left. Translating this procedure in terms of the tableau
P
, proves (
3.7
). On the other hand statement
(II)
is immediate from
Definition

3.3
, completing the proof.
∎

Example 3.10
.

In
Figure

12
we showed an example of the evaluation of
\iota_{2}
. Below we evaluate
\iota_{2}(P,Q)
using the alternative algorithm described in
Proposition

3.9
:

\includegraphics[width]{iota_2.pdf}.

3.4.
Asymptotic stabilization

Here we discuss the stabilization phenomenon, produced after iterating the cRSK map
\mathbf{cRSK}^{n}(P,Q)
, or its inverse for a sufficiently large number of times.

Proposition 3.11
.

Fix
(P,Q)\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}
and let
\bm{\lambda}=\bm{\lambda}[P,Q]
be the corresponding field of interlacing signatures on
\mathscr{C}_{n}
. Then, there exist
\mu\in\mathbb{Y}
,
t_{+}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mu^{-}\in\mathbb{Y}^{-}
and
t_{-}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
such that

(3.8a)

\displaystyle\bm{\lambda}((t+1)n+x,y)

\displaystyle=\bm{\lambda}(tn+x,y)+\mu,

\displaystyle\text{ for all }t>t_{+},~~x,y\in\{0,\dots,n\},

(3.8b)

\displaystyle\bm{\lambda}((t-1)n+x,y)

\displaystyle=\bm{\lambda}(tn+x,y)-\mu^{-},

\displaystyle\text{ for all }t<t_{-},~~x,y\in\{0,\dots,m\}.

Here
\mathbb{Y}^{-}:=\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}\mathbb{Y}^{-}_{n}
with
\mathbb{Y}^{-}_{n}:=\{(\lambda^{-}_{1},\lambda^{-}_{2},\dotsc,\lambda^{-}_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}^{n}\mid\lambda^{-}_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda^{-}_{n}\}
.

The proof of
Proposition

3.11
is based on a simple monotonicity property of the column internal insertion, explained next.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
P\in\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)
and
\widetilde{P}=\mathcal{C}_{[k]}(P)\in\mathrm{SST}(\widetilde{\lambda}/\widetilde{\rho},n)
for some admissible column
k
. Fix
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and let
(r,c)\in\lambda/\rho
,
(\widetilde{r},\widetilde{c})\in\widetilde{\lambda}/\widetilde{\rho}
be the cell containing the
j
-th leftmost
i
label respectively in
P
,
\widetilde{P}
(assuming it exists). Then

(3.9)

r\geq\widetilde{r}\qquad\text{and}\qquad c\leq\widetilde{c}.

In words, any tagged cell of
P
moves weakly in up-right direction under column internal insertion.

Proof.

This is a straightforward property of the column insertion.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.11
.

Without loss of generality we can assume that the field
\bm{\lambda}
is fully refined and as a result the tableaux
P,Q
are partially standard. By
Proposition

3.4
, we have, for all
\tau\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(3.10)

\bm{\lambda}(x,y+\tau)=\bm{\lambda}[\iota_{2}^{\tau}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})](n,0),

where
(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})=\iota_{1}^{x-n}\circ\iota_{2}^{y}(P,Q)
. By
Proposition

3.9
the operator
\iota_{2}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})
can be realized as a column internal insertion in
\widetilde{P}
at a column determined from
\widetilde{Q}
. Fix
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, so that
\widetilde{P}
has a unique
i
-cell and let
(r_{0},c_{0})
be its coordinate. Similarly define
(r_{\tau},c_{\tau})
be the coordinate of the
i
-cell of the
P
-tableau of
\iota_{2}^{\tau}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})
. By
Lemma

3.12
, the sequence
r_{0},r_{1},\dots
is weakly decreasing and since it is also bounded from below by 1, there exists
\tau^{\prime}
such that
r_{\tau^{\prime}}=r_{\tau^{\prime}+1}=\cdots
. Since the tagged cell was arbitrary, there must exists
\tau_{*}
such that the content of each row of the
P
-tableau of
\iota_{2}^{\tau}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})
remains constant for
\tau\geq\tau_{*}
. Setting
\tau=tn
and using the identity
\iota_{2}^{n}=\iota_{1}^{n}
we have

\iota_{2}^{tn}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})=\iota_{1}^{tn}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q}),

and the above argument applied to the
Q
-tableau of
\iota_{1}^{tn}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})
proves that there exists
t_{+}
large enough such that, for all
t\geq t_{+}
, the row content of each of the tableau of the pair
\iota_{1}^{tn}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})
does not change. Such stabilization of row contents implies, following the internal insertions in the computation of the transition

(3.11)

\iota_{2}^{tn}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})\to\iota_{2}^{tn+n}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})=\mathbf{cRSK}~\iota_{2}^{tn}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q}),

that there exists an array
\mu
such that

(3.12)

\bm{\lambda}[\iota_{2}^{tn}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})](2n,0)=\bm{\lambda}[\iota_{2}^{tn}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})](n,0)+\mu.

Here we used the fact that
\bm{\lambda}[\iota_{2}^{tn}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})](n,0)
is the external shape of the tableaux
\iota_{2}^{tn}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})
. Moreover the array
\mu
must necessarily be a partition for the relation (
3.12
) to hold for arbitrarily large
t
. Combining relations (
3.10
) and (
3.12
) completes the proof of (
3.8a
). The proof of (
3.8b
) follows an analogous argument and therefore we omit it.
∎

Corollary 3.13
.

Consider
(P_{t},Q_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
the cRSK dynamics with initial data
(P,Q)
. Let
\lambda/\rho
be the common shape of
P,Q
with
\lambda,\rho\in\mathbb{S}_{\ell}
. Then, there exist
\mu=(\mu_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}>0)\in\mathbb{Y}
,
t_{+}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mu^{-}=(0<\mu_{k}^{-}\leq\cdots\leq\mu_{1}^{-})\in\mathbb{Y}^{-}
and
t_{-}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
such that, for all
t\geq t_{+}

P_{t}=P_{t_{+}}+(t-t_{+})\times(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{\ell^{\prime}},0,\dots,0),\qquad Q_{t}=Q_{t_{+}}+(t-t_{+})\times(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{\ell^{\prime}},0,\dots,0),

and, for all
t\leq t_{-}

P_{t}=P_{t_{-}}+(t-t_{-})\times(0,\dots,0,\mu^{-}_{k},\dots,\mu^{-}_{1}),\qquad Q_{t}=Q_{t_{-}}+(t-t_{-})\times(0,\dots,0,\mu^{-}_{k},\dots,\mu^{-}_{1}).

Here, if
T=(\lambda^{(0)}\preceq\cdots\preceq\lambda^{(n)})
is a semistandard Young tableau, we denoted by
T+\nu=(\lambda^{(0)}+\nu\preceq\cdots\preceq\lambda^{(n)}+\nu)
.

Based on
corollary

3.13
, we define give the following two definitions.

Definition 3.14

(Stability)
.

Let
(P,Q)\in\mathrm{SST}(\lambda,\rho,n)
and for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
let
(P_{t},Q_{t})=\mathbf{cRSK}^{t}(P,Q)
. We call the pair
(P,Q)

stable
with
asymptotic shape

\mu=(\mu_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}>0)
if

P_{t}=P+t\times(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{\ell^{\prime}},0,\dots,0),\qquad Q_{t}=Q+t\times(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{\ell^{\prime}},0,\dots,0)\qquad\text{for all }t\geq 0.

Similarly, we call the pair
(P,Q)

negatively stable
with
negative asymptotic shape

\mu^{-}=(0<\mu_{k}^{-}\leq\cdots\leq\mu_{1}^{-})
if

P_{t}=P+t\times(0,\dots,0,\mu_{k}^{-},\dots,\mu_{1}^{-}),\qquad Q_{t}=Q+t\times(0,\dots,0,\mu_{k}^{-},\dots,\mu_{1}^{-})\qquad\text{for all }t\leq 0.

The next definition should be paired with
Definition

1.6
given in the introduction.

Definition 3.15
.

Given a pair of semistandard Young tableaux
(P,Q)
of skew shape we define the map

(3.13)

\Phi^{-}\colon(P,Q)\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho\in\mathbb{S}}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}\longrightarrow(H_{1}^{-},H_{2}^{-})\in\bigsqcup_{\mu^{-}\in\mathbb{Y}^{-}}\mathrm{HWT}(\mu^{-},n)^{2},

where
\mathbb{Y}^{-}:=\bigsqcup_{k\geq 1}\{(\mu_{k},\dotsc,\mu_{1})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{k}\mid\mu_{k}\leq\cdots\leq\mu_{1}\}
, and letting
\bm{\lambda}=\bm{\lambda}[P,Q]
, we set

(3.14a)

\displaystyle\#\{i\text{-cells at row }j\text{ of }H_{1}^{-}\}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to-\infty}\bm{\lambda}_{L+j}(t,i)-\bm{\lambda}_{L+j}(t,i-1),

(3.14b)

\displaystyle\#\{i\text{-cells at row }j\text{ of }H_{2}^{-}\}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to-\infty}\bm{\lambda}_{L+j}(t+i,0)-\bm{\lambda}_{L+j}(t+i-1,0).

Above
L=\max\{i\geq 0\mid\lim_{t\to-\infty}\bm{\lambda}_{i}(t+i,0)\neq-\infty\}
, where
L=0
if all the limits are
-\infty
.

In other words, the map
\Phi^{-}(P,Q)
collects the rows of negatively stable pairs
(P_{t},Q_{t})
into horizontally weak tableaux with shape given by a reverse partition
\mu^{-}
. We will prove in
Theorem

6.15
that the negatively asymptotic shape
\mu^{-}
is the sorting of the positively asymptotic shape
\mu
. We will also prove that
H_{i}^{-}
can be computed from
H_{i}
through the use of the combinatorial
R
-matrix.

Example 3.16
.

Let
(P,Q)=(P_{0},Q_{0})
from
Figure

7
. Then, we have

3.5.
Kernels of tableaux

By
Proposition

2.4
, given a Fomin field of interlacing signatures
\bm{\lambda}
on
\mathscr{C}_{n}
and a signature
\nu\in\mathbb{S}
we can define another Fomin field of signature
\overline{\bm{\lambda}}(p)=(\bm{\lambda}(p)^{\prime}+\nu^{\prime})^{\prime}
for all
p\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
by globally adding the signature
\nu
to
\bm{\lambda}
column-by-column. In a similar fashion we can globally substract from the field
\bm{\lambda}
a signature, provided such operation returns a signature at each point
p\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
.

Definition 3.17
.

Let
\bm{\lambda}
be a Fomin field of signatures. We define
\nu=\mathrm{ker}(\bm{\lambda})
to be the maximal signature
\nu
such that
\bm{\lambda}(p)^{\prime}-\nu^{\prime}
is a transposed signature for all
p\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
. Similarly, given
(P,Q)\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}
, we define
\ker(P,Q)=\ker(\bm{\lambda}[P,Q])
.

Notice that if
\nu,\eta\in\mathbb{S}
are signatures such that both
\bm{\lambda}(p)^{\prime}-\nu^{\prime}
and
\bm{\lambda}(p)^{\prime}-\eta^{\prime}
are transposed signatures, then also the signature
\max(\nu,\eta)
has the same property. This implies that there exists a unique maximal signature
\nu
such that
\bm{\lambda}(p)^{\prime}-\nu^{\prime}
is a transposed signature for all
p\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
justifying
Definition

3.17
.

We now state some basic proposition of the kernel.

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
(P,Q)\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}
and let
\bm{\lambda}=\bm{\lambda}[P,Q]
be the corresponding field of interlacing partitions on
\mathscr{C}_{n}
. Let also
\mu
be the asymptotic shape of the pair
(P,Q)
and
\nu=\ker(P,Q)
. The following properties hold.

(I)

We have
\nu=\ker\iota_{1}(P,Q)=\ker\iota_{2}(P,Q)=\ker\mathbf{cRSK}(P,Q)
.

(II)

Fix a down right loop
\mathbf{p}
of
\mathscr{C}_{n}
and let
\widetilde{\nu}
be the maximal signature such that
\bm{\lambda}(p)^{\prime}-\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}
is a transposed signature for all
p\in\mathbf{p}
. Then
\widetilde{\nu}=\nu
.

(III)

If
\lambda
is the shape of
P
, then
\ell(\lambda)=\ell(\nu)+\ell(\mu)
.

(IV)

We have
\nu_{i}=\lim_{t\to+\infty}\bm{\lambda}_{\ell(\mu)+i}(p+t\,\mathbf{e}_{1})
for all
i=1,\dots\ell(\nu)
and all
p\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
.

(V)

We have
\nu_{i}=\lim_{t\to-\infty}\bm{\lambda}_{i}(x-t,y)
for all
i=1,\dots\ell(\nu)
and all
x,y\in\{0,\dots,n\}
.

Proof.

Properties
(I)
are immediate consequences of
Proposition

3.4

(I)
,
(II)
,
(III)
, which express the Fomin fields of
\iota_{2}(P,Q),\iota_{1}(P,Q),\mathbf{cRSK}(P,Q)
as coordinate shifts of
\bm{\lambda}
. As a result the corresponding kernels are all identical.
To show
(II)
, notice that
\nu^{\prime}\leq\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}
, since the constraints for signature
\nu
contain those for
\widetilde{\nu}
. On the other hand, define the field
\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}
to be the (unique) Fomin field such that
\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}(p)^{\prime}=\bm{\lambda}(p)^{\prime}-\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}
for all
p\in\mathbf{p}
. Then, by
Proposition

2.4
the relation
\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}(p)^{\prime}=\bm{\lambda}(p)^{\prime}-\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}
holds for all
p\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
, which implies that
\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}\leq\nu^{\prime}
and as a result that
\widetilde{\nu}=\nu
.

To show
(III)
consider, for a fixed
p\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
and a fixed
i
the sequence
L_{t}(i)=\bm{\lambda}_{i}(p+t\,\mathbf{e}_{1})
. Then, by monotonicity properties of the Fomin growth operator the sequence
L_{t}(i)
is monotonic increasing and by
Proposition

3.11
,
L_{t}(i)
is diverging in
t
for
i\in\{1,\dots,\ell(\mu)\}
and converging to a finite value
L_{\infty}(i)
for
i\in\{\ell(\mu)+1,\dots\ell(\lambda)\}
. Define the signature
\widetilde{\nu}_{i}=L_{\infty}(\ell(\mu)+i)
, for
i=1\dots,\ell(\lambda)-\ell(\mu)
and let
t_{*}
such that
\bm{\lambda}_{\ell(\mu)+i}(p+t\,\mathbf{e}_{1})=\widetilde{\nu}_{i}
for all
t\geq t_{*}
. Fix now
\mathbf{p}
a down right loop such that
(0,0)\in\mathbf{p}
. Then it is clear that
\widetilde{\nu}
is the maximal signature such that
\bm{\lambda}(p+(t_{*}+n)\,\mathbf{e}_{1})^{\prime}-\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}
is a transposed signature for all
p\in\mathbf{p}
, which implies, by
(I)
, that
\widetilde{\nu}=\nu
. Notice that we also showed
(III)
. One can prove
(V)
through analogous argument as for
(IV)
.
∎

4.
Box-ball system and its linearization

4.1.
Box-ball system dynamics

In
Section

1.1
we introduced the BBS dynamics with space of states
\mathsf{BBS}
and transfer matrix
\mathsf{T}
. The support of a state
B\in\mathsf{BBS}
is
\operatorname{supp}(B):=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}\mid b_{x}=1\}
. In addition, we define

\mathsf{BBS}_{>0}:=\left\{B=(b_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathsf{BBS}~\middle|~b_{x}=0\text{ for all }x\leq 0\right\},

the set of binary sequences supported in
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. For any fixed
B_{0}\in\mathsf{BBS}
, the BBS dynamics is the sequence
(B_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
defined by

(4.1)

\displaystyle B_{t}=\mathsf{T}^{t}(B_{0}),

where for
t<0
we interpret
\mathsf{T}^{t}=(\mathsf{T}^{-1})^{|t|}
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
B\in\mathsf{BBS}
. A partition
\mu=(\mu_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{\ell}>0)
is called the
asymptotic shape
of
B
if, for all sufficiently large
t
, the configuration
\mathsf{T}^{t}(B)
contains exactly
\ell
clusters of consecutive balls, of lengths
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{\ell},
counted with multiplicity.

Notice that it is in fact non-trivial that the BBS dynamics stabilizes and that eventually states consist of constant (in time) clusters of consecutive particles evolving freely. This a well established fact; see
[
104
]
.

One could also define the
backward asymptotic shape
of a BBS state
B
to be a partition
\widetilde{\mu}=(\widetilde{\mu}_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\widetilde{\mu}_{m}>0)
such that, for all sufficiently large
t
, the configuration
\mathsf{T}^{-t}(B)
contains exactly
m
clusters of consecutive balls, of lengths
\widetilde{\mu}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\mu}_{m}
counted with multiplicity. Nevertheless it is well known, and illustrated by
Figure

1
, that the backward asymptotic shape coincides with the asymptotic shape
\mu
. This result was proven first in
[
104
]
and recalled in
Theorem

4.3
below.

4.2.
Linearization of the BBS dynamics

We now recall the linearization of the single-species BBS by the
Kerov–Kirillov–Reshetikhin (KKR) bijection

[
56
,
57
]
; see also
[
37
,
30
,
32
,
64
]
. We begin by introducing the target space of this bijection.

Definition 4.2
.

A
rigged configuration
is a pair
(\mu,J)
, where
\mu
is a partition and
J\in\mathcal{K}(\mu)
is a collection of integers, called
riggings
. We recall the set
\mathcal{K}(\mu)
was introduced in (
1.22a
) We write
\mathsf{RC}
for the set of all rigged configurations.
Finally we define the set

\mathsf{RC}^{\prime}=\{(\mu,J)\in\mathsf{RC}\mid J_{i}\geq-\mu_{i}\}.

Theorem 4.3

(KKR bijection and linearization,
[
64
]
)
.

There exists a bijection
\mathrm{KKR}\colon\mathsf{BBS}\leftrightarrow\mathsf{RC}
such that, if
\mathrm{KKR}(B)=(\mu,J)
, then
\mu
is the asymptotic shape of
B
(and also its backward asymptotic shape). The restriction to states
B\in\mathsf{BBS}_{>0}
gives a bijection
\mathrm{KKR}\colon\mathsf{BBS}_{>0}\leftrightarrow\mathsf{RC}^{\prime}
.
Moreover, defining the map
\hat{T}(\mu,J)=(\mu,J+\mu)
, then
\mathrm{KKR}\circ\mathsf{T}=\hat{T}\circ\mathrm{KKR}
, as illustrated by the commutative diagram (
1.3
).

Let us describe explicitly the bijection
\mathrm{KKR}
; see also
[
59
]
for the most general version for
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n}
. An example of
Theorem

4.3
is given in
Example

4.6

Definition 4.4

(The KKR bijection)
.

Let
B=(b_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathsf{BBS}
and let
\ell=\min\{\operatorname{supp}(B)\}
and
r=\max\{\operatorname{supp}(B)\}
denote the positions of the leftmost and rightmost particles. The rigged configuration
\mathrm{KKR}(B)
is constructed inductively by scanning the interval
[\ell,r]
from left to right and producing a sequence or rigged configurations
(\mu^{[k]},J^{[k]})_{k\in\{\ell,\dots,r\}}
. To each rigged configuration
(\mu^{[k]},J^{[k]})
we associate the
vacancy numbers

(4.2)

p_{i}^{[k]}:=k-2\sum_{j=1}^{\ell(\mu^{[k]})}\min(\mu_{j}^{[k]},i).

Given a rigged configuration
(\mu^{[k]},J^{[k]})
, we say that
i
is a
singular string
of length
\mu_{i}^{[k]}
if

J_{i}^{[k]}=p_{\mu_{i}^{[k]}}^{[k]}.

Set
(\mu^{[\ell-1]},J^{[\ell-1]})=(\emptyset,\emptyset)
. For
k=\ell,\ell+1,\dots,r
, define
(\mu^{[k]},J^{[k]})
from
(\mu^{[k-1]},J^{[k-1]})
as follows:

(1)

If
b_{k}=0
, then
(\mu^{[k]},J^{[k]})=(\mu^{[k-1]},J^{[k-1]})
.

(2)

If
b_{k}=1
, choose one of the longest singular strings in
(\mu^{[k-1]},J^{[k-1]})
and replace it with a singular string whose length is larger by one (with vacancy numbers computed with respect to
k
). We assume, by convention, that there always exists a singular string of length
0
.

After the step
k=r
, we yield
\mathrm{KKR}(B)=(\mu^{[r]},J^{[r]})
.

The inverse of the map
\mathrm{KKR}
is constructed next.

Definition 4.5

(The inverse KKR bijection)
.

Let
(\mu,J)\in\mathsf{RC}
be a rigged configuration. We reconstruct the BBS state
B=(b_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}=\mathrm{KKR}^{-1}(\mu,J)
recursively from right to left. First determine the position of the rightmost particle by

(4.3)

\displaystyle r=\max_{1\leq s\leq\ell(\mu)}p_{\mu_{s}}^{[J_{s}]}.

(Here
p_{\mu_{s}}^{[J_{s}]}
is computed as in (
4.2
) replacing
\mu^{[k]}
by
\mu
and
k
by
J_{s}
.)
Set
b_{k}=0
for all
k>r
and initialize
(\mu^{[r]},J^{[r]})=(\mu,J)
.

For
k=r,r-1,r-2,r-3,\ldots
, if
(\mu^{[k]},J^{[k]})=(\emptyset,\emptyset)
, then terminate, and otherwise construct
(\mu^{[k-1]},J^{[k-1]})
and
b_{k}
as follows:

(1)

If
(\mu^{[k]},J^{[k]})
contains no singular strings, then set

b_{k}=0,\qquad\quad(\mu^{[k-1]},J^{[k-1]})=(\mu^{[k]},J^{[k]}).

(2)

Otherwise set
b_{k}=1
and choose one of the shortest singular strings of
(\mu^{[k]},J^{[k]})
and replace it with a singular string whose length is smaller by one (with vacancy numbers computed with respect to
\mu^{[k-1]}
).

Suppose this terminates at
\ell
, then we set
b_{i}=0
for all
i<\ell
.

Example 4.6
.

Consider the BBS configuration
B
depicted in
Figure

1

(4.4)

B=\,\,\begin{matrix}{\color[rgb]{0.275,0.51,0.71}\cdots}&{\color[rgb]{0.275,0.51,0.71}\overline{1}}&{\color[rgb]{0.275,0.51,0.71}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.275,0.51,0.71}1}&{\color[rgb]{0.275,0.51,0.71}2}&{\color[rgb]{0.275,0.51,0.71}3}&{\color[rgb]{0.275,0.51,0.71}4}&{\color[rgb]{0.275,0.51,0.71}5}&{\color[rgb]{0.275,0.51,0.71}\cdots}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdots}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}0}&1&1&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}0}&1&1&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}0}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\cdots}\end{matrix}\,\,.

Then, we can compute
\mathrm{KKR}(B)=(\mu,J)
, with
\mu=(3,1)
and
J=(-4,0)
, as show in the right panel of
Figure

13
.
Similar computations show that, letting
(B_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the BBS dynamics with initial data
B_{0}=B
, we have

\mathrm{KKR}(B_{t})=(\mu,J+t\mu)\qquad\text{for all }t\in\mathbb{Z},

in agreement with
Theorem

4.3
.

Figure 13
.
In the left panel, a representation of a rigged configurations
(\mu^{[k]},J^{[k]})
as the Young diagrams
\mu^{[k]}
with values of
J_{i}
placed at the right of row
i
and vacancy numbers
p^{[k]}_{i}
placed at the left of rows of
\mu^{[k]}
of length
i
(see, e.g.,
[
47
]
). In the right panel, the evaluation of the map
\mathrm{KKR}(B)
with
B
given in (
4.4
).

4.3.
cRSK
, BBS and KKR

Define the set

(4.5)

\mathrm{SST}(1)=\bigsqcup_{\rho,\lambda\in\mathbb{S}}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,1)

of semi standard tableaux of skew shape with all labels equal to 1. (Notice that
\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,1)
is either empty or it consists of a single element
T=(\rho\preceq\lambda)
.)
In this subsection, we build on the equivalence between BBS and cRSK dynamics on pairs of identical tableaux
(T,T)
with
T\in\mathrm{SST}(1)
stated in
Remark

1.5
and formalized in
Proposition

4.10
. The main result is
Proposition

4.15
, which relates the shape of
T
with the rigging associated to
B
.

Definition 4.7

(The projection
\Pr
)
.

Let
T=(\rho\preceq\lambda)
be a tableau in
\mathrm{SST}(1)
. Then we can define the projection
\Pr\colon\mathrm{SST}(1)\rightarrow\mathsf{BBS}
as

\Pr(T)=\lambda^{\prime}-\rho^{\prime}.

Example 4.8
.

Consider the tableau
T\in\mathrm{SST}(1)
as

T=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{gray}\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$\hss}\vss}}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{gray}\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$\hss}\vss}}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{gray}\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$\hss}\vss}}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\cdots$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=8.4pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=8.4pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont &\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{gray}\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$\hss}\vss}}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 1$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 1$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 2415.4pt}}$}\,.

Then, we have
\Pr(T)=B
, where
B
is given in (
4.4
).

Lemma 4.9
.

The map

(4.10)

\Xi\colon T\in\mathrm{SST}(1)\longrightarrow(B,\nu)\in\mathsf{BBS}\times\mathbb{S},\qquad\text{with}\qquad B=\Pr(T)\quad\text{and}\quad\nu=\ker(T,T)

is a bijection.
Given
B=(b_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathsf{BBS}
and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}
, the inverse
T=\Xi^{-1}(B,\nu)
is constructed as follows. Setting
\ell=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{1}_{b_{x}=0}\mathbf{1}_{b_{x+1}=1}
, we define the signature
\overline{\rho}
as

(4.11)

\overline{\rho}_{i}=\sup\left\{j<\overline{\rho}_{i-1}~\middle|~(b_{j},b_{j+1})=(0,1)\right\},\qquad\text{for }i=1,\dots,\ell,

where we assume the convention
\overline{\rho}_{-1}=+\infty
. Then, we set

\rho^{\prime}=\overline{\rho}^{\prime}+\nu^{\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lambda^{\prime}=\rho^{\prime}+B.

Proof.

The map constructed in (
4.11
) can be described in words as follows. Given a BBS configuration
B=(b_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathsf{BBS}
, we scan it from the right to the left and set
\overline{\rho}_{i}=r_{i}
where
r_{i}
is the location of the
i
-th hole immediately to the left of a particle. Then, we set
\rho^{\prime}=\overline{\rho}^{\prime}+\nu^{\prime}
,
\lambda^{\prime}=\rho^{\prime}+B
and the tableau
T=(\rho\preceq\lambda)
clearly has the property that
\Pr(T)=B
and
\ker(T,T)=\nu
, by
Proposition

3.18
,
(II)
since
\nu
is the maximal signature one can remove from
T
without breaking the semistandard property.
∎

The equivalence between the cRSK dynamics of the BBS dynamics is summarized as follows.
Recall that
\mathsf{T}
denotes the one time step evolution of the BBS described in
Section

1.1
.

Proposition 4.10
.

For any
T\in\mathrm{SST}(1)
, denote
T^{\prime}=\mathbf{cRSK}(T)
if
(T^{\prime},T^{\prime})=\mathbf{cRSK}(T,T)
. We have
\Pr\circ\,\mathbf{cRSK}(T)=\mathsf{T}\circ\Pr(T)
.

Proof.

This is essentially
Remark

1.5
. If
T=(\rho\preceq\lambda)
and
T^{\prime}=(\lambda\preceq\eta)=\mathbf{cRSK}(T)
, then the increments
B=\lambda^{\prime}-\rho^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}=\eta^{\prime}-\lambda^{\prime}
are related as
B^{\prime}=\mathsf{T}(B)
, implying
\Pr\circ\,\mathbf{cRSK}(T)=\mathsf{T}\circ\Pr(T)
.
∎

Let
T=(\rho\preceq\lambda)\in\mathrm{SST}(1)
.
We will have an explicit relation between the inner shape
\rho
and the rigged configuration
(\mu,J)=\mathrm{KKR}\circ\Pr(T)
; see
Proposition

4.15
below. For this, a result from
[
68
]
will play a key role. For its statement we introduce two functions
\Gamma_{B},\tau_{\mu,J}\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition 4.11
.

Let
B\in\mathsf{BBS}
and let
B_{t}=(b^{t}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
be its evolution under the BBS dynamics with initial data
B_{0}=B
. We define the function
\Gamma_{B}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}

\Gamma_{B}(i)=\sum_{t\geq 0}\sum_{j\leq i}b_{j}^{t}.

The function
\Gamma_{B}(i)
counts the number of balls weakly to the left of location
i
in all configurations
B_{0}(=B),B_{1},B_{2},\ldots
.
Since the original configuration
B
is finitely supported and in the BBS dynamics every particle has positive drift, then
\Gamma_{B}(i)
is always a finite quantity.

The following lemma provides a way to reconstruct the configuration
B
from the function
\Gamma_{B}
. For any function
f\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{C}
we define its discrete first and second derivatives

\nabla f(i)=f(i)-f(i-1),\qquad\qquad\Delta f(i)=f(i)-2f(i-1)+f(i-2).

Lemma 4.12
.

For any
B\in\mathsf{BBS}
, we have

\Delta\Gamma_{B}(i)=\mathbf{1}_{b_{i-1}=0}\mathbf{1}_{b_{i}=1}.

In particular,
\Gamma_{B}
is piecewise linear, and its slopes
\nabla\Gamma_{B}(i)
have increments being either 0 or 1.

Proof.

Note that
\Delta\Gamma_{B}(i)=\sum_{t\geq 0}(b^{t}_{i}-b^{t}_{i-1})
. Also observe that in the BBS dynamics, the possible configurations of four elements
b^{t}_{j-1},b^{t}_{j},b^{t+1}_{j-1},b^{t+1}_{j}
are, for any
t\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}

(4.12)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}b^{t}_{j-1}&b^{t}_{j}\\[5.0pt]
b^{t+1}_{j-1}&b^{t+1}_{j}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}.

Let us focus on the behavior of the partial sum
\sum_{t=s}^{k}(b^{t}_{i}-b^{t}_{i-1})
for some
s\leq k
. By checking all cases in (
4.12
), we see that if
(b^{s}_{i-1},b^{s}_{i})\in\{(0,0),(1,0),(1,1)\}
, then we can always find
k\geq s
such that

\sum_{t=s}^{k}(b^{t}_{i}-b^{t}_{i-1})=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad(b^{k+1}_{i-1},b^{k+1}_{i})\in\{(0,0),(1,0),(1,1)\}.

If
(b^{s}_{i-1},b^{s}_{i})\in\{(0,0),(1,1)\}
one can take
k=s
. On the other hand if
(b^{s}_{i-1},b^{s}_{i})=(1,0)
, then one can take
k=s+1
. This shows that
\Delta\Gamma_{B}(i)=0
whenever
(b^{0}_{i-1},b^{0}_{i})\in\{(0,0),(1,0),(1,1)\}
. Similarly, if
(b^{0}_{i-1},b^{0}_{i})=(0,1)
, we have
\Delta\Gamma_{B}(i)=1+\sum_{t\geq 1}(b^{t}_{i}-b^{t}_{i-1})=1
, since
\sum_{t\geq 1}(b^{t}_{i}-b^{t}_{i-1})=0
by the previous argument.
∎

Next we introduce another function
\tau_{\mu,J}\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition 4.13
.

For any
(\mu,J)\in\mathsf{RC}
define the
ultradiscrete tau function

\tau_{\mu,J}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
as

\tau_{\mu,J}(i)=-\min_{n\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}}\left\{\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\ell}\left(\mu_{\alpha}-i\right)n_{\alpha}+\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{\ell}\min(\mu_{\alpha},\mu_{\beta})n_{\alpha}n_{\beta}\right\}.

In
[
68
]
, the authors showed the two functions
\Gamma_{B}
and
\tau_{\mu,J}
coincide.

Proposition 4.14

(
[
68
, Theorem 7.4 with
n=1
]
)
.

Let
B\in\mathsf{BBS}
and let
(\mu,J)=\mathrm{KKR}(B)
. Then, we have

\Gamma_{B}(i)=\tau_{\mu,J}(i)\qquad\text{for all }i\in\mathbb{Z}.

Combining
lemma

4.12
with
proposition

4.14
, we obtain the following result.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
T=(\rho\preceq\lambda)\in\mathrm{SST}(1)
and let
(B,\nu)=\Xi(T)
, where the map
\Xi
is defined in (
4.10
). Let
(\mu,J)=\mathrm{KKR}(B)
. Then, we have

\lvert\rho\rvert=|\nu|+\lvert J\rvert+\lvert\mu\rvert+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell(\mu)}2(j-1)\mu_{j}.

Proof.

By
Lemma

4.9
, it suffices to prove the case
\nu=\varnothing
. Otherwise, one can apply the proposition to the tableau
\overline{T}=(\overline{\rho}\preceq\overline{\lambda})
with
\overline{\rho}^{\prime}=\rho^{\prime}-\nu^{\prime}
and
\overline{\lambda}^{\prime}=\lambda^{\prime}-\nu^{\prime}
, for which one has
|\overline{\rho}|=|\rho|-|\nu|
.

Assume
\nu=\varnothing
and denote by
\ell=\ell(\mu)=\ell(\rho)=\ell(\lambda)
. Basic algebraic manipulations show that the tau function
\tau_{\mu,J}
can be written as

(4.13)

\tau_{\mu,J}(i)=-\min_{\gamma\in\{0,\dots,\ell\}}\left\{\sigma_{\mu,J}(\gamma)-i\gamma\right\},

where the function
\sigma_{\mu,J}
is defined as

(4.14)

\sigma_{\mu,J}(\gamma)=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}\\
|n|=\gamma\end{subarray}}\left\{\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\ell}(J_{\alpha}+\mu_{\alpha})n_{\alpha}+2\sum_{1\leq\alpha<\beta\leq\ell}\mu_{\alpha}n_{\alpha}n_{\beta}\right\}.

Moreover, an immediate consequence of
Proposition

4.14
and
Lemma

4.12
is that
\tau_{\mu,J}
is piecewise linear with slopes
\nabla\tau_{\mu,J}(i)
having increments being
0
or
1
. Then, by expression (
4.13
), we have

\nabla\tau_{\mu,J}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\{0,\dots,\ell\}\qquad\text{surjectively}

and there exists a strictly increasing function
\theta\colon\{0,\dots,\ell+1\}\to\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
such that

\tau_{\mu,J}(i)=\gamma i-\sigma_{\mu,J}(\gamma),\qquad\text{for all }i\in\{\theta(\gamma),\dots,\theta(\gamma+1)\},

for
\gamma\in\{0,\dots,\ell\}
, with
\theta(0)=-\infty
and
\theta(\ell+1)=+\infty
.

Lemma

4.12
also indicates that the locations
\theta(\gamma)
where
\tau_{\mu,J}
changes slope are

\theta(\gamma)=\inf\{j>\theta(\gamma-1)\mid(b_{j},b_{j+1})=(0,1)\}.

In particular, comparing the above expression with (
4.11
), we have

(4.15)

\theta(\ell-i+1)=\rho_{i},\qquad\text{for }i\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}.

On the other hand, the equality

\tau_{\mu,J}\bigl(\theta(\gamma)\bigr)=\tau_{\mu,J}\bigl(\theta(\gamma-1)\bigr)+(\gamma-1)\Bigl(\theta(\gamma)-\theta(\gamma-1)\Bigr),

implies that the coefficients
\theta(\gamma)
can be written as

\theta(\gamma)=\nabla\sigma_{\mu,J}(\gamma),\qquad\text{for }\gamma\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}.

The above analysis produces the following chain of identities

(4.16)

\lvert J\rvert+\lvert\mu\rvert+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}2(j-1)\mu_{j}=\sigma_{\mu,J}(\ell)=\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\ell}\nabla\sigma_{\mu,J}(\gamma)=\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\ell}\theta(\gamma)=\lvert\rho\rvert,

where the first equality is a simple evaluation of (
4.14
) and the fourth equality comes from (
4.15
). This completes the proof.
∎

5.
Affine crystals and leading paths

5.1.
Affine crystal structure on horizontally weak tableaux

In this subsection, we equip the set of horizontally weak tableaux
\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
with an affine crystal structure.
This is achieved by identifying
\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
with a tensor product of certain Kirillov–Reshetikhin (KR) crystals that are modeled by single row semistandard Young tableauax; see, e.g.,
[
32
,
51
,
50
]
and
[
96
]
.
Below we will only recall the strictly necessary aspects of Kashiwara’s crystal basis theory and refer the reader to
[
10
]
for a complete treatment.

An
affine crystal
or
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n}
-crystal
(associated to the Drinfel’d–Jimbo affine quantum group
U_{\upsilon}^{\prime}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n})
without derivation; see,
e.g.
,
[
11
]
) is a set with Kashiwara operators and weight function that give it the structure of a special edge-colored weighted directed graph.
Our starting point is the following crystal.

Definition 5.1
.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. For
s\geq 0
, the
Kirillov–Reshetikhin crystal

\mathcal{H}^{1,s}
is the set of semistandard tableaux with labels in
\{1,\dots,n\}
of a single row of length
s
, or equivalently nondecreasing words over
\{1,\dotsc,n\}
of length
s
.
Additionally, we will sometimes consider the elements
h\in\mathcal{H}^{1,s}
as multisets, where we are sorting the elements as necessary.
The Kashiwara operators will be described as a special case of the following definition.

Definition 5.2
.

Fix some
\ell>0
and
\mu\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
.
Define the
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n}
-crystal

(5.1)

\mathcal{H}^{\mu}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}^{1,\mu_{\ell}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{1,\mu_{\ell-1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{H}^{1,\mu_{1}},

as the Cartesian product
\mathcal{H}^{1,\mu_{\ell}}\times\mathcal{H}^{1,\mu_{\ell-1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{H}^{1,\mu_{1}}
with
Kashiwara operators

e_{i},f_{i}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\mu}\to\mathcal{H}^{\mu}\sqcup\{\mathbf{0}\}
for
i=0,1,2,\dotsc,n-1
defined by the
signature rule
as follows.
Consider some

H:=h_{\ell}\otimes h_{\ell-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{1}=(h_{\ell},h_{\ell-1},\dotsc,h_{1})\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}.

Fix some
i=0,1,2,\dotsc,n-1
and let
j:=i+1\pmod{n}
.

(1)

Make a single word
w
from
H
in the following way. For
e_{i}
or
f_{i}
with
i=1,\ldots,n-1
,
w=h_{\ell}\cdots h_{2}h_{1}
obtained by concatenating all elements in
H
, while for
i=0
,
we form
w=\tilde{h}_{\ell}\tilde{h}_{\ell-1}\cdots\tilde{h}_{1}
with
\tilde{h}_{k}
being the reverse of
h_{k}
.

(2)

In
w
, replace all
i
’s
5
5

5

When
i=0
, we are replacing all
n
’s with “
)
”s.
with “
)
”s and all
j
’s with “
(
”s and ignore all other letters.

(3)

Erase each consecutive symbols “
(
” “
)
” until no such consecutive symbols appear; i.e., the remaining pattern of the symbols becomes
)\cdots))((\cdots(
.

(4e)

For the remaining symbols, if there is no “
(
” then
e_{i}(H)=\mathbf{0}
, and otherwise
e_{i}(H)
is given by replacing the
j
corresponding to the leftmost “
(
” with
i
in
H
.

(4f)

For the remaining symbols, if there is no “
)
” then
f_{i}(H)=\mathbf{0}
and otherwise
f_{i}(H)
is given by replacing the
i
corresponding to the rightmost “
)
” with
j
in
H
.

We use the weight function
\wt
given in
definition

2.7
; note
\wt(H)=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\wt(h_{j})
.
The
crystal graph
of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
is the (weighted) directed graph with vertices
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
and colored directed edges
H\xrightarrow{i}H^{\prime}
, for
H,H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
and
i=0,1,\dotsc,n-1
, if and only if
H^{\prime}=f_{i}(H)
, or equivalently
H=e_{i}(H^{\prime})
. See
Figure

14
for an example.

We identify elements of
\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
with
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
by having an element
H=h_{\ell}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{1}\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
correspond to the tableau in
\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
whose
j
th row is
h_{j}
.

Example 5.3
.

Let
H=24\otimes 233\otimes 2\otimes 12\otimes 12\otimes 133\otimes 23\in\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
with
\mu=(2,3,2,2,1,3,2)
and
n=4
. Applying the signature rule for
i=2
yields:

(5.2)

\begin{matrix}(1)&&w=&2&4&2&3&3&2&1&2&1&2&1&3&3&2&3\\
(2)&&&)&&)&(&(&)&&)&&)&&(&(&)&(\\
(3)&&&)&&)&&&&&&&)&&(&&&(\\
(4\mathrm{e})&&&)&&)&&&&&&&)&&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0})}&&&(\\
(4\mathrm{f})&&&)&&)&&&&&&&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(}&&(&&&(\end{matrix}

so that

e_{2}(H)=24\otimes 233\otimes 2\otimes 12\otimes 12\otimes 1{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}3\otimes 23\qquad\text{and}\qquad f_{2}(H)=24\otimes 233\otimes 2\otimes 12\otimes 1{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}\otimes 133\otimes 23.

Figure 14
.
In the left panel the KR crystal graph
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
for
\mu=(2,1,1)
and
n=2
. We have arranged vertically its classically connected components and framed in dotted contours elements with same energy function
D
. In the right panel we removed all
0
arrows such that
D(f_{0}(b))\neq D(b)+1
. The red shaded component is no longer connected to the rest of the graph.

Remark 5.4
.

In the crystal graph of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
, one can check the following three properties, which are an important aspects of the (affine) crystal structure.

(1)

The in and out degree of any color and any vertex is at most one.

(2)

Let
\epsilon_{i}
,
i=1,\dotsc,n
, be the standard bases on
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
; for convenience, we identify
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{n}
.
Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
be the standard dot product on
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
defined by
\langle a,b\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}b_{i}
for
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.
Then for
i=0,\dotsc,n-1
and
b\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
,

(5.3)

\displaystyle\langle\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{i+1},\wt(H)\rangle=\varphi_{i}(H)-\varepsilon_{i}(H),

where

\varepsilon_{i}(H)=\max\{m\mid e_{i}^{m}(H)\neq\mathbf{0}\},\qquad\qquad\varphi_{i}(H)=\max\{m\mid f_{i}^{m}(H)\neq\mathbf{0}\}.

(3)

For
H,H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
satisfying
H^{\prime}=f_{i}(H)
for some
i\in\{0,1,\dotsc,n-1\}
, we have

(5.4)

\displaystyle\wt(H^{\prime})=\wt(H)-\epsilon_{i}+\epsilon_{i+1}.

Remark 5.5
.

Let
\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
be the
promotion operator

[
94
,
93
]
which acts by cyclically relabeling entries
i\to i+1
for
i=1,\dots n-1
and
n\to 1
followed by a reordering within each tensor factor so that elements remain non-decreasing. The Kashiwara operators satisfy the relations

(5.5)

e_{i-1}=\mathrm{pr}^{-1}\circ e_{i}\circ\mathrm{pr}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad f_{i-1}=\mathrm{pr}^{-1}\circ f_{i}\circ\mathrm{pr}

for
i=1\dots,n-1
(see
[
96
]
). In particular the operators
e_{0},f_{0}
can be defined through (
5.5
); see also
[
78
, Thm. 5.5.1]
.

Remark 5.6
.

It was shown in
[
3
]
that the crystal graph
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
is a strongly connected digraph for arbitrary
\mu
and
n
; that is, for any two elements
H,H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
, there exists a
directed
path from
H
to
H^{\prime}
.

5.2.
Classically connected components

The
classically connected components
of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
, are the connected components of the crystal graphs obtained by removing all
0
-arrows; that is, not considering the Kashiwara operators
e_{0},f_{0}
.
It is known
[
54
,
53
,
50
]
that each such component contains a unique element
H_{\mathrm{hw}}
and
H_{\mathrm{lw}}
satisfying, respectively,

e_{i}(H_{\mathrm{hw}})=\mathbf{0}\qquad\text{and}\qquad f_{i}(H_{\mathrm{lw}})=\mathbf{0}\qquad\text{for all}\quad i=1,\dots,n-1.

We call them the
classically highest
and
classically lowest weight elements
, respectively, and denote their corresponding sets as
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{\mathrm{hw}}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{\mathrm{lw}}
.

Example 5.7
.

In
Figure

14
, the graph in the left panel has four classically connected components. The set of classically highest weight elements is

\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{\mathrm{hw}}=\left\{1\otimes 1\otimes 11,\,2\otimes 1\otimes 11,\,1\otimes 2\otimes 11,\,2\otimes 2\otimes 11\right\},

while that of classically lowest weight elements is

\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{\mathrm{lw}}=\left\{2\otimes 2\otimes 22,\,2\otimes 1\otimes 22,\,2\otimes 2\otimes 12,\,2\otimes 2\otimes 11\right\}.

Classically highest and lowest weight elements can be characterized via the
Yamanouchi conditions
on their row reading words (see,
e.g.
,
[
97
]
).

Remark 5.8
.

Let
H=h_{\ell}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{2}\otimes h_{1}\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
.
Since each
h_{j}
is a weakly increasing word, the signature rule (the Yamanouchi condition; see
[
97
, Prop. 7.10.3(d)]
) implies the following constrains.
If
H
is classically highest weight, then
h_{1}
consists of only
1
’s.
Similarly, if
H
is classically lowest weight, then
h_{\ell}
consists of only
n
’s.
More generally, in classically highest weight elements
H
, the letter
i
can only appear in
h_{j}
for
j\geq i
.
In classically lowest weight elements, the letter
n-i+1
can only appear in
h_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq\ell-i+1
.

5.3.
Combinatorial
R
matrices

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
, and let
\sigma\in S_{\ell}
be a permutation. It turns out that, for every
n
the crystal graphs
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\sigma(\mu)}
are isomorphic
[
50
]
(see also
[
3
,
86
]
). Such isomorphism is unique and it is constructed through the
combinatorial
R
-matrix
, the unique crystal isomorphism
\mathcal{R}\colon\mathcal{H}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{(\mu_{2},\mu_{1})}
.

We depict the combinatorial
R
-matrix
\mathcal{R}(a\otimes b)=b^{\prime}\otimes a^{\prime}
as the scattering diagram

(5.6)
