In
[
82
, Rule 3.11]
, the authors obtained an explicit algorithm for
\mathcal{R}
.
As such, we refer to this as the
Nakayashiki–Yamada rule
(for tensor products of single row Kirillov–Reshetikhin crystal), see also
[
47
, Sec. 2.2.3]
.
The following definition also includes the local energy that was introduced in
[
49
]
.

Definition 5.9

(The Nakayashiki–Yamada rule
[
82
, Rule 3.11]
)
.

Let
K\leq L
, and let
a\in\mathcal{H}^{K}
,
b\in\mathcal{H}^{L}
. The element
b^{\prime}\times a^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}(a\otimes b)
is constructed as follows.

(1)

Prepare two columns of
n
boxes. For each
i=1,\dots,n
, draw
\wt_{i}(a)
dots in the
i
-th box (from the top) of the right column and
\wt_{i}(b)
dots in the
i
-th box of the left column.

(2)

Pair each dot
x
in the right column (processed in arbitrary order) to the lowest unpaired dot
y
in the left column lying in a box strictly higher than that of
x
. If no such dot exists choose the lowest unpaired dot
y
. We refer to pairs
(x,y)
with
y
below
x
as
winding pairs
.

(3)

Move all unpaired dots from their box in the left column to the same box in the right column. The resulting configuration of dots determines
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
.

We also define the
local energy function
6
6

6

This is typically denoted by
H
in the literature, but we are using a different name to avoid confusion with HWT.

(5.7)

W(a\otimes b)=\#\text{ of winding pairs in }a\otimes b.

When
L<K
,
\mathcal{R}(a\otimes b)
is computed exchanging
a
(resp.
a^{\prime}
) with
b
(resp.
b^{\prime}
) in (1) (resp. (3)) in the above algorithm.

Remark 5.10
.

The output
b^{\prime}\otimes a^{\prime}
of the Nakayashiki–Yamada rule, as well as the winding number is independent of the processing order of dot-pairing in step (2); this was proven in
[
82
, Prop. 3.20]
.

Example 5.11
.

Applying
Definition

5.9
to
a=112
,
b=1223
, we get
a^{\prime}=123
,
b^{\prime}=1122
as follows:

In this example, the local energy function is
2
.
The corresponding scattering diagram is shown on the right, where the winding number has been written near the crossing.

The combinatorial
R
-matrix is an involution and satisfies the

Yang–Baxter equation
:

(5.8)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}\otimes\mathbf{1})(\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathcal{R})(\mathcal{R}\otimes\mathbf{1})=(\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathcal{R})(\mathcal{R}\otimes\mathbf{1})(\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathcal{R}).

Fix
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. For
i=1,\dots,\ell-1
, define the operator

(5.9)

\sigma_{i}=\mathbf{1}^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes\mathcal{R}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\otimes(\ell-i-1)},

which acts on a tensor product
h_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{\ell}
as
\mathcal{R}
on the
i
-th and
(i+1)
-th tensor factors and as the identity on the remaining factors.
This defines an action of the symmetric group
S_{\ell}
on
\bigsqcup_{\mu:\ell(\mu)=\ell}\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
.
Note that the braid relation for
\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i+1}
is the Yang–Baxter equation (
5.8
).
With a slight abuse of notation, we denote by
\sigma\in S_{\ell}
both a permutation and the corresponding operator obtained by composing the
\sigma_{i}
.
Hence, we have

(5.10)

\sigma\mathcal{H}^{\mu}=\sigma_{i_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{i_{k}}\mathcal{H}^{\mu}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}\mu}=\mathcal{H}^{\sigma\mu}

for any choice of reduced expression
\sigma=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}
(where
s_{i}=(i\;i+1)
denotes the
i
-th elementary transposition).
Since the combinatorial
R
-matrix is a crystal isomorphism, this action commutes with the Kashiwara operators.
In particular, for any
\sigma\in S_{\ell}
, the map (
5.10
) is the unique crystal isomorphism between
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\sigma(\mu)}
.

Remark 5.12
.

Denote by

\sigma^{-}:=(\ell,\ell-1,\dots,1)=(s_{1}\cdots s_{\ell-1})\cdots(s_{1}s_{2})(s_{1}),

which is referred to as the long element as it requires the most number of simple transpositions (see,
e,g.
,
[
91
]
).
For
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dotsc,\mu_{\ell})
and its reverse
\mu^{-}:=\sigma^{-}(\mu)=(\mu_{\ell},\dotsc,\mu_{2},\mu_{1})
, the crystal isomorphism
\sigma^{-}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\mu^{-}}
can be represented by the scattering diagram of
Figure

15
left panel, where the input is
H=h_{\ell}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{1}
and the output is
\sigma^{-}(H)=h^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes h^{\prime}_{\ell}
.

5.4.
The Lusztig involution

In the next definition we introduce two involutions
\eta^{\prime}
and
\eta
. In particular
\eta^{\prime}
is a combinatorial realization of (the crystal limit of) the
Lusztig involution

[
7
,
79
]
(see also
[
75
, Prop. 2.3]
).

Definition 5.13
.

Let
\sigma^{-}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
be the map defined in
remark

5.12
. Let
\mathrm{flip}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\mu^{-}}

be the map defined by

\mathrm{flip}\colon h_{\ell}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{2}\otimes h_{1}\mapsto h_{1}\otimes h_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{\ell}.

Let
\mathrm{comp}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
be the map defined by inverting all words
h_{i}
,
i=1,\dotsc,\ell
then changing all
j
-entries to
n+1-j
-entries. Then we define the maps
\eta^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\mu^{-}}
and
\eta\colon\mathcal{H}^{\mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
as

(5.11)

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}=\mathrm{comp}\circ\mathrm{flip}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\eta=\eta^{\prime}\circ\sigma^{-}.

The map
\eta^{\prime}
is called tne
Lusztig involution
.

Lemma 5.14

(
[
85
,
75
]
)
.

The map
\eta^{\prime}
is an involution such that for each
i\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}

(5.12)

\displaystyle e_{n-i}\circ\eta^{\prime}=\eta^{\prime}\circ f_{i},

where we set
e_{n}=e_{0}
.

Proof.

We provide a short proof for completeness.
Equation (
5.12
) follows from the signature rule in
definition

5.2
and definitions of
\mathrm{flip}
and
\mathrm{comp}
in
definition

5.13
.
∎

Lemma 5.15
.

The map
\eta
is an involution such that for each
i\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
we have

(5.13)

\displaystyle e_{n-i}\circ\eta=\eta\circ f_{i},

where we set
e_{n}=e_{0}
.

Proof.

Equation (
5.13
) follows from the relation
f_{i}\circ\sigma^{-}=\sigma^{-}\circ f_{i}
(since
\sigma^{-}
a crystal isomorphism) and
lemma

5.14
. Relation (
5.13
) also implies that
\eta^{2}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\mu}\to\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
is a crystal isomorphism and by uniqueness it must be the identity.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

For any
\mu\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
the involution
\eta
restricts to a bijection
\eta\colon\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{\mathrm{hw}}\longleftrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{\mathrm{lw}}
.

5.5.
Energy functions

In this subsection, we describe two notions of energy functions
D,D^{\prime}
for
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
.

Definition 5.17

(Energy functions)
.

Recall the winding number
W
from (
5.7
). For any

\mu\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
, define the
global energy

D(H)=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq\ell(\mu)}W_{i}\bigl(\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i+2}\cdots\sigma_{j-1}(H)\bigr),

where
W_{i}(h_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{\ell})=W(h_{i}\otimes h_{i+1})
. The
dual global energy
is

D^{\prime}(H)=D(\eta(H)),

where
\eta
is the map in (
5.11
).

The energy functions were introduced in
[
31
,
34
]
7
7

7

More precisely, only
D
was defined in
[
31
,
34
]
, while both
D
and
D^{\prime}
have subsequently appeared in the literature. See
[
73
, Rem. 4.10]
and
[
74
, Def. 2.4]
for precise statements.
. In what follows, rather than the algorithmic definition, we mainly use the structural properties of
D
recorded below. Alternative explicit descriptions of the energy
D
and
D^{\prime}
are given respectively in
Remark

5.36
and
Remark

5.43
.

Figure 15
.
In the left panel the scattering diagrams representing the isomorphism
\overline{\sigma}^{-}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\mu}\to\mathcal{H}^{\mu^{-}}
. In the right panel an explicit example: values in red near the crossings are the winding numbers of the corresponding application of the
R
-matrix.

First, the energy functions are constant on classically connected components (see
[
31
,
34
,
32
,
96
]
).

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
g_{i}
be
e_{i}
, or
f_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,2,\dotsc,n-1\}
. If
g_{i}(H)\neq\mathbf{0}
, then we have

(5.14)

\displaystyle D\bigl(g_{i}(H)\bigr)=D(H)\qquad\text{and}\qquad D^{\prime}\bigl(g_{i}(H)\bigr)=D^{\prime}(H),

i.e., all elements in a classical connected component have the same energy and dual energy.

The following key property of the energy function is well-known to experts in KR crystals, but has not explicitly appeared in the literature as far as the authors are aware.

8
8

8

We thank Masato Okado for the proof. This proof is type-independent and holds for arbitrary tensor products of KR crystals.

Lemma 5.19
.

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
.
The classically connected component of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
having the highest energy
D
is unique, whose highest weight element and energy are

(5.15)

\displaystyle H_{\max}:=\underbrace{1\cdots 1}_{\mu_{\ell}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\underbrace{1\cdots 1}_{\mu_{1}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad D(H_{\max})=\left\lVert\mu^{+}\right\rVert:=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell(\mu)}(i-1)\mu_{i}^{+},

respectively.
Here
\mu^{+}=(\mu^{+}_{1},\dotsc,\mu^{+}_{\ell})\in\mathbb{Y}_{\ell}
is obtained by sorting the elements in
\mu
in nonincreasing order.
Moreover,
D(H_{\max})=D^{\prime}(H_{\max})
.

Proof.

By
lemma

5.18
,
D
is constant on each classical connected component. Hence it suffices to consider classically highest weight elements.
By
[
56
,
57
]
, the
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n}
-KKR bijection (a generalization of the one presented in
Section

4.2
) identifies highest weight elements
H\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
with
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n}
rigged configurations
(\bm{\nu},\bm{J})=(\nu^{(a)},J^{(a)})_{a=1}^{n-1}
, where
\nu^{(a)}
are partitions such that
|\nu^{(a)}|=\wt_{a+1}(H)
and the riggings
J^{(a)}
are lists of non-negative integers.
Under this bijection, the quantity
c(H)=\left\lVert\mu^{+}\right\rVert-D(H)
, known as the
cocharge
of
b
, is given by the expression (see,
e.g.
,
[
47
, Eq. (4.43)]
)

c(H)=\sum_{a,b=1}^{n-1}\sum_{i,j\geq 1}C_{a,b}\,\min(i,j)\,m_{i}(\nu^{(a)})m_{j}(\nu^{(b)})\;+\;\sum_{a=1}^{n-1}\sum_{k\geq 1}J^{(a)}_{k},

where
C_{a,b}=2\delta_{a=b}-\delta_{|a-b|=1}
. The right hand side is a non-negative, non-degenerate quadratic form in the multiplicities
m_{i}(\nu^{(a)})
, plus a non-negative contribution from the riggings.
It is therefore uniquely minimized when
\bm{\nu}=\bm{J}=\varnothing
, corresponding to
H=H_{\max}
, where
c(H_{\max})=0
, yielding the claims.

To show that
D^{\prime}(H_{\max})=D(H_{\max})
, we compute

e_{1}^{\lvert\mu\rvert}\cdots e_{n}^{\lvert\mu\rvert}\eta(H_{\max})=e_{1}^{\lvert\mu\rvert}\cdots e_{n}^{\lvert\mu\rvert}(\underbrace{n\cdots n}_{\mu_{\ell}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\underbrace{n\cdots n}_{\mu_{1}})=H_{\max},

and hence the claim follows.
∎

Remark 5.20
.

We have that
D^{\prime}\neq D
in general.
Consider the case and
\mu=(1,1,1)
and
n=3
. Below we report the classically highest weight elements of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
, their energy
D
, the image of
\eta
, and
D^{\prime}
.

\begin{array}[]{c|cccc}H_{\mathrm{hw}}&1\otimes 1\otimes 1&2\otimes 1\otimes 1&1\otimes 2\otimes 1&3\otimes 2\otimes 1\\[1.99997pt]
D(H_{\mathrm{hw}})&3&2&1&0\\[1.99997pt]
\eta(H_{\mathrm{hw}})&3\otimes 3\otimes 3&3\otimes 3\otimes 2&3\otimes 2\otimes 3&3\otimes 2\otimes 1\\[1.99997pt]
D^{\prime}(H_{\mathrm{hw}})&3&1&2&0\end{array}

The following result was proven in
[
33
]
(where the opposite tensor product convention is used).

Proposition 5.21

(
[
33
, Prop. 1.1]
)
.

Let
H=h_{\ell}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{1}
be an element such that, for all
j\in\{2,3,\dotsc,\ell\}
, if
e_{0}(h_{j}\otimes h_{1})=e_{0}(h_{j})\otimes h_{1}
, then
e_{0}(\widetilde{h}_{1}\otimes\widetilde{h}_{j})=e_{0}(\widetilde{h}_{1})\otimes\widetilde{h}_{j}
, where
\mathcal{R}(h_{j}\otimes h_{1})=\widetilde{h}_{1}\otimes\widetilde{h}_{j}
.
Then
D(e_{0}(H))=D(H)-1
.

5.6.
Regular subgraphs and connectivity

In this subsection, we introduce a distinguished subgraph of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
obtained by selecting the
f_{0}
arrows that increase the energy by one unit, and study its structural properties.

Definition 5.22

(Regular subgraph)
.

Let
H\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
and assume that
f_{0}(H)\neq\mathbf{0}
. We say that
H\to f_{0}(H)
is a
regular 0-arrow
if

D\bigl(f_{0}(H)\bigr)=D(H)+1.

The
regular subgraph
of
\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
is the subgraph obtained by removing all 0-arrows that are not regular. More generally,
H\to g(H)
is a
regular arrow
if it is either a regular 0-arrow or if
g\in\{e_{1},f_{1},\dots,e_{n-1},f_{n-1}\}
.

Remark 5.23
.

The regular subgraph of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
is
graded
by the energy function
D
. In particular along any path in the regular subgraph of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}

H^{\prime}=g_{1}\cdots g_{k}(H),

we have

D(H^{\prime})=D(H)+\#\{j\mid g_{j}=f_{0}\}-\#\{j\mid g_{j}=e_{0}\}.

We now state the main result of this section.
For
\mu\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
, we define
\ell(\mu):=\lvert\{i=1,\dotsc,\ell\mid\mu_{i}>0\}\rvert
as the number of nonzero entries in
\mu
.

Theorem 5.24
.

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
.
If
n\geq\ell(\mu)
, the regular subgraph of the
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n}
-crystal
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
is connected.

Remark 5.25
.

While the crystal graph
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
is strongly connected, as pointed out in
Remark

5.6
, the same is not true in general for its regular subgraph, making the result of
Theorem

5.24
nontrivial. For instance, in
Figure

14
right panel, we see that the regular subgraph of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
with
\mu=(2,1,1)
and
n=2
is not connected.

Remark 5.26
.

A similar result to
Theorem

5.24
was proved in
[
92
, Thm. 6.1]
(see also
[
23
]
), where the connectedness was shown for the Demazure subgraph of tensor products of “single column” KR crystals (as opposed to single rows that we are considering in the present paper) using properties not present in our case.

Remark 5.27
.

We have observed in
Figure

14
right panel, that the regular subgraph of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
could be disconnected if
n<\ell(\mu)
. We do not expect this to be a general pathology and in fact conjecture that regular subgraph of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
can only be disconnected for
n=2
and
\ell(\mu)>2
. This should be related to
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
not being simply-laced (see
5.35
below).

To prove
Theorem

5.24
we construct an explicit path in the regular subgraph from the arbitrary element
H\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
to
H_{\max}
.
The main tools for its definition are given next.

Definition 5.28
.

Define the map
\overline{\mathcal{P}}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{\mathrm{hw}}\to\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
such that for
H_{\mathrm{hw}}\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{\mathrm{hw}}

\overline{\mathcal{P}}(H_{\mathrm{hw}})=\mathrm{pr}^{M}(H_{\mathrm{hw}})\qquad\text{ with }\qquad M=n-\max(H_{\mathrm{hw}}),

where
\max(H)
is the largest entry in
H
. For brevity we will denote

\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}=\overline{\mathcal{P}}(H_{\mathrm{hw}}).

In words
\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}
is the element obtained by adding
M
to each entry of
H_{\mathrm{hw}}
.

Next, we note that the elements
H_{\mathrm{hw}},\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}
belong to the same classically connected component.

Lemma 5.29
.

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
. For any
H_{\mathrm{hw}}\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{\mathrm{hw}}
, let
M=\max(H_{\mathrm{hw}})
. Then, we have

(5.20)

\displaystyle\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}=g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{M}(H_{\mathrm{hw}}),

where for each
j=1,\dotsc,M
, we set
g_{j}=f^{\wt_{j}(H_{\mathrm{hw}})}_{j+n-M-1}\cdots f_{j+1}^{\wt_{j}(H_{\mathrm{hw}})}f_{j}^{\wt_{j}(H_{\mathrm{hw}})}
.

Proof.

A direct inspection of the map
g_{M}=f^{\wt_{M}(H_{\mathrm{hw}})}_{n-1}\cdots f_{M}^{\wt_{M}(H_{\mathrm{hw}})}
shows that its action on
H_{\mathrm{hw}}
raises every
M
entry to an
n
entry.
Each application of the Kashiwara operators
f^{\wt_{M}(H_{\mathrm{hw}})}_{k}
, for
k=M,\dotsc,n-1
is non-vanishing since the element
f^{\wt_{M}(H_{\mathrm{hw}})}_{k-1}\cdots f^{\wt_{M}(H_{\mathrm{hw}})}_{M}(H_{\mathrm{hw}})
has exactly
\wt_{M}(H_{\mathrm{hw}})

k
entries and no
k+1
entry.
By downwards induction on
j=M,\ldots,1
, the map
g_{j}
turns every
j
entry in the element
g_{j+1}\dots g_{M}(H_{\mathrm{hw}})
into a
j+n-M
entry, proving (
5.20
).
∎

Example 5.30
.

For
\mu=(4,3,3,2,1)
and
n=7
, we consider
H_{\mathrm{hw}}=2\otimes 11\otimes 123\otimes 122\otimes 1111
, and so
\max(H_{\mathrm{hw}})=3
.
Adding
4=7-3
to each entry we get

\overline{b_{\mathrm{hw}}}=6\otimes 55\otimes 567\otimes 566\otimes 5555=(f_{4}^{8}f_{3}^{8}f_{2}^{8}f_{1}^{8})(f_{5}^{4}f_{4}^{4}f_{3}^{4}f_{2}^{4})(f_{6}f_{5}f_{4}f_{3})b_{\mathrm{hw}}.

In addition, we can show that
f_{0}(\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}})\neq\mathbf{0}
, when
n\geq\ell(\mu)
.

Lemma 5.31
.

Fix
\mu\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
and suppose
2\leq\ell\leq n
.
For any
H_{\mathrm{hw}}\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{\mathrm{hw}}
, we write
\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}=\bar{h}_{\ell}\otimes\bar{h}_{\ell-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\bar{h}_{1}
.
Then, calling
k
is the smallest index such that
\overline{h}_{k}
contains an
n
, we have

(5.21)

\displaystyle f_{0}(\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}})=\bar{h}_{\ell}\otimes\cdots\otimes\bar{h}_{k+1}\otimes f_{0}(\bar{h}_{k})\otimes\bar{h}_{k-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\bar{h}_{1}.

Proof.

From
Definition

5.28
, it is clear that there exists at least one
n
entry in
\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}
.
Then, by the signature rule defining
f_{0}
(
Definition

5.2
), the claim reduces to showing that no
1
entry appears in any
\bar{h}_{j}
for
j>k
(see also
Remark

5.8
).
Let
H_{\mathrm{hw}}=h_{\ell}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{1}
.
Then, the highest weight condition and a straightforward induction over
j
imply that
\max(h_{j})\leq j
. Therefore, if
\ell<n
, all entries in
\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}
are strictly greater than
1
and the claim follows.
For the case
\ell=n
, if there is an
n
entry it must occur in
h_{n}
and in such case we have
\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}=H_{\mathrm{hw}}
,
k=n
and the claim follows.
∎

Example 5.32
.

We act
f_{0}
on
\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}=6\otimes 55\otimes 567\otimes 566\otimes 5555
in
example

5.30
, which applying the algorithm, we easily find
f_{0}(\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}})=6\otimes 55\otimes 156\otimes 566\otimes 5555
.

Example 5.33
.

Note that the conclusion of Lemma
5.31
does not hold for all elements
\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}
in Figure
14
, where the condition
\ell(\mu)\leq n
fails.
Indeed, consider the element
H_{\mathrm{hw}}=\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}=1\otimes 2\otimes 11
as
n=\max(H_{\mathrm{hw}})=2
, but we have
f_{0}(\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}})=\mathbf{0}
.

The next proposition states that
\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}\to f_{0}(\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}})
is a regular 0-arrow whenever
\mu
is a partition,
\ell(\mu)\leq n
and
H_{\mathrm{hw}}\neq H_{\max}
.

Proposition 5.34
.

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
such that
2\leq\ell(\mu)\leq n
.
Let
H_{\mathrm{hw}}\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{\mathrm{hw}}\setminus\{H_{\max}\}
, and recall the element
\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}
in
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
of
definition

5.28
.
Then, we have

(5.22)

\displaystyle D\bigl(f_{0}(\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}})\bigr)=D(\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}})+1.

Proof.

We first consider the case
\max(H_{\mathrm{hw}})<n
, where we have
\ell=\ell(\mu)<n
. By
Lemma

5.31
,
H=f_{0}(\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}})\neq\mathbf{0}
is an element with a single
1
entry and all other entries strictly greater than
1
. Suppose such
1
entry occurs in the
k
-th tensor factor of
H=h_{\ell}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{1}
and thus there are no
n
’s appearing in any tensor factors
h_{j}
for all
j<k
. Since
H_{\mathrm{hw}}\neq H_{\max}
, we have
k>1
. We now check that
H
satisfies the hypotheses of
Proposition

5.21
.

For
j\neq 1
, let
\widetilde{h}_{1}\otimes\widetilde{h}_{j}=\mathcal{R}(h_{j}\otimes h_{1})
. For all
j\neq k
, we have
e_{0}(h_{j}\otimes h_{1})=e_{0}(\widetilde{h}_{1}\otimes\widetilde{h}_{j})=\mathbf{0}
, since there are no
1
entries in either factors
h_{1},h_{j}
(recall the combinatorial
R
-matrix is weight preserving).
We also have
e_{0}(h_{k}\otimes h_{1})=e_{0}(h_{k})\otimes h_{1}
, since
h_{1}
does not contain any
n
entry. The Nakayashiki–Yamada rule (
Definition

5.9
) implies that in the tensor product
\widetilde{h}_{1}\otimes\widetilde{h}_{k}
, the only 1 entry belongs to
\widetilde{h}_{1}
, while
\widetilde{h}_{k}
posseses no
n
entries. As a result
e_{0}(\widetilde{h}_{1}\otimes\widetilde{h}_{k})=e_{0}(\widetilde{h}_{1})\otimes\widetilde{h}_{k}
and
Proposition

5.21
implies that

D\bigl(e_{0}(H)\bigr)=D(H)-1,

which is equivalent to (
5.22
).

Now we assume
n=\max(H_{\mathrm{hw}})
, which also forces
\ell(\mu)=n
and
\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}=H_{\mathrm{hw}}
. Denote
H_{\mathrm{hw}}=h_{n}^{\prime}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{1}^{\prime}
and
H=f_{0}(H_{\mathrm{hw}})=h_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{1}
. Then
h_{n}=f_{0}(h_{n}^{\prime})
, since
h_{n}^{\prime}
is the only tensor factor over which
f_{0}
has non-vanishing action, and
h_{j}=h_{j}^{\prime}
for all
j\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
. Notice also that
h_{1}
consists of a row of length
\mu_{1}
with only 1 entries.

For any
j\neq 1
denote again
\widetilde{h}_{1}\otimes\widetilde{h}_{j}=\mathcal{R}(h_{j}\otimes h_{1})
.
A simple application of the signature rule (
Definition

5.2
) gives

e_{0}(h_{j}\otimes h_{1})=\begin{cases}e_{0}(h_{j})\otimes h_{1}\qquad&\text{if $h_{j}$ contains at least a 1 entry},\\
h_{j}\otimes e_{0}(h_{1})\qquad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

To apply
Proposition

5.21
we are only interested in the first case. If
h_{j}
contains at least a 1 entry, then the Nakayashiki–Yamada rule implies that
\widetilde{h}_{1}
consists of a single row having all the entries of
h_{j}
and additional
\mu_{1}-\mu_{j}
1 entries, while
\widetilde{h}_{j}
is a single row of length
\mu_{j}
with only 1’s. Then, the signature rule implies that

e_{0}(\widetilde{h}_{1}\otimes\widetilde{h}_{j})=e_{0}(\widetilde{h}_{1})\otimes\widetilde{h}_{j},

if
h_{j}
contains at least a 1-entry. By
Proposition

5.21
, also in this case we have
D(e_{0}(H))=D(H)-1
, which implies (
5.22
) and completes the proof.
∎

The proof of
Proposition

5.34
relies on
Proposition

5.21
, a result from
[
33
]
. A direct, alternative proof is given in
appendix

A
.

Conjecture 5.35
.

The assumption
\ell(\mu)\leq n
in
proposition

5.34
can be reduced to simply
n>2
or
\ell(\mu)\leq 2
for some other element
\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}
when
n=\max(H_{\mathrm{hw}})
in the same classical component as
H_{\mathrm{hw}}
.

We also note the necessity of changing the element
\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}
in
5.35
.
Consider the element
H_{\mathrm{hw}}=1\otimes n\otimes\cdots\otimes 2\otimes 1
, which has
\overline{H}_{\mathrm{hw}}=H_{\mathrm{hw}}
(as defined in
Lemma

5.29
).
Then by the bracketing rule, we have
f_{0}(\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}})=\mathbf{0}
.
Analogous elements exist for any such
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
for
\ell(\mu)>n
.

The result of
Proposition

5.34
yields an explicit regular 0-arrow in every classically connected component of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
whose energy is not maximal.

We are now ready to prove
Theorem

5.24
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.24
.

We show that any element
H\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
is connected in the regular subgraph of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
to
H_{\max}
. Let
H\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
and let
H_{\mathrm{hw}}
be the classically highest weight element in its classically connected component. If
H_{\mathrm{hw}}=H_{\max}
, we simply join
H
to
H_{\max}
applying to
H
sufficiently many
e_{i}
operators with
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
. Otherwise, by
Lemma

5.29
,
H
is connected to
\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}
via classical arrows and by
Proposition

5.34
, the arrow
\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}\to f_{0}(\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}})
is a regular
0
-arrow and increases the energy
D
by
1
. Iterating this procedure, we construct a path in the regular subgraph along which the energy strictly increases at each step where we move from one classically connected component to another. Since the energy is bounded above and the maximal element is unique, this process terminates at the unique classically connected component with
H_{\max}
.
∎

Remark 5.36
.

The explicit path constructed in the proof of
Theorem

5.24
can be used as a definition of the energy function
D
of a generic element
H\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
as long as
n\geq\ell(\mu)
. Let
g_{1}\cdots g_{K}
be the sequence of Kashiwara operators required to connect
H
to
H_{\max}
following the construction in the proof of
Theorem

5.24
. Then, we have
D(H)=\left\lVert\mu\right\rVert-\#\{j\mid g_{j}=f_{0}\}
.

5.7.
Dual regular subgraphs and leading paths

Adapting the strategy of
Section

5.6
, we introduce a distinguished subgraph of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
obtained by selecting the
e_{0}
arrows that increase the dual energy by one unit, and study its structural properties.

Definition 5.37

(Dual regular subgraph)
.

Let
H\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
and assume that
e_{0}(H)\neq\mathbf{0}
. We say that
H\to e_{0}(H)
is a
dual regular 0-arrow
if

D^{\prime}\bigl(e_{0}(H)\bigr)=D^{\prime}(H)+1.

The
dual regular subgraph
of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
is the subgraph obtained by removing all 0-arrows that are not dual regular. More generally,
H\to g(H)
is a
dual regular arrow
if it is either a regular 0-arrow or if
g\in\{e_{1},f_{1},\dots,e_{n-1},f_{n-1}\}
.

Remark 5.38
.

The dual regular subgraph of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
is
graded
by the dual energy function
D^{\prime}
. In particular along any path in the dual regular subgraph of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}

H^{\prime}=g_{1}\cdots g_{k}(H),

we have

D^{\prime}(H^{\prime})=D^{\prime}(H)+\#\{j\mid g_{j}=e_{0}\}-\#\{j\mid g_{j}=f_{0}\}.

The following lemma relates regular arrows and dual regular arrows.

Lemma 5.39
.

Let
H\to H^{\prime}
be a regular arrow. Then
\eta(H)\to\eta(H^{\prime})
is a dual regular arrow.

Proof.

Let
H^{\prime}=g(H)
for a Kashiwara operator
g\in\{e_{0},f_{0},\dotsc,e_{n-1},f_{n-1}\}
.
Then, by
Lemma

5.15
we have
\eta(H^{\prime})=g^{*}(\eta(H))
for some Kashiwara operator
g^{*}
.
If
g\notin\{e_{0},f_{0}\}
, then also
g^{*}\notin\{e_{0},f_{0}\}
and
\eta(H)\to\eta(H^{\prime})
is a dual regular arrow. If
g=f_{0}
, then
g^{*}=e_{0}
. By regular 0-arrow condition we have
D(H^{\prime})=D(H)+1
, which implies

D^{\prime}\bigl(e_{0}\eta(H)\bigr)=D^{\prime}\bigl(\eta(H^{\prime})\bigr)=D(H^{\prime})=D(H)+1=D^{\prime}\bigl(\eta(H)\bigr)+1.

In the second equality we used the definition of dual energy
D^{\prime}
and the fact that
\eta
is an involution. Hence
\eta(H)\to\eta(H^{\prime})
is a dual regular 0-arrow. The case
g=e_{0}
and
g^{*}=f_{0}
is treated analogously.
∎

As a consequence of
Theorem

5.24
and
Lemma

5.39
, we give the following result.

Theorem 5.40
.

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
such that
\ell(\mu)\leq n
. Then, the dual regular subgraph of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
is connected.

Proof.

Fix
H,H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
and call
\widetilde{H}=\eta(H)
,
\widetilde{H}^{\prime}=\eta(H^{\prime})
. By
Theorem

5.24
, there exists a path in the regular subgraph of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
connecting
\widetilde{H}
to
\widetilde{H}^{\prime}
. By
Lemma

5.39
the image of such path under the involution
\eta
consists of dual regular arrows and it joins
\eta(\widetilde{H})=H
and
\eta(\widetilde{H}^{\prime})=H^{\prime}
. Since
H,H^{\prime}
are generic, the dual regular subgraph of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
is connected.
∎

Based on
Theorem

5.40
, we now construct an explicit path on the dual regular graph joining any element
H\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
to
H_{\max}
. For this we define, similarly to
Definition

5.28
the map
\underline{\mathcal{P}}:\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{\mathrm{lw}}\to\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
such that for any
H_{\mathrm{lw}}\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{\mathrm{lw}}

(5.23)

\underline{\mathcal{P}}(H_{\mathrm{lw}})=\mathrm{pr}^{-(m-1)}(H_{\mathrm{lw}})\qquad\text{with}\qquad m=\min(H_{\mathrm{lw}}),

where
\min(H)
is the value of the minimal entry in
H
.

Lemma 5.41
.

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
.
Given
H_{\mathrm{lw}}\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{\mathrm{lw}}
, denote

\underline{H_{\mathrm{lw}}}=\underline{\mathcal{P}}(H_{\mathrm{lw}}),

and let
H_{\mathrm{hw}}=\eta(H_{\mathrm{lw}})\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{\mathrm{hw}}
.
Then we have

(5.24)

\underline{H_{\mathrm{lw}}}=\eta(\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}}).

Moreover, if
H_{\mathrm{hw}}\neq H_{\max}
, then
\underline{H_{\mathrm{lw}}}\to e_{0}(\underline{H_{\mathrm{lw}}})
is a dual regular arrow.

Proof.

First recall that
H_{\mathrm{hw}}
is a highest weight element by
Remark

5.16
. The identity (
5.24
) is a consequence of
Lemma

5.29
and
Lemma

5.15
, from which we have, setting
M=\max(H_{\mathrm{hw}})
,

\eta(\overline{H_{\mathrm{hw}}})=\eta\bigl(g_{1}\cdots g_{M}(H_{\mathrm{hw}})\bigr)=g_{1}^{*}\cdots g_{M}^{*}(H_{\mathrm{lw}})=\underline{H_{\mathrm{lw}}}.

Here operators
g_{i}
are combinations of Kashiwara operators which raise the value of every
i
-entry in
H_{\mathrm{hw}}
by
n-M
. Similarly, by
Lemma

5.15
, operators
g_{i}^{*}
lower the value of the
n-i+1
entry in
H_{\mathrm{lw}}
by
m-1
, where
m=n-M+1
.

The fact that
\underline{H_{\mathrm{lw}}}\to e_{0}(\underline{H_{\mathrm{lw}}})
is a dual regular arrow follows from
Proposition

5.34
and
Lemma

5.39
.
∎

In the definition below we denote by
\langle g_{1},\dots,g_{k}\rangle
the free group generated by operators
g_{1},\dots,g_{k}
.

Definition 5.42
.

Consider the
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n}
-crystal
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
with
\mu\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
.
Fix
H\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
, and assume that
n\geq\ell(\mu)
.
The
leading path

\mathcal{L}_{H}
from
H
to
H_{\max}
is a composition of Kashiwara operators constructed recursively as follows.

(1)

If
D^{\prime}(H)=D^{\prime}(H_{\max})
, then
H
and
H_{\max}
lie in the same classical connected component by
Lemma

5.19
. In this case we choose a composition of classical raising operators

\mathcal{L}_{H}\in\langle e_{1},\dots,e_{n-1}\rangle,\qquad\text{such that}\qquad\mathcal{L}_{H}(H)=H_{\max}.

(2)

If
D^{\prime}(H)\neq D^{\prime}(H_{\max})
, let
\mathcal{G}_{H}\in\langle e_{1},f_{1},\dots,e_{n-1},f_{n-1}\rangle
be a composition of classical Kashiwara operators such that
\mathcal{G}_{H}(H)=\underline{H_{\mathrm{lw}}},
where
H_{\mathrm{lw}}
is the lowest weight element in the classical connected component of
H
. Set
H^{\prime}=e_{0}(\underline{H_{\mathrm{lw}}})
. By
Lemma

5.41
, the arrow
\underline{H_{\mathrm{lw}}}\longrightarrow H^{\prime}
is dual regular and
D^{\prime}(H^{\prime})=D^{\prime}(H)+1
.
We then define

\mathcal{L}_{H}=\mathcal{L}_{H^{\prime}}\circ e_{0}\circ\mathcal{G}_{H}.

Remark 5.43
.

The leading path gives an alternative definition of the dual energy
D^{\prime}
. Assuming the leading path of the element
H
is written as
\mathcal{L}_{H}=g_{1}\cdots g_{K}
for
g_{i}
Kashwara operators, then we have
D^{\prime}(H)=\left\lVert\mu\right\rVert-\#\{j\mid g_{j}=e_{0}\}
.

Figure 16
.
An example of a leading path.

5.8.
(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n}\oplus\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n})
-crystal on pairs of HWTs

We now equip the set of pairs
(H,H^{\prime})
of horizontally weak tableaux of the same shape with two commuting
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n}
-crystal structures acting independently on each factor.
(Recall that we have identified
\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
with
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
.) This makes
\bigsqcup_{\mu}\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)^{2}
into an
(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n}\oplus\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n})
-crystal.

Definition 5.44
.

For
k\in\{1,2\}
and
i\in\{0,\dotsc,n-1\}
, define operators

E^{(k)}_{i},\,F^{(k)}_{i}\colon\bigsqcup_{\mu}\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)^{2}\;\longrightarrow\;\bigsqcup_{\mu}\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)^{2}\;\sqcup\;\{\mathbf{0}\},

which act componentwise for all
i

(5.25)

\displaystyle E^{(1)}_{i}=e_{i}\times\mathbf{1},\qquad\qquad E^{(2)}_{i}=\mathbf{1}\times e_{i},\qquad\qquad F^{(1)}_{i}=f_{i}\times\mathbf{1},\qquad\qquad F^{(2)}_{i}=\mathbf{1}\times f_{i}.

Definition 5.45

(Leading path for pairs of HWTs)
.

Given a partition
\mu
and a pair of elements
H,H^{\prime}\in\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
, a
leading path for the pair
(H,H^{\prime})
is a composition of Kashiwara operators

\mathcal{L}_{H,H^{\prime}}=G^{(2)}_{k^{\prime}}\cdots G^{(2)}_{1}G^{(1)}_{k}\cdots G^{(1)}_{1},

where
G^{(1)}_{j}=g_{j}\times\mathbf{1}
,
G^{(2)}_{j}=\mathbf{1}\times g_{j}^{\prime}
with
g_{j},g_{j}^{\prime}\in\{e_{i},f_{i},0\leq i\leq n-1\}
are such that
\mathcal{L}_{H}=g_{k}\cdots g_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{H^{\prime}}=g_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime}\cdots g_{1}^{\prime}
are leading paths for
H
and
H^{\prime}
respectively.

If the partition
\mu
is such that
\ell(\mu)\leq n
, then for any pair
H,H^{\prime}\in\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
a leading path
\mathcal{L}_{H,H^{\prime}}
exists and we have

\mathcal{L}_{H,H^{\prime}}(H,H^{\prime})=(H_{\max},H_{\max}).

6.
Commuting affine crystal structures on pairs of Young tableaux

6.1.
Commuting affine crystal structures

In this subsection, we equip the set
\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}
with two commuting affine crystal structures.
However, unlike for pairs of HWT, the crystal operators do not entirely act on each component independently.
Let us first extend the action of classical Kashiwara operators on semistandard tableaux; for more details see
[
10
,
52
]
.

Definition 6.1
.

For any
P\in\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)
and
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, we define
e_{i}(P)
and
f_{i}(P)
through the signature rule of
Definition

5.2
(1)-(4e) and (4f). Recall that the row reading word of a semistandard tableaux was defined in
Definition

2.8
.

We now define two families of Kashiwara operators on pairs of tableaux. In
Theorem

6.8
, we will prove that they commute with each other.

Definition 6.2
.

For
k\in\{1,2\}
and
i\in\{0,\dotsc,n-1\}
, define operators

E^{(k)}_{i},\,F^{(k)}_{i}\colon\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}\;\longrightarrow\;\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}\;\sqcup\;\{\mathbf{0}\}.

For
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
they act componentwise

(6.1)

\displaystyle E^{(1)}_{i}=e_{i}\times\mathbf{1},\qquad\qquad E^{(2)}_{i}=\mathbf{1}\times e_{i},\qquad\qquad F^{(1)}_{i}=f_{i}\times\mathbf{1},\qquad\qquad F^{(2)}_{i}=\mathbf{1}\times f_{i},

while the 0-operators are defined by conjugation as

(6.2)

\displaystyle E_{0}^{(k)}=\iota_{k}\circ E^{(k)}_{1}\circ\iota_{k}^{-1},\qquad\qquad F_{0}^{(k)}=\iota_{k}\circ F^{(k)}_{1}\circ\iota_{k}^{-1},

where
\iota_{k}
(
k=1,2
) was given in
definition

3.3
.

Example 6.3
.

Consider the pair
(P,Q)
of
Example

3.16
. Below we compute
F^{(1)}_{0}\circ E^{(1)}_{1}(P,Q)

(6.3)

E^{(1)}_{1}(P,Q).\end{split}

In the right hand side of the first line we highlighted in red the entry of
P
that was modified by the action of
E^{(1)}_{1}
Recall that the operator
\iota_{1}
can also be evaluated through column internal insertion as described in
Proposition

3.9
.

Example

6.3
shows that, while the action of operators
E_{i}^{(k)},F_{i}^{(k)}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
and
k\in\{1,2\}
does not modify the shape of tableaux
(P,Q)
, the same is not true for 0 operators
E_{0}^{(k)},F_{0}^{(k)}
. For instance, the action of
F_{0}^{(1)}
in (
6.3
) shifted by one box to the left the third row of both tableaux.

In the next example we observe the action of a 0-operator on stable pairs of tableaux.

Example 6.4
.

The pair
(P,Q)
below is stable and we apply
F^{(1)}_{0}
to it

8.19447pt\mathrm{pr}^{-1}\hskip 8.19447pt}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 2$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}=f_{0}(H_{1}).

Compare now the change of
H_{1}
in (
6.4
) with that of the
P
-tableau in (
6.4
). We see that they are completely equivalent as long as we focus only on the labeled cells in
P
. That is,
\iota_{1}^{-1}
act on the positive entries in
P
in the same way as
pr
on
H_{1}
. Moreover we notice that the row that gets shifted to the left by the action of
F^{(1)}_{0}
is precisely the row that gets modified by the action of
F_{1}^{(1)}
.

The above example suggests a precise correspondence between the operator
\iota_{1}
and the inverse promotion, as well as a commutativity between the projection
\Phi
and the action of Kashiwara operators
E^{(k)}_{i},F^{(k)}_{i}
for
k\in\{1,2\}
and
i\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Fix
k\in\{1,2\}
and let
(P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}
be such that
\iota_{k}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})
is stable in the sense of
corollary

3.13
. Let
(H_{1},H_{2})=\Phi(P_{1},P_{2})
and let
\mu
be the shape of
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
.

(I)

Call
(P_{1}^{*},P_{2}^{*})=\iota_{k}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})
and
H_{k}^{*}=\mathrm{pr}(H_{k})
. Then, entries of
P_{k}^{*}
are row-by-row equal to those of
H_{k}^{*}
. Moreover, calling
\lambda^{*}/\rho^{*}
the skew shape of
(P_{1}^{*},P_{2}^{*})
, we have

(6.24)

\lambda_{i}^{*}=\lambda_{i}-\#\{n\text{-entries at row $i$ of }P_{k}\},\qquad\rho_{i}^{*}=\rho_{i}-\#\{n\text{-entries at row $i$ of }P_{k}\}.

(II)

Suppose that
e_{0}(H_{k})\neq\mathbf{0}
and that the action of
e_{0}
changes the
r
-th row of
H_{k}
. Then,
(\widetilde{P}_{1},\widetilde{P}_{2})=E_{0}^{(k)}(P_{1},P_{2})\neq\mathbf{0}
and the line up of the positive entries in
\widetilde{P}_{k}
coincides with that of
e_{0}(H_{k})
. Moreover, calling
\widetilde{\lambda}/\widetilde{\rho}
the skew shape of
(\widetilde{P}_{1},\widetilde{P}_{2})
, we have

\tilde{\lambda}_{i}=\lambda_{i}+\mathbf{1}_{i=r},\qquad\qquad\tilde{\rho}_{i}=\rho_{i}+\mathbf{1}_{i=r}.

(III)

Similarly, if the action of
f_{0}(H_{k})\neq\mathbf{0}
and that the action of
f_{0}
changes the
r
-th row of
H_{k}
, we have
(\widehat{P}_{1},\widehat{P}_{2})=F_{0}^{(k)}(P_{1},P_{2})\neq\mathbf{0}
. Moreover entries of
\widehat{P}_{k}
are row-by-row equal to those of
f_{0}(H_{k})
and

\widehat{\lambda}_{i}=\lambda_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{i=r},\qquad\qquad\widehat{\rho}_{i}=\rho_{i}-\mathbf{1}_{i=r},

where
\widehat{\lambda}/\widehat{\rho}
is the skew shape of
(\widehat{P}_{1},\widehat{P}_{2})
.

Proof.

Call
k^{\prime}=3-k
, so that
k^{\prime}=2
if
k=1
and
k^{\prime}=1
if
k=2
.

Let us start by proving
(I)
. We use
Proposition

3.9
to evaluate the action of
\iota_{k}
on
(P_{1}^{*},P_{2}^{*})
. Since
(P_{1}^{*},P_{2}^{*})
is stable, the row content of
P^{*}_{k^{\prime}}
is equal to that of
P_{k^{\prime}}
and the two tableaux differ by a shift in the external and internal shapes. On the other hand, the
i
-th row of
P_{k}
is obtained from the
i
-th row of
P_{k}^{*}
as follows: first vacate all its cells with entry
1
, then subtract 1 from all remaining entries and finally append as many
n
-cells as
1
-cells were vacated. This proves the relation (
6.24
). Moreover, comparing the procedure transforming
P_{k}^{*}
into
P_{k}
with the promotion
\mathrm{pr}
, we see that
H_{k}=\mathrm{pr}^{-1}(H_{k}^{*})
, since the stability of
(P_{1}^{*},P_{2}^{*})
implies that the rows of
P_{k}
and
P_{k}^{*}
correspond to those of
H_{k}
and
H_{k}^{*}
.

The statements
(II)
,
(III)
are basic consequences of
(I)
and of the expressions
e_{0}=\mathrm{pr}\circ e_{1}\circ\mathrm{pr}^{-1}
and
f_{0}=\mathrm{pr}\circ f_{1}\circ\mathrm{pr}^{-1}
, given in
Remark

5.5
.
∎

In the following lemma we state that the column internal insertion in a tableau
P
commutes with the action of classical Kashiwara operators.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
P\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)
and
\mathcal{C}_{[c]}
be the column internal insertion introduced in
definition

3.5
.
For any corner cell
c
of
P
and any choice of
g\in\{e_{1},f_{1},\dots,e_{n-1},f_{n-1}\}
we have

(6.25)

\displaystyle g\circ\mathcal{C}_{[c]}(P)=\mathcal{C}_{[c]}\circ g(P).

Proof.

It is well known that the action of the classical Kashiwara operators commutes with another combinatorial operation called
jeu de taquin slides (jdt)
; see e.g.
[
10
, Thm. 8.7]
. We will not introduce the jdt here, and we refer the interested reader to
[
78
,
91
]
and references therein. The internal column insertion
\mathcal{C}_{[c]}
on the tableau
P
can be realized by a finite sequence of jdt slides; see
[
97
, App. A1.2]
and
[
44
, Prop. A.4, Thm. A.2, Rem A.3]
. This shows that the commutation relation (
6.25
) holds.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
(P,Q)\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}
and let
(k,k^{\prime})\in\{(1,2),(2,1)\}
. Then, for any
G^{(k)}\in\{E_{i}^{(k^{\prime})},F_{i}^{(k^{\prime})}\mid 0\leq i\leq n-1\}
, we have

(6.26)

\displaystyle G^{(k)}\circ\iota_{k^{\prime}}(P,Q)=\iota_{k^{\prime}}\circ G^{(k)}(P,Q).

Proof.

Let us prove only the case
k=1,k^{\prime}=2
, as the complementary case
k=2,k^{\prime}=1
can be shown in similar fashion. Let us first consider the case where
G
is not a 0-Kashiwara operator
G^{(1)}=g\times\mathbf{1}
for
g\in\{e_{i},f_{i}:1\leq i\leq n-1\}
. Denote by
(\overline{P},\overline{Q})=\iota_{2}(P,Q)
. By
Proposition

3.9
, we have

\overline{P}=\mathcal{C}_{[c_{1}]}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{C}_{[c_{s}]}(P),

where the columns
c_{1},\dots,c_{s}
are determined by the cells with entry 1 in
Q
. Then, by
Lemma

6.6
and by the description of
\overline{Q}
provided by
Proposition

3.9
, we have

(6.27)

G^{(1)}\circ\iota_{2}(P,Q)=(g\circ\mathcal{C}_{[r_{1}]}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{C}_{[r_{s}]}(P),\overline{Q})=(\mathcal{C}_{[r_{1}]}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{C}_{[r_{s}]}\circ g(P),\overline{Q})=\iota_{2}\circ G^{(1)}(P,Q).

This proves the relation (
6.26
) for
k=1,k^{\prime}=2
, whenever
G^{(1)}
is not a 0-Kashiwara operator. Now, let
G^{(1)}\in\{E_{0}^{(1)},F_{0}^{(1)}\}
and by the definition (
6.2
) we can write
G^{(1)}=\iota_{1}\circ\widetilde{G}^{(1)}\circ\iota_{1}^{-1}
for
\widetilde{G}^{(1)}\in\{E_{1}^{(1)},F_{1}^{(1)}\}
. Then, we have

\begin{split}G^{(1)}\circ\iota_{2}&=\iota_{1}\circ\widetilde{G}^{(1)}\circ\iota_{1}^{-1}\circ\iota_{2}=\iota_{1}\circ\widetilde{G}^{(1)}\circ\iota_{2}\circ\iota_{1}^{-1}\\
&=\iota_{1}\circ\iota_{2}\circ\widetilde{G}^{(1)}\circ\iota_{1}^{-1}=\iota_{2}\circ\iota_{1}\circ\widetilde{G}^{(1)}\circ\iota_{1}^{-1}=\iota_{2}\circ G^{(1)}.\end{split}

Above, in the second and in the fourth equality we used the commutation relation
Proposition

3.4
,
(VI)
, while in the third equality we used (
6.27
).
This completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 6.8
.

The families of Kashiwara operators
\{E_{i}^{(1)},F_{i}^{(1)}\mid 0\leq i\leq n-1\}
and
\{E_{i}^{(2)},F_{i}^{(2)}\mid 0\leq i\leq n-1\}
commute with each other.
That is, they equip
\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}
with a
(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n}\oplus\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n})
-crystal structure.

Proof.

It is straightforward from the definition (
6.1
) that for all

G^{(k)}\in\{E_{i}^{(k)},F_{i}^{(k)}\mid 1\leq i\leq n-1\},\qquad\text{where }k\in\{1,2\},

and all pairs of tableaux
(P,Q)\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}
we have

G^{(1)}\circ G^{(2)}(P,Q)=G^{(2)}\circ G^{(1)}(P,Q).

This is because non-
0
operators act component wise.
Therefore we only need to show commutation relations involving the Kashiwara
0
operators.
Let
G_{0}^{(1)}=\iota_{1}\circ G_{1}^{(1)}\circ\iota_{1}^{-1}
for
G_{1}^{(1)}\in\{E^{(1)}_{1},F^{(1)}_{1}\}
.
Consider the non-
0
operator
G_{i}^{(2)}\in\{E_{i}^{(1)},F_{i}^{(1)}\mid 1\leq i\leq n-1\}
.
Then, using
Lemma

6.7
, we have

\begin{split}G^{(1)}_{0}\circ G_{i}^{(2)}&=\iota_{1}\circ G_{1}^{(1)}\circ\iota_{1}^{-1}\circ G_{i}^{(2)}=\iota_{1}\circ G_{1}^{(1)}\circ G_{i}^{(2)}\circ\iota_{1}^{-1}\\
&=\iota_{1}\circ G_{i}^{(2)}\circ G_{1}^{(1)}\circ\iota_{1}^{-1}=G_{i}^{(2)}\circ\iota_{1}\circ G_{1}^{(1)}\circ\iota_{1}^{-1}=G_{i}^{(2)}\circ G_{0}^{(1)}.\end{split}

The remaining cases of when
G^{(2)}
is the Kashiwara
0
operator (with possibly
G^{(1)}
being a Kashiwara
0
operator, where we need to use the above case) is analogous.
∎

Theorem 6.9
.

The skew column RSK map is an isomorphism of
(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n}\oplus\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n})
-crystals; that is, for all
G\in\{E_{i}^{(k)},F_{i}^{(k)}\mid 0\leq i\leq n-1,1\leq k\leq 2\}

we have

(6.28)

\displaystyle\mathbf{cRSK}\circ G=G\circ\mathbf{cRSK}.

We call the commutation relations (
6.28
) the
crystal symmetry of the column skew RSK dynamics
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

3.4
,
(V)
we have
\mathbf{cRSK}=\iota_{1}^{n}=\iota_{2}^{n}
. For
k\in\{1,2\}
, let
G\in\{E_{i}^{(k)},F_{i}^{(k)}\mid 0\leq i\leq n-1\}
. Then by
Lemma

6.7
,
G
commutes with
\iota_{k^{\prime}}
, for
k^{\prime}=3-k
and hence with
\mathbf{cRSK}
.
∎

6.2.
Leading paths for pairs of skew tableaux

The result of
Theorem

6.9
allows us to relate the
(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n}\oplus\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n})
-crystal structures on the sets of pairs of skew tableaux
\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}
and on the set
\bigsqcup_{\mu}\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)^{2}
, the latter defined in
Section

5.8
. For the next lemma recall the projections
\Phi,\Phi^{-}
introduced in
Definition

1.6
and
Definition

3.15
.

Lemma 6.10
.

For any
k\in\{1,2\}
and
i\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
, we have

(6.29)

E^{(k)}_{i}\circ\Phi=\Phi\circ E^{(k)}_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad F^{(k)}_{i}\circ\Phi=\Phi\circ F^{(k)}_{i}.

In other words
\Phi
is a strict crystal morphism. Similarly, also
\Phi^{-}
is a strict crystal morphism.

Proof.

For any pair of semistandard Young tableaux
(P,Q)
, call
(H_{1},H_{2})=\Phi(P,Q)
. By
Theorem

6.9
, we have
\mathbf{cRSK}^{t}\circ E^{(k)}_{i}(P,Q)=E^{(k)}_{i}\circ\mathbf{cRSK}^{t}(P,Q)
for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
. Taking
t>0
large enough this implies that, when
i\neq 0
, we have
\Phi\circ E^{(k)}_{i}(P,Q)=E^{(k)}_{i}(H_{1},H_{2})
. On the other hand case
i=0
was already covered in
Proposition

6.5
(II)
. This proves the first relation in (
6.29
). The remaining relation in (
6.29
) as well as the intertwining relations involving
\Phi^{-}
can be proven analogously.
∎

In view of
lemma

6.10
, we define the leading path for a pair of skew tableaux as the pull-back along
\Phi
of the leading path for pairs of horizontally weak tableaux.

Definition 6.11
.

Let
(P,Q)\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)
and
(H_{1},H_{2})=\Phi(P,Q)
.
The
leading path
\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}
for the pair
(P,Q)
is

\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}=\mathcal{L}_{H_{1},H_{2}},

where
\mathcal{L}_{H_{1},H_{2}}
is the leading path for the pair
(H_{1},H_{2})
introduced in
Definition

5.45
.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
\lambda,\rho\in\mathbb{S}
be signatures, and let
(P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)
. Assume that
(H_{1},H_{2})=\Phi(P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
for some partition
\mu
with
\ell(\mu)\leq n
. Assume that, for some
k\in\{1,2\}
and for some
H_{\mathrm{lw}}\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{\mathrm{lw}}
such that
D^{\prime}(H_{\mathrm{lw}})<D^{\prime}(H_{\max})
, we have

H_{k}=\underline{H_{\mathrm{lw}}},

where the element
\underline{H_{\mathrm{lw}}}
was introduced in
Lemma

5.41
. Let
(\overline{P}_{1},\overline{P}_{2})=E^{(k)}_{0}(P_{1},P_{2})
and let
\overline{\lambda}/\overline{\rho}
we the shape of
\overline{P}_{1},\overline{P}_{2}
. Then, for
\ell=\ell(\lambda)
, we have

(6.30a)

\displaystyle\overline{\lambda}_{\ell}=\lambda_{\ell},\qquad\qquad\overline{\rho}_{\ell}=\rho_{\ell},

(6.30b)

\displaystyle\lvert\overline{\rho}\rvert=\lvert\rho\rvert+1.

Proof.

Let
\ell^{\prime}=\ell(\mu)
, so that
\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell
and let
m=\min(H_{\mathrm{lw}})
. Denote by
H_{\mathrm{lw}}=h_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\prime}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{1}^{\prime}
where
\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell
and
\underline{H_{\mathrm{lw}}}=\underline{h_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\prime}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\underline{h_{1}^{\prime}}
. Since
H_{\mathrm{lw}}
is a lowest weight element,
\min(h_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\prime})=m
only if
m=n
, in which case
H_{\mathrm{lw}}
is in the same classically connected component of
H_{\max}
. Then, the hypothesis that
D^{\prime}(H_{\mathrm{lw}})<D^{\prime}(H_{\max})
implies that
h_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\prime}
contains no
m
entry and
\underline{h_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\prime}}
contains no
1
entries.

Let us prove the Lemma in the case
k=1
, since an analogous proof also works for the
k=2
case. By hypothesis we have
\Phi(P_{1},P_{2})=(\underline{H_{\mathrm{lw}}},H)
for some
H\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
. Since
\underline{H_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\prime}}
does not possess any
1
entry, then also the last row of
P_{1}
does not possess any
1
entry: this is a basic consequence of
lemma

3.12
, of the fact that the map
\mathbf{cRSK}=\iota_{2}^{n}
can be realized through column internal insertion (
Proposition

3.9
) and the definition of the projection
\Phi
.

By direct analysis of the action of the Kashiwara operator
E^{(1)}_{0}
, we see that the only way any of the equalities (
6.30a
) fails is that the action of
E^{(1)}_{1}
on
(\widetilde{P}_{1},\widetilde{P}_{2})=\iota_{1}^{-1}(P_{1},P_{2})
turns a 2 entry in the last row of
\widetilde{P}_{1}
into a 1 entry. Since the number of 2 entries in the last row of
\widetilde{P}_{1}
is equal to the number of 1 entries in the last row of
P_{1}
(which is zero), equalities (
6.30a
) always hold if
H_{\mathrm{hw}}\neq H_{\max}
. Finally, (
6.30b
) follows from the definition of the operator
E^{(k)}_{0}
and always holds as long as
E^{(1)}_{0}(P,Q)\neq\mathbf{0}
.
∎

We can now examine the transformation of a pair of tableaux under leading paths.

Theorem 6.13
.

Let
\lambda,\rho\in\mathbb{S}_{\ell}
be signatures of length
\ell
and let
(P,Q)\in\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)
. Assume that
(H_{1},H_{2})=\Phi(P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)^{2}
for some partition
\mu
with
\ell(\mu)\leq n
. Then, for any leading paths
\mathcal{L}_{H_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{H_{2}}
for
H_{1},H_{2}
, letting
\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}
be the corresponding leading path for
(P,Q)
and letting
\overline{\lambda}/\overline{\rho}
be the shape of
\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}(P,Q)
, we have

(6.31)

\lvert\overline{\rho}\rvert=\lvert\rho\rvert+2D^{\prime}(H_{\max})-D^{\prime}(H_{1})-D^{\prime}(H_{2})

and

(6.32)

\bar{\lambda}_{\ell}=\lambda_{\ell},\qquad\qquad\bar{\rho}_{\ell}=\rho_{\ell}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{H_{1}}
,
\mathcal{L}_{H_{2}}
be the leading paths corresponding to
H_{1},H_{2}
. They consist of classical Kashiwara operators and of 0-arrows joining
\underline{H_{\mathrm{lw}}}\to e_{0}(\underline{H_{\mathrm{lw}}})
for some lowest weight element
H_{\mathrm{lw}}
. Then, each path
\mathcal{L}_{H_{i}}
contains exactly
D^{\prime}(H_{\max})-D^{\prime}(H_{i})

e_{0}
arrows and, by repeated applications of
Lemma

6.12
, the action of the path
\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}
on the pair
(P,Q)
transforms the internal shape according to (
6.31
). Similarly, by
Lemma

6.12
the length last row of the tableaux
(P,Q)
never gets modified and (
6.32
) also holds.
∎

6.3.
Scattering rules

Using the crystal symmetry of
Theorem

6.9
we deduce asymptotic properties of the cRSK dynamics. For this recall the notion of stability of
Definition

3.14
.

Proposition 6.14
.

Let
(P,Q)\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)
and for

G\in\{\iota_{k},E^{(k)}_{i},F^{(k)}_{i}\mid 0\leq i\leq n-1,1\leq k\leq 2\},

let
(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q}):=G(P,Q)
. Then we have

(I)

(P,Q)
and
(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})
have a common positive and negative asymptotic shape;

(II)

\ker(P,Q)=\ker(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})
.

Proof.

Consider first the case
G\in\{\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\}
. It is evident from
Proposition

3.11
,
Proposition

3.4
,
(I)
,
(II)
and
Proposition

3.18
,
(IV)
, that the action of
G
cannot modify neither the asymptotic shape nor the kernel of
(P,Q)
.

Consider then the case
G\in\{E^{(k)}_{i},F^{(k)}_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n-1,1\leq k\leq 2\}
. By
Theorem

6.9
and
Proposition

3.4
,
(V)
, we have
\mathbf{cRSK}^{t}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})=\mathbf{cRSK}^{t}\circ G(P,Q)=G\circ\mathbf{cRSK}^{t}(P,Q)
for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
.
In particular, if
t
is large enough so that both
\mathbf{cRSK}^{t}(P,Q)
and
\mathbf{cRSK}^{t}(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})
are stable, it follows that the asymptotic increments and the kernel of
(P,Q)
and
(\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q})
are the same since
G
does not modify the shape of the tableaux it acts upon.

Finally, the statements
(I)
,
(II)
for the case
G\in\{E_{0}^{(k)},F_{0}^{(k)},1\leq k\leq 2\}
, follow combining the above two cases, since 0-operators are conjugations of 1-operators and
\iota
-maps as in (
6.2
).
∎

Theorem 6.15
.

Fix
(P,Q)\in\bigsqcup_{\rho,\lambda}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}
and let
(H_{1},H_{2})=\Phi(P,Q)
and
(H_{1}^{-},H_{2}^{-})=\Phi^{-}(P,Q)
. Assume that
H_{1},H_{2}\in\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
and
H_{1}^{-},H_{2}^{-}\in\mathrm{HWT}(\widetilde{\mu},n)
with
\ell(\mu)\leq n
. Then, we have

(I)

\widetilde{\mu}=\mu^{-}
, where
\mu^{-}=\sigma^{-}(\mu)
and
\sigma^{-}
is the total reverse permutation;

(II)

H_{k}^{-}=\sigma^{-}(H_{k})
for
k\in\{1,2\}
, where
\sigma^{-}
is the isomorphism of crystals defined in
Remark

5.12
.

Proof.

Consider a leading path
\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}
for the pair
(P,Q)
and let
(T,T)=\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}(P,Q)
be its image. Then,
T=\overline{\rho}\preceq\overline{\lambda}
is a tableaux with 1-entries only. By
Proposition

6.14
, the positive and negative asymptotic shapes
\mu,\widetilde{\mu}
of the pair
(P,Q)
are the same as those of the pair
(T,T)
. By
Proposition

4.10
the cRSK dynamics with initial data
(T,T)
is equivalent to the BBS dynamics with initial data
B=\Pr(T)
, where
\Pr
is the projection of
Definition

4.7
. Hence,
\mu
is also the asymptotic shape of the configuration
B
, which can be computed through the KKR map
\mathrm{KKR}(B)=(\mu,J)
for some rigging
J
. By
Theorem

4.3
this is equal, upon reversal, to the negative asymptotic shape
\widetilde{\mu}=\mu^{-}
and this proves
Item

(I)
.

To prove
Item

(II)
recall that, by
Theorem

6.9
, the map
\mathbf{cRSK}^{2t}:(P_{-t},Q_{-t})\to(P_{t},Q_{t})
commutes with the Kashiwara operators
E^{(k)}_{i},F^{(k)}_{i}
for
k\in\{1,2\}
and
i\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
.
Let
\mathcal{G}_{P,Q}
be a finite collection of compositions of Kashiwara operators so that the projection of the orbit of the pair
(P,Q)

\{\Phi(g(P,Q))\mid g\in\mathcal{G}_{P,Q}\}=\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
is the full set of HWT of shape
\mu
. Take
t>0
large enough so that all pairs in the sets
\{g(P_{-t},Q_{-t})\mid g\in\mathcal{G}_{P,Q}\}
and
\{(P_{t},Q_{t})\mid g\in\mathcal{G}_{P,Q}\}
are respectively negatively and positively stable. Then the map

\mathbf{cRSK}^{2t}\colon\{g(P_{-t},Q_{-t})\mid g\in\mathcal{G}_{P,Q}\}\to\{(P_{t},Q_{t})\mid g\in\mathcal{G}_{P,Q}\}

induces a surjective morphism of crystals
\mathrm{HWT}(\mu^{-},n)^{2}\to\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)^{2}
, which maps the element
(H_{\max}^{-},H_{\max}^{-})
to
(H_{\max},H_{\max})
, where
H_{\max}^{-}\in\mathrm{HWT}(\mu^{-},n)
,
H_{\max}\in\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
are the unique elements with only 1 entries. By symmetry also, the map
\mathbf{cRSK}^{-2t}
induces a surjective morphism
\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)^{2}\to\mathrm{HWT}(\mu^{-},n)^{2},
and we must have
(H_{1},H_{2})\to(\sigma^{-}(H_{1}),\sigma^{-}(H_{2}))
, since
\sigma^{-}
is the unique crystal isomorphism between
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\mu^{-}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

7.
The linearizing map: proof and examples

7.1.
Proof of
Theorem

1.7

In this subsection we construct explicitly the bijection
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}
transforming a pair of semistandard tableaux
(P,Q)
into a quadruple
(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa,\nu)
where
H_{1},H_{2}
are horizontally weak tableaux of same partition shape,
\kappa
is a list of integers and
\nu
is a signature. Most of the necessary ingredients to our construction have already been defined and studied in the above section.

Definition 7.1

(The map
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}
)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and let
(P,Q)\in\bigsqcup_{\rho,\lambda}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)^{2}
. We define the map

\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}(P,Q)=(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa,\nu)

as follows. First, we set
(H_{1},H_{2})=\Phi(P,Q)
, where
\Phi
is the soliton data map of
Definition

1.6
and denote by
\mu
be the common shape of
H_{1},H_{2}
. Set

n^{\prime}=\max\{\ell(\mu),n\}.

We regard
H_{1},H_{2}
as elements of
\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n^{\prime})
and
P,Q
as elements in
\bigsqcup_{\rho,\lambda}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n^{\prime})^{2}
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{H_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{H_{2}}
be the leading paths in
\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n^{\prime})
constructed in
Definition

5.42
. Using the corresponding operators on pairs of tableaux define
\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}=\mathcal{L}_{H_{1},H_{2}}
as in
Definition

6.11
. Then

(T,T)=\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}(P,Q),

for some tableau
T\in\mathrm{SST}(1)
. Applying to
T
the map
\Xi
of (
4.10
) we obtain a pair
(B,\nu)=\Xi(T)
. Finally let
(\mu,J)=\mathrm{KKR}(B)
be the rigged configuration associated with the BBS state
B
and set
\kappa=J+\mu
.

Remark 7.2
.

In the definition of
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}
, the passage from
n
to
n^{\prime}=\max\{\ell(\mu),n\}
should be understood only as an embedding of sets

\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)\subseteq\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n^{\prime}).

It is not an embedding of
U_{q}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n})
-crystals.
This enlargement of the alphabet is needed to guarantee the existence of the leading paths
\mathcal{L}_{H_{1}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{H_{2}}
used in the construction; see
Definition

5.42
. Since
n^{\prime}
is fixed by definition, the construction is unambiguous.

Definition 7.3

(The inverse map
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}^{-1}
)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and fix a quadruple

(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa,\nu)\in\bigsqcup_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}}\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)^{2}\times\mathcal{K}(\mu)\times\mathbb{S}.

Let
\mu
be the common shape of
H_{1},H_{2}
and let
J=\kappa-\mu
. Let
B=\mathrm{KKR}^{-1}(\mu,J)
be the corresponding BBS state and define
T=\Xi^{-1}(B,\nu)
.

Set
n^{\prime}=\max(\ell(\mu),n)
. Viewing
H_{1},H_{2}
as elements of
\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n^{\prime})
, let
\mathcal{L}_{H_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{H_{2}}
be the leading paths for
H_{1},H_{2}
constructed in
Definition

5.42
. Define
\mathcal{L}^{-1}=\mathcal{L}_{H_{1},H_{2}}^{-1}
. Finally, set
\mathcal{L}^{-1}(T,T)=(P,Q)
and define

\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}^{-1}(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa,\nu)=(P,Q).

We are now ready to prove our main theorem.

Proof of
Theorem

1.7
.

The map
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}
constructed in
Definition

7.1
is well posed and so is its inverse
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}^{-1}
given in
Definition

7.3
. The property (
1.24
) is part of the definition of
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}
. We then turn to the volume identity (
1.25
). Let
\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}
be the explicit leading map associated to the pair
(P,Q)
and let
(T,T)=\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}(P,Q)
with
T=\overline{\lambda}/\overline{\rho}
. Then, if
\lambda/\rho
is the shape of
P,Q
we have, combining
Theorem

6.13
and
Proposition

4.15

\begin{split}|\overline{\rho}|&=|\rho|+2D^{\prime}(H_{\max})-D^{\prime}(H_{1})-D^{\prime}(D_{2})\\
&=|\nu|+|J|+|\mu|+2\left\lVert\mu\right\rVert\end{split}

which implies (
1.25
), since,
\kappa=J+\mu
and
D^{\prime}(H_{\max})=\left\lVert\mu\right\rVert
by
Lemma

5.19
.

Let us now establish relation (
1.26
). Let
\bm{\lambda}=\bm{\lambda}[P,Q]
be the field of interlacing signatures associated to
P,Q
. Then, by definition, all signatures
\bm{\lambda}(p)
for
p\in\mathscr{C}_{n}
have the same length
\ell=\ell(\lambda)
. Recall the definition (
1.27
) of the signature
\nu
. Then for
i\in\{1,\dots,\ell(\nu)\}
the limit
\lim_{t\to-\infty}\bm{\lambda}_{i}(t,0)
exists finite, while for
i\in\{\ell(\nu)+1,\dots,\ell\}
we have, by
Proposition

3.11
and
Theorem

6.15
,

\lim_{t\to-\infty}\frac{\bm{\lambda}_{i}(t,0)}{t}=\mu_{i-\ell(\nu)}^{-}=\mu_{\ell(\mu)-i+\ell(\nu)+1}.

This shows that
\ell=\ell(\nu)+\ell(\mu)
.

Finally, assume that the shape
\lambda/\rho
of the pair
P,Q
is such that
\lambda,\rho
are partitions (i.e. all their entries are non-negative). By
Theorem

6.13
, the action of the leading path
\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}
on the pair
(P,Q)
produces a pair
(T,T)=\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}(P,Q)
where the tableau
T
has shape
\overline{\lambda}/\overline{\rho}
such that
\overline{\lambda}=\lambda_{\ell}\geq 0
and
\overline{\rho}=\rho_{\ell}\geq 0
. Then,
\Xi(T)=(B,\nu)
is such that
B\in\mathsf{BBS}_{>0}
and
\nu
is a signature with non negative entries. Since by
Theorem

4.3
, the image of the KKR map
\mathrm{KKR}(B)=(\mu,J)
is such that
J_{i}\geq-\mu_{i}
we have
\kappa\in\mathcal{K}_{+}(\mu)
. This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 7.4
.

Let
(P,Q)\in\bigsqcup_{\rho,\lambda}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)
and let
(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})=\mathbf{cRSK}(P,Q)
. Then,

(7.1)

\text{if}\quad\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}(P,Q)=(H_{1},H_{2};\kappa,\nu),\quad\text{we have}\quad\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})=(H_{1},H_{2};\kappa+\mu,\nu).

Proof.

It is clear, by
Definition

1.6
of the map
\Phi
and (
1.27
) that the elements
H_{1},H_{2},\nu
are common in the image under
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}
of
(P,Q)
and
(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
.
Let
\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}
be the leading map associated to the pair
(P,Q)
. By the crystal symmetry of the cRSK dynamics (
Theorem

6.9
),
\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}
is also a leading map for the pair
(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
. Let
(T,T)=\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}(P,Q)
and
(T^{\prime},T^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
. Then, always by
Theorem

6.9
, we have
(T^{\prime},T^{\prime})=\mathbf{cRSK}(T,T)
. By
Proposition

4.10
, denoting
B=\Pr(T)
and
B^{\prime}=\Pr(T^{\prime})
, we have
B^{\prime}=\mathsf{T}(B)
and their image under the KKR map is, by
Theorem

4.3

\mathrm{KKR}(B)=(\mu,J),\qquad\mathrm{KKR}(B^{\prime})=(\mu,J+\mu).

Then, by
Definition

7.1
, (
7.1
) follows.
∎

7.2.
An example

Let us review the construction of the map
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}
through an example. Consider the pair of tableaux
(P,Q)
given below

Figure 17
.
An example of a skew column RSK dynamics.

The explicit leading path for the horizontally weak tableau
H_{2}
was presented in
Figure

16
, whereas the one for
H_{1}
is given below

\includegraphics[width]{leading_path_example_3.pdf}.

Then, the leading path for the pair
(P,Q)
is

(7.17)

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}=&F^{(1)}_{1}\circ\bigl(F^{(1)}_{2}\bigr)^{3}\circ\underline{\mathcal{P}}^{(1)}\circ E_{0}^{(1)}\circ\bigl(E^{(1)}_{1}\bigr)^{6}\circ E_{2}^{(1)}\\
&\quad\circ\bigl(F_{1}^{(2)}\bigr)^{2}\circ\bigl(F_{2}^{(2)}\bigr)^{3}\circ\underline{\mathcal{P}}^{(2)}\circ E_{0}^{(2)}\circ F_{1}^{(2)}\circ\bigl(F_{1}^{(2)}\bigr)^{4}\circ\underline{\mathcal{P}}^{(2)}\circ E_{0}^{(2)}\circ\left(E^{(2)}_{1}\right)^{6}\circ E_{2}^{(2)},\end{split}

where we denoted by
\underline{\mathcal{P}}^{(1)}=\underline{\mathcal{P}}\times 1
and
\underline{\mathcal{P}}^{(2)}=1\times\underline{\mathcal{P}}
the componentwise action of the map
\underline{\mathcal{P}}
of (
5.23
).
We can then compute its action as in
Figure

18
, from which we find

\mathcal{L}_{P,Q}(P,Q)=(T,T)\qquad\text{with}\qquad 8.
Generalized Greene’s invariants

In this section we prove
Theorem

1.11
which gives a last passage percolation characterization of the asymptotic shape
\mu
of the cRSK dynamics.

8.1.
The skew row RSK dynamics and the skew column RSK dynamics

Let us describe a basic relation between the cRSK dynamics and the
skew (row) RSK dynamics
introduced in
[
44
]
. We will only discuss the case of standard Young tableaux, as this is all we will need.

Given a signature
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}_{\ell}
of length
\ell
recall that its Young diagram is the set of cells
(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
such that
i\leq\lambda_{j}
and
j\in\{1,\dotsc,\ell\}
.
We define its
transposed diagram

\lambda^{\mathsf{t}}
as the set of cells
(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\mathbb{Z}
such that
(j,i)
belongs to the Young diagram of
\lambda
, i.e.
j\leq\lambda_{i}
and
i\in\{1,\dotsc,\ell\}
.
In other words, the transposed diagram
\lambda^{\mathsf{t}}
is obtained from
\lambda
by a reflection across the main diagonal of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
When
\lambda
is a partition, the notion of transposed
\lambda^{\mathsf{t}}
coincides with the conjugate diagram
\lambda^{\prime}
.

We have defined a standard Young tableau
P
of shape
\lambda/\rho
as a sequence of signatures
P=(\rho=\lambda^{(0)}\preceq\cdots\preceq\lambda^{(n)}=\lambda)
such that
|\lambda^{(k)}/\lambda^{(k-1)}|\in\{0,1\}
for all
k=1,\dots,n
. We define the
transposed standard tableau

P^{\mathsf{t}}
as the sequence of transposed diagrams

P^{\mathsf{t}}=\left(\rho^{\mathsf{t}}=\left(\lambda^{(0)}\right)^{\mathsf{t}}\dot{\preceq}\cdots\dot{\preceq}\left(\lambda^{(N)}\right)^{\mathsf{t}}=\lambda^{\mathsf{t}}\right).

(Here the notation
\eta^{\mathsf{t}}\dot{\preceq}\,\nu^{\mathsf{t}}
means that
\eta\preceq\nu
.)
We can also represent the transposed standard tableau
P^{\mathsf{t}}
by assigning label
k
to the cell
(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\mathbb{Z}
if
\lambda^{(k)}/\lambda^{(k-1)}=\{(j,i)\}\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. In other words, the transposed tableau
P^{\mathsf{t}}
is obtained from
P
through a reflection across the main diagonal of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

Given a
fully refined
Fomin field
\bm{\lambda}
, let
(P_{t},Q_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the associated cRSK dynamics with initial data
(P_{0},Q_{0})
as in
Definition

1.4
.
The
skew row RSK dynamics
associated to the field
\bm{\lambda}
is the sequence of pairs of transposed standard tableaux
(P_{t}^{\mathsf{t}},Q_{t}^{\mathsf{t}})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
.
In
Section

3.3
we presented an alternative construction of the cRSK dynamics through a sequence of column Schensted insertions.
An analogous description of the skew row RSK dynamics can be given replacing column insertions by row insertion.

Example 8.1
.

The fully refined Fomin field of signatures
\bm{\lambda}
of
Figure

11
is associated to the skew row RSK dynamics
(P_{t}^{\mathsf{t}},Q_{t}^{\mathsf{t}})
which at times
t=-2,0,2
take values

\includegraphics[width]{row_RSK_dynamics_example.pdf}.

Notice that tableaux
(P_{t}^{\mathsf{t}},Q_{t}^{\mathsf{t}})
are simply the transposition of tableaux
(P_{t},Q_{t})
given in
Figure

7
, after standardization.

Just as in the case of the skew (column) RSK dynamics, we can define soliton data of skew row RSK dynamics
(P_{t}^{\mathsf{t}},Q_{t}^{\mathsf{t}})
.
We define the
asymptotic pair of vertically strict (standard) taubleax

(V_{1},V_{2})
associated to the skew row RSK dynamics
(P_{t}^{\mathsf{t}},Q_{t}^{\mathsf{t}})
, or equivalently to the Fomin field
\bm{\lambda}
, as

V_{1}=H_{1}^{\mathsf{t}},\qquad\qquad V_{2}=H_{2}^{\mathsf{t}},

where
(H_{1},H_{2})
is the asymptotic pair of horizontally strict tableaux associated to the Fomin field
\bm{\lambda}
. (Here, as above, the transposed of a horizontally weak tableau
H
is the tableau
V=H^{\mathsf{t}}
which has at cell
(i,j)
the value
H
has at cell
(j,i)
.) For example, the asymptotic pair of vertically strict tableaux associated to the dynamics of
Example

8.1
is

V_{1}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern
