height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 4$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}.

Let us now cite a result from
[
44
]
, which will be useful to us.

Proposition 8.2

(Special case of
[
44
]
, Theorem 6.6)
.

Let
\bm{\lambda}
be a fully refined Fomin field on
\mathscr{C}_{n}
and let
\mathbf{c}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime},\{0,1\})
be its environment; recall the notation (
1.30
). Let
(P_{t}^{\mathsf{t}},Q_{t}^{\mathsf{t}})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the skew row RSK dynamics associated to
\bm{\lambda}
and let
(V_{1},V_{2})
be the asymptotic pair of vertically strict tableau of shape
\mu
. Then, we have

\mu_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+\mu_{k}^{\prime}=\mathrm{LPP}^{\circlearrowleft}_{k}(\mathbf{c})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mu_{1}+\dots+\mu_{k}=\mathrm{LPP}^{\nearrow}_{k}(\mathbf{c}).

Remark 8.3
.

The result of
Proposition

8.2
cannot be naively generalized to Fomin fields which are not fully refined. The generalization to such cases involves changing the definition of the statistics
\mathrm{LPP}^{\nearrow}_{k}
and
\mathrm{LPP}^{\circlearrowleft}_{k}
; see
[
44
]
, Theorem 6.6 for more details.

8.2.
Generalized Greene’s invariants for the skew column RSK dynamics

In this subsection we prove
Theorem

1.11
. Let us first present the proof of items
(II)
,
(III)
,
(IV)
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.11

(II)
,
(III)
,
(IV)
.

By the periodic column RSK correspondence of
Proposition

1.10
, any environment
\mathbf{c}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime},\mathbb{N})
is in bijection with a pair
(P,Q)
with
\ker(P,Q)=\varnothing
. Then, we define
\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mathrm{col}}(\mathbf{c})=(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa)
, where
(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa,\nu=\varnothing)=\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}(P,Q)
. Let
\bm{\lambda}=\bm{\lambda}[P,Q]
be the Fomin field of interlacing signatures on
\mathscr{C}_{n}
associated to the pair
(P,Q)
and denote by
\mu
the shape of
H_{1},H_{2}
.

The restriction property
(IV)
is immediate from the definition of
\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mathrm{col}}
. In fact, by
Theorem

1.7
,
\kappa\in\mathcal{K}_{+}(\mu)
if and only if the tableaux
P,Q
have partition shape. This happens if and only if for each
i,j\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
and
k\geq 0
the signature
\bm{\lambda}(i,j+nk)
is a partition. Then, by
Proposition

2.3
and (
2.12
) we have

(8.7)

\begin{split}&\mathbf{c}(i+\frac{1}{2},j+\frac{1}{2}+kn)\\
&=\sum_{s\geq 1}\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{\prime}(i+1,j+1+kn)-\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{\prime}(i,j+1+kn)-\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{\prime}(i+1,j+kn)+\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{\prime}(i,j+kn)\\
&=|\bm{\lambda}(i+1,j+1+kn)/\bm{\lambda}(i,j+1+kn)|-|\bm{\lambda}(i+1,j+kn)/\bm{\lambda}(i,j+kn)|=0,\end{split}

for each
i,j\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
and
k\geq 0
. Then,
\kappa\in\mathcal{K}_{+}(\mu)
implies that
\mathbf{c}
is supported in
\mathscr{C}_{n}^{-}
. The same argument also shows that if
\mathbf{c}
is supported in
\mathscr{C}_{n}^{-}
we also necessarily have
\kappa\in\mathcal{K}_{+}(\mu)
.

The property (
1.34
) relating the weights of tableaux
H_{1},H_{2}
with the environment
\mathbf{c}
follows from relation (
3.4
) and from the definition of
H_{1},H_{2}
as the limits (
1.20a
), (
1.20a
).

To prove (
1.35
), let
K_{*}>0
be large enough so that
\mathbf{c}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}+Kn)=\mathbf{c}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}-Kn)=0
for all
i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in\{\frac{1}{2},\dots,n-\frac{1}{2}\}
and
K\geq K_{*}
. This implies that
\bm{\lambda}(i,j+Kn)
(resp.
\bm{\lambda}(i,j-Kn)
) is a signature with only negative elements for all
K\geq K_{*}
. Then, we have

(8.8)

\begin{split}|\bm{\lambda}(0,K_{*}n)|&=\sum_{s\geq 1}\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{\prime}(0,K_{*}n)\\
&=\sum_{s\geq 1}\left[\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{\prime}(0,K_{*}n)-2\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{\prime}(0,(K_{*}-1)-n)+\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{\prime}(-n,(K_{*}-1)n)\right]\\
&\hskip 20.0pt+\sum_{s\geq 1}\left[2\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{\prime}(0,(K_{*}-1)n)-\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{\prime}(-n,(K_{*}-1)n)\right]\\
&=\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in\{\frac{1}{2},\dots,n-\frac{1}{2}\}}\mathbf{c}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}+(K_{*}-1)n)\\
&\hskip 20.0pt+\sum_{s\geq 1}\left[2\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{\prime}(0,(K_{*}-1)n)-\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{\prime}(-n,(K_{*}-1)n)\right],\end{split}

where in the last equality we used (
2.12
). Iterating the above relation we find

(8.9)

\begin{split}|\bm{\lambda}(0,K_{*}n)|&=\sum_{k=1}^{2K_{*}}\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in\{\frac{1}{2},\dots,n-\frac{1}{2}\}}k\,\mathbf{c}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}+(K_{*}-k)n)\\
&\hskip 20.0pt+\sum_{s\geq 1}\left[2\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{\prime}(0,-K_{*}n)-\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{\prime}(-n,-K_{*}n)\right],\\
&=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in\{\frac{1}{2},\dots,n-\frac{1}{2}\}}k\,\mathbf{c}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}+(K_{*}-k)n)\\
&=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in\{\frac{1}{2},\dots,n-\frac{1}{2}\}}k^{\prime}\,\mathbf{c}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}-k^{\prime}n)-K_{*}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in\{\frac{1}{2},\dotsc,n-\frac{1}{2}\}}\,\mathbf{c}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}-k^{\prime}n),\end{split}

where in the second equality, we extended the sum over
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, using the fact that the environment
\mathbf{c}
is zero for these terms, and we suppressed the sum
\sum_{s\geq 1}\left[2\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{\prime}(0,-K_{*}n)-\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{\prime}(-n,-K_{*}n)\right]
, since every of its summands is zero. In the last equality we simply operated the change of variables
k^{\prime}=k-K_{*}
. From (
1.34
) and the fact that
|\bm{\lambda}(0,Kn)|-|\bm{\lambda}(0,(K-1)n)|
for any
K\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

|\mu|=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in\{\frac{1}{2},\dots,n-\frac{1}{2}\}}\,\mathbf{c}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}-k^{\prime}n)=\lvert\bm{\lambda}(0,Kn)\rvert-\lvert\bm{\lambda}(0,(K-1)n)\rvert,

and plugging the above relation in (
8.9
) we obtain

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in\{\frac{1}{2},\dots,n-\frac{1}{2}\}}k^{\prime}\,\mathbf{c}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}-k^{\prime}n)=\bm{\lambda}(0,0).

Then, since
\bm{\lambda}(0,0)=\rho
is the empty shape of the tableaux
P,Q
, this proves (
1.35
) using (
1.25
).

∎

From
Proposition

8.2
we can easily prove
Theorem

1.11
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.11

(I)
.

Adopt the notation used above in the subsection. Define also
\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}
the refinement of the field
\bm{\lambda}
, let
(\widetilde{P}_{t},\widetilde{Q}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the associated cRSK dynamics and let
\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathscr{C}_{N}^{\prime},\{0,1\})
be the corresponding environment, where
N=|\bm{\lambda}(n,0)/\bm{\lambda}(0,0)|
.

Consider
(\widetilde{P}^{\mathsf{t}}_{t},\widetilde{Q}^{\mathsf{t}}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
the skew row RSK dynamics associated to the field
\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}
and its soliton data consisting of a pair of vertically strict tableaux
(V_{1},V_{2})
of shape
\mu^{\prime}
. Then
Proposition

8.2
implies that

\mu_{1}+\cdots+\mu_{k}=\mathrm{LPP}^{\circlearrowleft}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mu_{1}^{\prime}+\dots+\mu_{k}^{\prime}=\mathrm{LPP}^{\nearrow}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}),

for all
k\geq 1
.
To complete the proof we need to observe that, for all
k\geq 1
, we have

\mathrm{LPP}^{\circlearrowleft}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})=\mathrm{LPP}^{\circlearrowleft}_{k}(\mathbf{c})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{LPP}^{\nearrow}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})=\mathrm{LPP}^{\nearrow}_{k}(\mathbf{c}).

This is relatively straightforward from the construction of
\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}
from
\mathbf{c}
. Below we only show the identity
\mathrm{LPP}^{\circlearrowleft}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})=\mathrm{LPP}^{\circlearrowleft}_{k}(\mathbf{c})
as the remaining one can be proved analogously.

Recall, from
Proposition

3.1
, the embedding
\phi:\mathscr{C}_{n}\to\mathscr{C}_{N}
and for each
p^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime}
define the rectangle
\mathscr{R}(p^{\prime})\subset\mathscr{C}_{N}
of all faces in the rectangular sublattice of
\mathscr{C}_{N}
with vertices
\phi(p^{\prime}\pm\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{e}_{1}\pm\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{e}_{2})
. Denote also by
\mathscr{D}(p^{\prime})
the set of all faces
\widetilde{p}\,^{\prime}\subset\mathscr{R}(p^{\prime})
such that
\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}(\widetilde{p}\,^{\prime})=1
. Then,
\mathscr{D}(p^{\prime})
consists of
\mathbf{c}(p^{\prime})
faces arranged along an anti-diagonal of
\mathscr{R}(p^{\prime})
as described in
Proposition

2.2
and
Figure

9
.

Let
\mathbf{p}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{(k)}
be
k
non-intersecting simple loops in
\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime}
. Then, we can construct
k
non-intersecting simple loops
\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}^{(1)},\dots,\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}^{(k)}
in
\mathscr{C}_{N}^{\prime}
such that

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{2n+1}\mathbf{c}(\mathbf{p}_{j}^{(i)})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{2n+1}\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}_{j}^{(i)}),

by first replacing any node
\mathbf{p}_{j}^{(i)}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime}
by a sequence of nodes crossing all faces of
\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{p}_{j}^{(i)})
and later connecting these nodes with down-right paths. An example of such construction is given below

\includegraphics[width]{path_construction.pdf}.

Such procedure is possible since the set of faces
\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{p}_{j+1}^{(i)})
lies strictly down and to the right of
\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{p}_{j}^{(i)})
for all
j
. This implies that

\mathrm{LPP}^{\circlearrowleft}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})\geq\mathrm{LPP}^{\circlearrowleft}_{k}(\mathbf{c}).

For the opposite inequality consider a
k
-tuple of non-intersecting down-right loops
\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(1)},\dots,\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(k)}
of
\mathscr{C}_{N}^{\prime}
. We start by re-routing
\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(1)}
as follows: if
\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(1)}
intersects
\mathscr{R}(p^{\prime})
for some
p^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime}
, then we deform it so that contains all cells of
\mathscr{D}(p^{\prime})
: such deformation can be obtained through a finite sequence of "flips"
(\widetilde{p}^{\prime},\widetilde{p}^{\prime}+\mathbf{e}_{1},\widetilde{p}^{\prime}+\mathbf{e}_{1}-\mathbf{e}_{2})\leftrightarrow(\widetilde{p}^{\prime},\widetilde{p}^{\prime}-\mathbf{e}_{2},\widetilde{p}^{\prime}+\mathbf{e}_{1}-\mathbf{e}_{2})
of right-down turn into down-right turn (or viceversa) of
\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(1)}
. If during such deformation the loop
\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(1)}
crosses other loops
\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(i)}
, we push the latter in the direction suggested by the flips, so to avoid intersections. After completing such procedure we obtain a new
k
-tuple of non-intersecting down-right loops, which we call
\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(1,1)},\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(2,1)},\dots,\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(k,1)}
on
\mathscr{C}_{N}^{\prime}
, with the property that

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{2N+1}\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(i,1)}_{j})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{2N+1}\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(i)}_{j}).

We then sequentially proceed to generate
k
-tuple of loops
\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(1,\ell)},\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(2,\ell)},\dots,\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(k,\ell)}
for
\ell=1,\dots,k
, where at each
\ell

we re-route the paths
\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(j,\ell)}
for
j>\ell
with the same strategy described in the case
\ell=1
and the additional condition that if
\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(j,\ell)}
crosses some rectangle
\mathscr{R}(p^{\prime})
which is already crossed by
\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(i,\ell)}
with
i\leq\ell
(which implies that the
\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(i,\ell)}
contains all faces of
\mathscr{D}(p^{\prime})
), then we deform
\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(j,\ell)}
so that it does not intersect the rectangle
\mathscr{R}(p^{\prime})
. As in the
\ell=1
case we still have the property

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{2N+1}\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(i,\ell)}_{j})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{2N+1}\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(i,\ell-1)}_{j}).

By the end of this procedure we will be left with a
k
-tuple
\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}^{(i)}=\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}^{(i,k)}
for
i=1,\dotsc,k
.
By construction, none of the rectangles
\mathscr{R}(p^{\prime})
is crossed by more that one loop
\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}^{(i)}
and whenever a loop
\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}^{(i)}
crossed
\mathscr{R}(p^{\prime})
it also crosses the faces
\mathscr{D}(p^{\prime})
.
As a result we can associate to the
k
-tuple of loops
\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}^{(1)},\dotsc,\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}^{(k)}
on
\mathscr{C}_{N}^{\prime}
a
k
-tuple of non-intersecting loops
\mathbf{p}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{(k)}
on
\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime}
with the property that

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{2N+1}\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}^{(i)}_{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{2N+1}\mathbf{c}(\mathbf{p}^{(i)}_{j}).

As a result we have

\mathrm{LPP}^{\circlearrowleft}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})\leq\mathrm{LPP}^{\circlearrowleft}_{k}(\mathbf{c}),

which completes the proof.
∎

9.
Symmetric environments and symmetric fields

In this section we analyze special cases of the bijections of
Theorem

1.7
and
Theorem

1.11
under symmetric inputs. For this we define the transposition of an environment
\mathbf{c}
as
\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{t}}(i,j)=\mathbf{c}(j,i)
. Similar to (
1.30
), for any set
B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
such that
0\in B
, we define the set of symmetric environments

\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathrm{sym}}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime},B)=\{\mathbf{c}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime},B)\mid\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{t}}=\mathbf{c}\}.

Corollary 9.1
.

Restricting the bijection
\Psi
of
Proposition

1.10
to symmetric inputs
(P,P)
yields a bijection

(9.1)

\Psi\colon P\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho\in\mathbb{S}}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)\longrightarrow(\mathbf{c},\nu)\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathrm{sym}}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime},\mathbb{N})\times\mathbb{S}.

The result of
Corollary

9.1
is a variant of
[
90
, Corollary 4.4]
. The fact that for any tableau
P
the associated environment
\mathbf{c}
such that
\Psi(P)=(\mathbf{c},\nu)
is symmetric is consequence of the symmetry of the Fomin local rule.

For the next theorem recall that for any array
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
,
\mathrm{odd}(\alpha)
is the number of its odd entries.

Theorem 9.2
.

Restricting the bijection
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}
of
Theorem

1.7
to symmetric inputs
(P,P)
yields a bijection

(9.2)

\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}\colon P\in\bigsqcup_{\lambda,\rho\in\mathbb{S}}\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)\longrightarrow(H,\kappa,\nu)\in\bigsqcup_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}}\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)\times\mathcal{K}(\mu)\times\mathbb{S}.

The shapes
\rho,\lambda
are integer partitions if and only if
\nu
is also a partition and
\kappa\in\mathcal{K}_{+}(\mu)
.
Moreover, we have

(9.3)

\mathrm{odd}(\lambda)=\mathrm{odd}(\nu)+\mathrm{odd}(\kappa+\mu)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{odd}(\rho)=\mathrm{odd}(\nu)+\mathrm{odd}(\kappa).

Proof.

The cRSK dynamics with initial data
(P,P)
is a sequence of pairs of identical tableaux
(P_{t},P_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
as a consequence of the symmetry of the Fomin local rules. As a result the associated soliton data also consists of a pair of identical horizontally weak tableaux
\Phi(P,P)=(H,H)
. This proves the first part of the theorem.

Relations (
9.3
) are less trivial. Notice that we can assume that
\lambda,\rho
are partitions, otherwise we would consider the tableau
\widetilde{P}
obtained shifting each row of
P
by
2m
cells to the right with
2m\geq\rho_{\ell}
. The image under
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}
of
\widetilde{P}
can be checked to be the triple
(H,\widetilde{\kappa},\widetilde{\nu})
with
\widetilde{\kappa}_{i}=\kappa+2m
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{i}=\nu_{i}+2m
and such even shifts leave relations (
9.3
) unchanged.
Moreover, since relations (
9.3
) only concern the shape of the tableau
P
and
H
and the value of
\kappa
, we can further assume that
P
is a standard tableau. The transposed tableau
P^{\mathsf{t}}
can be put in bijection with the triple
(H^{\mathsf{t}},\kappa,\nu^{\prime})
, where
(H^{\mathsf{t}},H^{\mathsf{t}})
is the asymptotic pair of vertically strict tableaux associated to the skew row RSK dynamics
(P_{t}^{\mathsf{t}},P_{t}^{\mathsf{t}})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
with initial condition
(P^{\mathsf{t}},P^{\mathsf{t}})
. Such bijection is a particular case of the one discovered in
[
44
]
and for that we can invoke
[
39
, Lemmas 3.2, 3.6]
, which claims that

\mathrm{odd}(\lambda^{\prime})=\mathrm{odd}(\nu^{\prime})+\mathrm{odd}(\mu^{\prime}+\kappa),\qquad\mathrm{odd}(\rho^{\prime})=\mathrm{odd}(\nu^{\prime})+\mathrm{odd}(\kappa),

where
\mu^{\prime}
is the shape of
H^{\mathsf{t}}
. Transposing partitions
\lambda,\rho,\mu,\nu
in the above relation proves (
9.3
).
∎

For any environmnent
\mathbf{c}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime},\mathbb{N})
define

\mathrm{oddTr}(\mathbf{c})=\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{c}(i+kn,i)\text{ is odd}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{oddTr}^{-}(\mathbf{c})=\sum_{k\geq 0}(-1)^{k}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{c}(i+kn,i)\text{ is odd}}.

Theorem 9.3
.

Restricting the bijection
\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mathrm{col}}
of
Theorem

1.11
to symmetric environments yields a bijection

(9.4)

\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mathrm{col}}\colon\mathbf{c}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathrm{sym}}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime},\mathbb{N})\longrightarrow(H,\kappa)\in\bigsqcup_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}}\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)\times\mathcal{K}(\mu).

The environment
\mathbf{c}
is supported in
\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime-}
if and only if
\kappa\in\mathcal{K}_{+}(\mu)
.
Moreover, we have

(9.5)

\mathrm{oddTr}(\mathbf{c})=\mathrm{odd}(\kappa+\mu)+\mathrm{odd}(\kappa)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{oddTr}^{-}(\mathbf{c})=\mathrm{odd}(\kappa+\mu)-\mathrm{odd}(\kappa).

Proof.

The only non immediate statements are those in (
9.5
) and as in the proof of
Theorem

9.2
, we will borrow results from
[
44
,
39
]
.
Let
\bm{\lambda}\colon\mathscr{C}_{n}\to\mathbb{S}
be a symmetric Fomin field of signatures associated to the environment
\mathbf{c}
and let
\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}\colon\mathscr{C}_{N}\to\mathbb{S}
be its refinement for
N\geq n
chosen appropriately. Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathrm{sym}}(\mathscr{C}_{N}^{\prime},\{0,1\})
be the environment associated to the field
\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}
. The fields
\mathbf{c}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}
are related by
Proposition

2.2
and
Proposition

3.1
. Then, defining

\mathrm{Tr}(\mathbf{c})=\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{c}(i+kn,i)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{Tr}^{-}(\mathbf{c})=\sum_{k\geq 0}(-1)^{k}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{c}(i+kn,i),

we notice that

(9.6)

\mathrm{oddTr}(\mathbf{c})=\mathrm{oddTr}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})=\mathrm{Tr}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{oddTr}^{-}(\mathbf{c})=\mathrm{oddTr}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})=\mathrm{Tr}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}).

The equalities
\mathrm{oddTr}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})=\mathrm{Tr}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})
and
\mathrm{oddTr}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})=\mathrm{Tr}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})
are straightforward, since
\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}
only takes values in
\{0,1\}
. The equality
\mathrm{oddTr}(\mathbf{c})=\mathrm{oddTr}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})
follows from
Proposition

2.2
. The refinement of the generic diagonal cell
(i,i)
, where the environment takes value
c=\mathbf{c}(i,i)
produces a
N_{i}\times N_{i}
square sublattice
\Lambda_{N_{i},N_{i}}\subset\mathscr{C}_{N}
for some
N_{i}
where
\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}(p^{\prime})=1
only if
p^{\prime}=(A_{i}+k,A_{i}+c+1-k)
for some
k\in\{1,\dots,c\}
and where the number
A_{i}
is determined through
Proposition

3.1
and
Proposition

2.2
. It is clear that the set
\{(A+k,A+c+1-k)\mid 1\leq k\leq c\}\}
admits a point on the diagonal only when
c
is odd. Similarly one shows that
\mathrm{oddTr}^{-}(\mathbf{c})=\mathrm{oddTr}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})
.

Now consider the images
\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mathrm{col}}(\mathbf{c})=(H,\kappa)
and
\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mathrm{col}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})=(\widetilde{H},\kappa)
. By construction
\widetilde{H}
is the standardization of
H
and both tableaux have the same shape
\mu
. Moreover the map
\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mathrm{row}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})=(\widetilde{H}^{\mathsf{t}},\kappa)
, is a bijection between the set of fully refined environments
\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}
and pairs
(\widetilde{H}^{\mathsf{t}},\kappa)
where
\widetilde{H}^{\mathsf{t}}
is a vertically strict standard tableau of shape
\mu^{\prime}
and
\kappa\in\mathcal{K}(\mu)
which is a particular case of
[
44
, Corollary 8.2]
. For the bijection
\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mathrm{row}}
it was found in
[
39
, Lemma 3.6]
that

\mathrm{Tr}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})=\mathrm{odd}(\kappa+(\mu^{\prime})^{\prime})+\mathrm{odd}(\kappa)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{Tr}^{-}(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}})=\mathrm{odd}(\kappa+(\mu^{\prime})^{\prime})-\mathrm{odd}(\kappa).

In the above relations we kept the dependence on
\mu^{\prime}
as it is the shape of
\widetilde{H}^{\mathsf{t}}
.
Since
(\mu^{\prime})^{\prime}=\mu
, combining these identities with relations (
9.6
), completes the proof of (
9.5
).
∎

10.
Summation identities for transformed Hall–Littlewood polynomials

10.1.
Transformed Hall–Littlewood polynomials

In this section we present bijective proofs of Cauchy, Littlewood identities and their refinements for transformed Hall–Littlewood polynomials.

Definition 10.1
.

Fix parameters
q,x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
and a partition
\mu
.
The
transformed Hall–Littlewood polynomial
is

(10.1)

\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x;q)=\sum_{H\in\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)}q^{D^{\prime}(H)}x^{H}.

There is another combinatorial description of the transformed Hall–Littlewood polynomials as the graded character of
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
(equivalently
\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
).
Recall that
\wt_{j}(b)
being the number of entries ‘
j
’s in
b
and
D
is the energy function defined in
definition

5.17
.

Remark 10.2
.

The transformed Hall–Littlewood polynomials are symmetric with respect to the permutation of the
x
variables. This is consequence of the fact that the action of classical Kashiwara operators induces an action of the symmetric group
S_{n}
on
\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)
: see
[
55
, Thm. 7.2.2]
. Then, the relation
D^{\prime}(H)=D(\eta(H))
and an inspection of the involution
\eta
of (
5.11
), implies that the polynomials
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x;q)
could be equivalently defined, replacing the dual energy
D^{\prime}
with
D
, as

(10.2)

\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x;q)=\sum_{H\in\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)}q^{D(H)}x^{H}.

Such definition is more common in literature; see
[
59
, Thm. 2.10]
and
[
56
,
57
,
82
]
.

Remark 10.3
.

Our convention for the transformed Hall–Littlewood polynomials
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(x;q)
match what were called the modified Hall–Littlewood polynomials in
[
25
]
.
Indeed, these differ from what are usually called the modified Hall–Littlewood polynomials in the combinatorics literature (
e.g.
,
[
29
,
27
,
28
]
), which are a specialization of the modified Macdonald polynomials
\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{\mu}(x;q,t)
by
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\mu}=q^{\left\lVert\mu\right\rVert}\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{\mu}(x;0,q^{-1})
, where
\left\lVert\mu\right\rVert
is given in (
5.15
).

The difference reflects our energy convention: the maximal weight tableau, whose entries are all
1
, has maximal rather than minimal energy as in e.g.
[
34
,
32
,
31
,
47
,
59
,
68
]
. Thus, we have the specialization
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\mu}(1,0,\ldots;q)=q^{\left\lVert\mu\right\rVert}
.
In terms of Macdonald polynomials
P_{\lambda}(x;q,t),Q_{\lambda}(x;q,t)
, borrowing the notation of
[
80
]
, we have

\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x;q)=Q_{\mu}(x/(1-q);0,q)=\omega P_{\mu^{\prime}}(x;q,0),

where the substitution
x/(1-q)
is to be interpreted as a plethysm and
\omega
is the Hall involution. The above equality relates the transformed Hall-Littlewood polynomials with the Hall-Littlewood polynomials
Q_{\mu}(x;0,q)
and with the
q
-Whittaker polynomials
P_{\mu}(x;q,0)
.

In the following subsections we will use the following coefficients

(10.3)

\mathpzc{b}_{\mu}(q)=\prod_{i\geq 1}\frac{1}{(q;q)_{m_{i}(\mu)}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathpzc{b}_{\mu}(\alpha,\beta;q)=\prod_{i=1,3,5,\dots}\frac{[\alpha+q\beta]_{q^{2}}^{m_{i}(\mu)}}{(q^{2};q^{2})_{m_{i}(\mu)}}\prod_{i=2,4,6,\dots}\frac{[1+q\alpha\beta]_{q^{2}}^{m_{i}(\mu)}}{(q^{2};q^{2})_{m_{i}(\mu)}},

where
m_{i}(\mu)=\#\{j:\mu_{j}=i\}
is the multiplicity of
i
in
\mu
and the symbol
[a+b]_{p}^{n}
denotes

(10.4)

[a+b]_{p}^{n}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}_{p}a^{n-k}b^{k},\qquad\text{with}\qquad\binom{n}{k}_{q}=\frac{(q;q)_{n}}{(q;q)_{k}(q;q)_{n-k}},\quad\text{for }0\leq k\leq n.

10.2.
Cauchy identities and refinements

Here we prove identities (
1.36
) from
theorem

1.14
and (
1.38
) from
Theorem

1.15
.

Proof of
theorem

1.14
, eq. (
1.36
).

Using the geometric series to expand every factor
1/(1-q^{k}x_{i}y_{j})
in the left hand side of (
1.36
), we have

\begin{split}\prod_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(x_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}}&=\prod_{i,j=1}^{n}\prod_{k\geq 0}\left(\sum_{c_{i,j,k}\geq 0}\left(q^{k}x_{i}y_{j}\right)^{c_{i,j,k}}\right)\\
&=\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime-},\mathbb{N})}\left(q^{\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\sum_{k\geq 0}k\,\mathbf{c}(i,j+k)}\right)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\mathbf{c}(i,\ell)}\right)\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}^{\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\mathbf{c}(\ell,j)}\right)\\
&=\sum_{(H_{1},H_{2};\kappa)\in\bigsqcup_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}}\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)^{2}\times\mathcal{K}_{+}(\mu)}q^{D^{\prime}(H_{1})+D^{\prime}(H_{2})+|\kappa|}x^{H_{1}}y^{H_{2}}\\
&=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}}\left(\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{K}_{+}(\mu)}q^{|\kappa|}\right)\left(\sum_{H_{1}\in\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)}q^{D^{\prime}(H_{1})}x^{H_{1}}\right)\left(\sum_{H_{1}\in\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)}q^{D^{\prime}(H_{2})}y^{H_{2}}\right)\\
&=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}}\mathpzc{b}_{\mu}(q)\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x;q)\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\mu}(y;q).\end{split}

Above, in the third equality we used
Theorem

1.11
,
(IV)
and relations (
1.34
) (
1.35
). In the last equality we used elementary geometric summations to write
\mathpzc{b}_{\mu}(q)=\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{K}_{+}(\mu)}q^{|\kappa|}
.
∎

Proof of
theorem

1.15
, eq. (
1.38
).

Expanding Schur polynomials as generating functions of semistandard Young tableaux of skew shape we can manipulate the left hand side of (
1.38
) as

\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}\\
\,\lambda_{1}^{\prime}=N\end{subarray}}q^{|\rho|}s_{\lambda/\rho}(x)s_{\lambda/\rho}(y)&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}\\
\,\lambda_{1}^{\prime}=N\end{subarray}}\sum_{P,Q\in\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)}q^{|\rho|}x^{P}y^{Q}\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu,\mu\in\mathbb{Y}\\
\nu_{1}^{\prime}+\mu_{1}^{\prime}=N\end{subarray}}\sum_{(H_{1},H_{2};\kappa)\in\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)^{2}\times\mathcal{K}_{+}(\mu)}q^{|\nu|+|\kappa|+D(H_{1})+D(H_{2})}x^{H_{1}}y^{H_{2}}\\
&=\sum_{\ell=0}^{N}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\in\mathbb{Y}\\
\nu_{1}^{\prime}=\ell\end{subarray}}q^{|\nu|}\bigg)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\mathbb{Y}\\
\mu_{1}^{\prime}=N-\ell\end{subarray}}\mathpzc{b}_{\mu}(q)\mathcal{Q}_{\mu}^{\prime}(x;q)\mathcal{Q}_{\mu}^{\prime}(y;q)\\
&=\sum_{\ell=0}^{N}\frac{q^{\ell}}{(q;q)_{\ell}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\mathbb{Y}\\
\mu_{1}^{\prime}=N-\ell\end{subarray}}\mathpzc{b}_{\mu}(q)\mathcal{Q}_{\mu}^{\prime}(x;q)\mathcal{Q}_{\mu}^{\prime}(y;q).\end{split}

Above, in the second equality we used
Theorem

1.7
and the fact that (
1.24
), (
1.25
) imply that, if
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{col}}(P,Q)=(H_{1},H_{2},\kappa,\nu)
, then
x^{P}=x^{H_{1}}
,
y^{Q}=y^{H_{2}}
and
|\rho|=D(H_{1})+D(H_{2})+|\kappa|+|\nu|
. We also used (
1.26
) to relate
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}
to
\mu_{1}^{\prime}+\nu_{1}^{\prime}
. In the third and in the last last equality we used elementary geometric summations express
\mathpzc{b}_{\mu}(q)
and to write
\frac{q^{\ell}}{(q;q)_{\ell}}=\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{Y},\,\nu_{1}^{\prime}=\ell}q^{|\nu|}
.
∎

10.3.
Littlewood identities and refinements

Let us recall a useful identity proven in
[
44
]
.

Lemma 10.4

(
[
44
]
, Lemma 10.6)
.

Fix
z,q\in\mathbb{C}
with
|q|<1
. Then, we have

\sum_{\varkappa\in\mathbb{Y}~:~\ell(\varkappa)\leq m}z^{\mathrm{odd}(\varkappa)}q^{|\varkappa|}=\frac{[1+qz]_{q^{2}}^{m}}{(q^{2};q^{2})_{m}},\qquad\sum_{\varkappa\in\mathbb{Y}~:~\ell(\varkappa)=m}z^{\mathrm{odd}(\nu)}q^{|\nu|}=\frac{[q^{2}+qz]_{q^{2}}^{m}}{(q^{2};q^{2})_{m}}.

Lemma 10.5
.

Fix
\alpha,\beta,q\in\mathbb{C}
with
|q|<1
. Then, we have

\mathpzc{b}_{\mu}(\alpha,\beta,q)=\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{K}(\mu)}\alpha^{\mathrm{odd}(\kappa+\mu)}\beta^{\mathrm{odd}(\kappa)}q^{|\kappa|}.

Proof.

We can decompose any
\kappa\in\mathcal{K}(\mu)
as
\kappa=(\varkappa^{(1)},\varkappa^{(2)},\dots)
with
\varkappa^{(i)}\in\mathbb{Y}
, and
\ell(\varkappa^{(i)})\leq m_{i}(\mu)
. Then, we have

\sum_{\kappa\in\mathcal{K}(\mu)}\alpha^{\mathrm{odd}(\kappa+\mu)}\beta^{\mathrm{odd}(\kappa)}q^{|\kappa|}=\prod_{i\geq 1}\left(\sum_{\varkappa\in\mathbb{Y}~:~\ell(\varkappa)\leq m_{i}(\mu)}\alpha^{\mathrm{odd}(i+\varkappa)}\beta^{\mathrm{odd}(\varkappa)}q^{|\varkappa|}\right).

Then, since for every
\varkappa\in\mathbb{Y}
such that
\ell(\varkappa)\leq m_{i}(\mu)
we have
\mathrm{odd}(i+\varkappa)=\mathrm{odd}(\varkappa)
if
i
is even and
\mathrm{odd}(i+\varkappa)=m_{i}(\mu)-\mathrm{odd}(\varkappa)
if
i
is odd, the right hand side in the above expression reduced, using
Lemma

10.4
, to the factorized expression in (
10.3
).
∎

Proof of
Theorem

1.14
, eq. (
1.37
).

Expanding the right hand side of (
1.37
) we find

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}

\displaystyle\frac{(-\alpha x_{i};q^{2})_{\infty}(-q\beta x_{i};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(x_{i}^{2};q^{2})_{\infty}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{1}{(x_{i}x_{j};q^{2})_{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathrm{sym}}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime-},\mathbb{N})}\left(\alpha^{\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{c}(i+2kn,i)\text{ is odd}}}\right)\left(\beta^{\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{c}(i+(2k+1)n,i)\text{ is odd}}}\right)

\displaystyle\times\left(q^{\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}k\,\mathbf{c}(i+kn,j)}\right)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{j=i}^{n}\mathbf{c}(i+kn,j)}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathrm{sym}}(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\prime-},\mathbb{N})}(\alpha\beta)^{\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{oddTr}(\mathbf{c})}\left(\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{oddTr}^{-}(\mathbf{c})}\left(q^{\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}k\,\mathbf{c}(i+kn,j)}\right)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{j=i}^{n}\mathbf{c}(i+kn,j)}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(H;\kappa)\in\bigsqcup_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}}\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)\times\mathcal{K}(\mu)}\alpha^{\mathrm{odd}(\kappa+\mu)}\beta^{\mathrm{odd}(\kappa)}q^{|\kappa|}q^{2D(H)}x^{H}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu}c_{\mu}(\alpha,\beta;q)\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x;q^{2}).

Above in the first equality we expanded through the geometric series the factors in the
q
-Pochhammer symbols in the denominator and combined them with those in the numerator in the right hand side. In the second equality we simply recognized the quantities
\mathrm{oddTr}(\mathbf{c}),\mathrm{oddTr}^{-}(\mathbf{c})
in the exponents of coefficients
\alpha,\beta
and in the third equality we used
Theorem

9.3
. In the last equality we used
Lemma

10.5
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

1.15
, eq. (
1.39
).

We expand the skew Schur polynomials as generating functions of semistandard tableaux in the left hand side of (
1.39
) and we find

(10.5)

\displaystyle\sum_{\rho\subset\lambda:\lambda_{1}^{\prime}=N}

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mathrm{odd}(\lambda)}\beta^{\mathrm{odd}(\rho)}q^{|\rho|}s_{\lambda/\rho}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rho\subset\lambda:\lambda_{1}^{\prime}=N}\sum_{P\in\mathrm{SST}(\lambda/\rho,n)}\alpha^{\mathrm{odd}(\lambda)}\beta^{\mathrm{odd}(\rho)}q^{|\rho|}x^{P}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu,\mu\in\mathbb{Y}\\
\nu_{1}^{\prime}+\mu_{1}^{\prime}=N\end{subarray}}\sum_{(H_{1},\kappa)\in\mathrm{HWT}(\mu,n)\times\mathcal{K}_{+}(\mu)}(\alpha\beta)^{\mathrm{odd}(\nu)}\alpha^{\mathrm{odd}(\kappa+\mu)}\beta^{\mathrm{odd}(\kappa)}q^{2D^{\prime}(H)+|\kappa|+|\nu|}x^{H}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\left(\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{Y}:\nu_{1}^{\prime}=k}(\alpha\beta)^{\mathrm{odd}(\nu)}q^{|\nu|}\right)\left(\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}:\mu_{1}^{\prime}=N-k}\mathpzc{b}_{\mu}(\alpha,\beta,q)\mathscr{P}_{\mu}(x;q^{2})\right).

Above, the second equality uses
Theorem

9.2
, while the third equality uses
Lemma

10.5
. Then, using
Lemma

10.4
the right hand side reduces to the right hand side of (
1.39
), completing the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Alternative proof of
proposition

5.34

Here we give a direct proof of
proposition

5.34
.
For this recall the notion of energy and dual energy function of
Definition

5.17
. Below we state another property of the energy function that is useful to us.
To do so, we note that we can organize the energy function in the form of a corner transfer matrix by the scattering diagram in Figure
15
.
Recall that
\sigma^{-}\in S_{n}
is the longest element as in
Remark

5.12
.

Lemma A.1

(
[
68
, Lemma 4.4]
)
.

For
H\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}
, the energy function
D(H)
is equal to the total sum of the winding numbers in all
\ell(\ell-1)/2
crossings (
5.6
) from the action of
\sigma^{-}\in S_{\ell}
.

Example A.2
.

When
H
is the second tableau in
example

2.6
, i.e.,
H=12\otimes 223\otimes 1122
, we can calculate the local energies
D_{1}(H),D_{2}(H)
as follows:

\displaystyle D_{1}(H)

\displaystyle=W(1122\otimes 223)=0,

\displaystyle D_{2}(H)

\displaystyle=W_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}(H)+W_{2}(H)=W(2223,12)+W(112,23)=2+0=2.

The corresponding diagram is illustrated in
Figure

15
, right panel.

Next, let us describe the idea of our direct proof of
Proposition

5.34
using the example with
\mu=(4,3,2,2,1)
and
n=5
.
Consider the tensor products

(A.1a)

\displaystyle\overline{H}

\displaystyle=5\otimes 44\otimes 45\otimes 455\otimes 4444,

\displaystyle D(\overline{H})

\displaystyle=9,

(A.1b)

\displaystyle f_{0}(\overline{H})

\displaystyle=5\otimes 44\otimes 45\otimes 145\otimes 4444,

\displaystyle D(f_{0}(\overline{H}))

\displaystyle=10,

and note that they only differ in the fourth elements (
455
and
145
).
To see their energies, let us compare the scattering diagrams given in
fig.

19
.

In this diagrams we can observe:

(1)

The edges in the diagram can be classified into two cases.

(a)

The edges where the attached scattering data are the same between
\overline{H}
and
f_{0}(\overline{H})
.
We color these edges in black.

(b)

Those edges where they are different (more precisely one element ‘5’ in
\overline{H}
is changed as ‘1’ in
f_{0}(\overline{H})
).
We colored these red for
\overline{H}
and blue for
f_{0}(\overline{H})
.

We can observe that the colored edges form paths that enter from one of the top vertical edges and exit from one of the rightmost horizontal edges.

(2)

None of the edges from left or bottom of the colored paths have elements ‘
n
’.

(3)

Here
W(\cdot)
is the local energy for the scattering diagram “
\cdot
” defined in
definition

5.17
.

In fact we can prove that the above properties (1)-(3) hold for any
\overline{H}
with
H\in\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{\mathrm{hw}}\setminus\{H_{\max}\}
.
We can show Equation (
5.22
) using them.
By (3), one sees that the sum of the local energies along the red path is one less than that along the blue path.
The local energies at the vertex surrounding to black edges are exactly the same between the two scattering diagrams.

For proving the above (1)-(3), we prepare two lemmas performing a comparison between two combinatorial
R
-matrices, here denoted
\mathcal{R}(a\otimes b)=d\otimes c
and
\mathcal{R}(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})=d^{\prime}\otimes c^{\prime}
.
Hereafter we will always assume that the length of
a
(resp.
a^{\prime}
) is shorter than or equal to
b
(resp.
b^{\prime}
).
The associated local energies are denoted by
W(a\otimes b)
and
W(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})
.

Lemma A.3
.

Suppose that

A.1:

a
includes at least one
n
and
a^{\prime}
is obtained from
a
with one
n
replaced by
1
;

A.2:

b=b^{\prime}
and they do not include
n
.

Then we have the following output data

c,d
and
c^{\prime},d^{\prime}
.

C.1:

c=c^{\prime}
and they do not have
n
.

C.2:

d
and
d^{\prime}
differ in only one element. In
d^{\prime}
, one
n
element in
d
is exchanged to
1
.

C.3:

W(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})=W(a\otimes b)+1
.

A graphical expression of
lemma

A.3
is shown in
fig.

.

R

).

Proof of
Lemma

A.3
.

We prove the lemma based on the Nakayashiki–Yamada rule in
definition

5.9
.
Instead of
C.1
and
C.2
, we can say in the language of the algorithm that among the dots in the left boxes associated to
b
, the dots connected by the lines are exactly the same as those for
b^{\prime}
.

Based on the algorithm, we connect each dot in the right boxes and each one in the left boxes with lines from the bottom dot to the top one in the right side.
Note that we can choose the order of connection arbitrarily because the positions of the unconnected dots and also the number of winding pairs do not depend on the order.
On the other hand, for the dots associated to
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
, we first connect the dot corresponding to the element
1
in
a^{\prime}
appeared by the replacement of an element
n
in
a
with a dot on the left.
The order of the second and the following connections is assumed to be the same as that for the case of “
a
”.
In
fig.

22
, we show the examples of
n=4
.
The left figure represents
a=12344,~b=122233
while the right one does
a^{\prime}=11234,~b^{\prime}=122233
, which satisfy the assumptions
A.1
and
A.2
.
The red superscripts mean the order of the connections.

b
a
1
2
3
4
5

b^{\prime}
a^{\prime}
1
2
3
4
5

Figure 22
.
An example with
n=4
of how the Nakayashiki–Yamada algorithm changes under a
f_{0}
that changes the winding number (i.e., local energy).

Comparing the boxes and dots for
a
and
a^{\prime}
, we find that the only difference is the position of the dots which we connect at first.
In
a
, the dot is in the bottommost (the
n
th) box while in
a^{\prime}
it is in the first box.
From the algorithm, the dot in the
n
th box in
a
is connected to the one of bottommost dots among the ones which are in the
1
st through
(n-1)
th boxes in
b
.
On the other hand, the dot in the first box in
a^{\prime}
is connected to the one of the bottommost boxes among the whole ones.
Combining this with
A.2
, we see that they are connected to the same dot in the side of
b(=b^{\prime})
.
Furthermore we find that after we connect them, the configurations of the boxes and dots have no differences and the order of connections are also exactly the same between the dots associated to
a
and
a^{\prime}
.
Thus we find that in the dots on the left sides associated to
b
and
b^{\prime}
, exactly the same dots are connected. This leads to
C.1
and
C.2
.

C.3
also follows from the above considerations. It is clear that for the first connections, the connecting pair of dots is unwinding for
a
and
b
while for
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
, it is winding. In addition there are no differences for the other connecting pairs. This leads to
C.3
.
∎

lemma

A.3
stated above is about a comparison between two scattering diagrams as depicted
in
example

A.4
, where
a^{\prime}
and
a

differ in only one element while
b=b^{\prime}
.
The next lemma deals with the opposite case, i.e.
a=a^{\prime}
, while
b
and
b^{\prime}
are different with only one element.

Lemma A.5
.

Assume

A.1:

a=a^{\prime}
and they do not have
1
s.

A.2:

b
has at least one
n
and
b^{\prime}
is obtained from
b
by replacing one
n
in
b

with
1
.

(1) If additionally
W(a\otimes b)=0
, then

C.1:

c=c^{\prime}
and they do not have
n
.

C.2:

d
has at least one
n
and
d^{\prime}
is obtained from
d
by replacing one
n
in
d
with
1
.

C.3:

W(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})=0
.

(2) If additionally
W(a\otimes b)>0
, then

C.4:

c
has at least one
n
and
c^{\prime}
is obtained from
c
by replacing one
n
in
c
with
1
.

C.5:

d=d^{\prime}
and they do not have
1
.

C.6:

W(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})=W(a\otimes b)-1
.

Similar to
fig.

20
, a graphical expression of
lemma

A.5
is shown in
fig.

23
with the black, red and blue edges are drawn in the same way.
When
h=h^{\prime}
with
h\in\{a,b,c,d\}
and
h
does not contain a
1
s, we mark the edges in gray.

\hbox to50.03pt{\vbox to40.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 32.75739pt\lower-17.27182pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Figure 23
.
Graphical expression of
lemma

A.5
.

Example A.6
.

In
fig.

24
, we show examples of the scattering diagrams for
lemma

A.5
cases (1) and (2) with
n=3
.
In case (1), we see that
a=a^{\prime}
and
c=c^{\prime}
, while
b
and
b^{\prime}
(resp.
d
and
d^{\prime}
) differ in only one element: one
n
in
b
(resp.
d
) is replaced by
1
in
b^{\prime}
(resp.
d^{\prime}
).
Similar things also happen in case (2). In this case
a=a^{\prime}
and
d=d^{\prime}
, while
b
and
b^{\prime}
(resp.
c
and
c^{\prime}
)
differ in one element in the same way as case (1).
As in the above figures, to help compare the left and right scattering diagrams, we mark the edges where the data changes in red (resp. blue) on the left (resp. right) and those that it does not in black.

Figure 24
.
Examples of scattering diagrams for
lemma

A.5
with
n=3
.

Proof of
Lemma

A.5
.

As in the proof of
lemma

A.3
, we give the proof by considering the algorithm in
definition

5.9
.
We compare two box and dots diagrams describing
a
,
b
and
a^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime}
.
Note that the dots in the left sides associated with
b
and
b^{\prime}
can be classified into two types, the ones connected with the dots in the right sides associated with
a
and
a^{\prime}
, and the disconnected ones.

First we consider the case (1).
To show
C.1
-
C.3
, it is sufficient to prove the following claim about the two types of dots:

(
\ast
)

All connected dots for
b
are exactly the same as those for
b^{\prime}
, and they do not includes the dots on the bottom box representing
n
s.

The claim (

∗

‣
A
) is clear from the algorithm.
From
A.3
, we immediately see that in the box and dots diagram for
a
and
b
, all dots in the left bottom box are not connected since otherwise we have
W(a\otimes b)>0
.
Thus we find that even if we remove one dot in the left bottom box and put it in the left top box, the configuration of the connected dots does not change.
This leads to the proof of (

∗

‣
A
) and thus the case (1).

Next we move to the case (2).
To show
C.4
and
C.5
, it is sufficient to show the following claim about the dots associated with
b
and
b^{\prime}
:

(
\ast\ast
)

All unconnected dots about
b
is exactly the same as those about
b^{\prime}
and they do not include the dots in the top box representing
1
s.

It is not difficult to check this property.
As in the proof of
lemma

A.3
, we allocate the numbers
1,2,\ldots
to the dots on the right side associated with
a
and
a^{\prime}
and connect them to the dots on the left side associated with
b
and
b^{\prime}
in the increasing order of the labels.
In
fig.

25
, we show the example for the case
a=a^{\prime}=1223333
,
b=112233344
, and
b^{\prime}=111223334
.
There we mark the labels in red.

b
a
1
2
3
4
5
6
7

b^{\prime}
a^{\prime}
1
2
3
4
5
6
7

Figure 25
.
Example of how the winding number changes after applying
f_{0}
.

Suppose that for the boxes and dots diagram about
a
and
b
, the winding appears for the first time in the
k
th connection.
We see from the algorithm that the dot labeled
k
is connected with the dot in the bottom box on the left side while in the diagram associated with
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
, the dot with the same label
k
is connected with the dot in the top box corresponding to the element 1 in
a^{\prime}
which appears as the replacement of the element
n
in
a
.
In
fig.

25
,
k=6
and we mark the associated dots in blue.
In addition, we see from the algorithm that the remaining dots have exactly the same pairings by the lines between two diagrams about
a
,
b
and
a^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime}
.
Thus the first statement in (

∗
⁣
∗

‣
A
) follows.
The second statement in (

∗
⁣
∗

‣
A
) is clear from the algorithm and the assumption in
A.1
that
a(=a^{\prime})
does not have
1
s since in the diagram about
a
and
b
, it is impossible to have unwinding pairs leaving unconnected dots in the top left box.
Combining these facts we show the claim (

∗
⁣
∗

‣
A
) and thus
C.4
and
C.5
.

C.6
also immediately follows from the derivation above.
In the boxes and dots diagram about
a
and
b
, the pairing between the dot labeled
k
and the one in the bottom box is a winding pair while this winding changes to unwinding in the diagram about
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
since the dot labeled
k
is connected with the dot in the top box.
Combining this with the fact that other pairings are exactly the same between the diagrams about
a
,
b
and
a^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime}
, we have shown
C.6
.
∎

Using
lemma

A.3
and
lemma

A.5
, we obtain the proof of
proposition

5.34
.

Alternative proof of
proposition

5.34
.

From
lemma

5.31
, we see that
\overline{H}
and
f_{0}(\overline{H})
differ in only one factor.
Suppose that the
k
th factors from the right are the different ones.
As stated in the proof of
lemma

5.31
, the
1
st through
(k-1)
th factors do not have the element
n
s while
(k+1)
th through
\ell
factors do not have the element
1
s.
Thus the input data of the scattering diagrams of
\overline{H}
and
f_{0}(\overline{H})
can be expressed graphically as

A.3
and

) the red and blue edges form a common path starting from the

to the vertices adjacent to the red and blue edges.
